**Summary.** The authors investigate how periodically driven spin systems exhibit long-lived prethermalization before full thermalization. They show that by carefully engineering the system's emergent quasiparticle band structure using further-range interactions, they can achieve a 'doubly tunable' control over the prethermal lifetime. This work establishes a powerful theoretical tool for stabilizing non-equilibrium quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete mechanism—band structure engineering—to stabilize non-equilibrium quantum phases in driven systems, bridging concepts from classical nonlinear dynamics (periodic orbits) to many-body chaos.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the conflict between linear stability analysis, which suggests trajectories remain near stable periodic orbits (SPOs), and thermodynamic principles, which predict eventual Floquet heating in periodically driven many-body systems.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that a long-lived prethermal regime exists, whose lifetime can be controlled by engineering the emergent quasiparticle band structure to achieve a 'doubly tunable' parametric dependence ($	au_{	ext{pre}} \propto R^{-W}$).

**Method.** The analysis involves linearizing dynamics around SPOs to obtain an effective quasiparticle band structure and then deriving the scaling of the prethermal lifetime by analyzing time-averaged excitation rate equations.

**Model / system.** The study uses a periodically driven (Floquet) spin system, specifically modeled as a 2D classical Ising system on a square lattice with nearest-neighbor and further-range couplings. The dynamics are governed by the periodicity $H(t) = H(t+T)$.

**Key observables.** Prethermal lifetime ($	au_{	ext{pre}}$), quasiparticle momentum space width ($R$), and the dispersion exponent ($W$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The driving period $T$, coupling constants ($J, g$), and the engineered coefficients ($c_m$) that determine the band structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include describing perturbations via an effective quasiparticle band structure and assuming the quasiparticle distribution remains close to its initial Gaussian form for the scaling derivation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spin dynamics following SPOs, stability diagrams showing stable regions away from high-frequency limits, and plots comparing initial versus time-evolved quasiparticle momentum distributions at varying exponents $W$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and the tension between linear stability and heating. It then uses PO construction and linear analysis to find a gapless dispersion relation. Finally, it derives the scaling law $	au \propto R^{-W}$ by analyzing excitation rates and shows how further-range interactions allow for engineering $W$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We uncover a family of many-body periodic orbits in a periodically driven (Floquet) spin system away from the high-frequency limit. While linear stability analysis predicts that perturbed many-body trajectories remain close to stable periodic orbits, thermodynamic principles dictate that Floquet heating will ultimately set in. Our work aims to resolve the tension between these two expectations. In particular, we show that perturbations away from the stable periodic orbits feature a description akin to a quasiparticle band structure. A long-lived prethermal regime appears when modes around the gapless point are slowly populated. The dispersion determines the prethermal lifetime, and we show how band engineering leads to a "doubly tunable" parametric dependence of the prethermal lifetime $R^{-W}$, with $R$ the width in momentum space of the quasiparticle distribution and $W$ the exponent of the dispersion around the gapless point. Our results not only establish a powerful route toward stabilizing non-equilibrium phases of matter in driven many-body systems but also establish a conceptual bridge between periodic orbits in 'low-dimensional' nonlinear systems and many-body chaos.

</details>

**Summary.** The research uses trapped-ion quantum computers to experimentally model the AdS/CFT correspondence via holographic error-correcting codes. By measuring entanglement entropy, they first verified a key formula relating entanglement to area (FLM) and then showed that introducing non-stabilizer resources generates state-dependent signatures predicted by theories of emergent gravity. This work establishes quantum computation as a novel platform for testing fundamental physics conjectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum computation hardware (trapped ions) with fundamental physics problems (quantum gravity), providing an experimental testbed for concepts like holographic duality and emergent spacetime geometry that are highly relevant to AMO physicists studying complex many-body dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is unifying quantum mechanics and general relativity by investigating whether spacetime geometry emerges from fundamental quantum entanglement degrees of freedom.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally confirmed the Faulkner-Lewkowycz-Maldacena formula using a holographic code model, and further demonstrated that introducing non-stabilizer resources leads to state-dependent entropy signatures analogous to gravitational backreaction.

**Method.** They implemented toy models of AdS/CFT duality (holographic codes) on a trapped-ion quantum computer, measuring entanglement entropies to calculate 'proto-area entropy' ($	ext{SPA}$).

**Model / system.** The system uses the HaPPY code, a tensor network realizing a quantum error-correcting code, implemented on a trapped-ion platform. The model is extended by injecting non-stabilizer resources ('magic') to simulate gravitational backreaction effects.

**Key observables.** Proto-area entropy ($	ext{SPA}$), boundary entanglement entropy ($S_A$), recovered bulk entropy ($S_a$), and the dependence of $	ext{SPA}$ on bulk entanglement ($	heta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entangling angle ($	heta$) controlling bulk entanglement, and the magic parameter ($M$) quantifying non-stabilizerness.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed effects are interpreted as modeling emergent spacetime geometry, but the authors caution that this is a toy model and not a literal probe of semi-classical gravity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the HaPPY code structure, compare measured $	ext{SPA}$ vs. theory predictions for FLM testing, and show how increasing bulk entanglement ($	heta$) causes $	ext{SPA}$ to change when magic resources are added.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the experimental realization of the holographic duality test (FLM formula). It then enriches the model with non-stabilizerness ('magic') to observe state-dependent signatures, culminating in analogies involving wormhole geometries and two-sided entanglement structures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The unification of quantum mechanics and general relativity remains one of the major open problems of theoretical physics. The Anti-de Sitter/Conformal Field Theory (AdS/CFT) correspondence provides a valuable theoretical framework for this effort via a holographic duality between a theory of quantum gravity in asymptotically AdS spacetime and a conformal quantum field theory on the lower-dimensional boundary. Here, we implement a toy model of this duality called the HaPPY code, a quantum error-correcting code in the form of a tensor network with hyperbolic entanglement patterns, on a trapped-ion quantum computer. We present the first experimental confirmation of the Faulkner-Lewkowycz-Maldacena formula in this model - a key test of the holographic correspondence. We then enrich it with non-stabilizerness, or magic, and observe entropic precursors expected of emergent gravity. Finally, we present and measure a code construction whose entropic behavior is reminiscent of a highly quantum wormhole. Our experiments illustrate how quantum computers can serve as testbeds for modeling the emergence of spacetime.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates a method using SUPER excitation pulses to deterministically prepare specific collective quantum states in a triangular array of three coupled quantum emitters. The efficiency is critically dependent on the emitter spacing, showing near-perfect control only when the emitters are placed at deep-subwavelength separations. This work suggests that highly compact nanostructures are excellent platforms for controlling collective quantum phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum control in engineered nanoscale light-matter interactions, providing concrete theoretical predictions for achieving high-fidelity collective state preparation relevant to solid-state emitters and bio-inspired photonics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is the deterministic preparation of specific collective radiative states within a nanoscale array of quantum emitters.

**Main result.** Near-unity efficiency for preparing the symmetric collective state is achieved when the emitter spacing is deep-subwavelength, demonstrating that compact geometries are highly favorable for this control scheme.

**Method.** The study uses numerical simulation solving the Lindblad master equation under the influence of tailored, time-overlapping, red-detuned ultrashort excitation pulses (SUPER).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as an equilateral triangular trimer of three dipole-coupled two-level quantum emitters, inspired by biological light-harvesting structures. The dynamics are governed by the full Hamiltonian and a Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** State selectivity, preparation efficiency (population inversion) into specific collective states like $|S_1\rangle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-emitter spacing ($d$), which is critical; deep-subwavelength separations ($d \ll 0.01\lambda_0$) are optimal.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model relies on the coupled-dipole approximation and assumes weak system-environment interaction, though robustness against minor disorder is shown.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of population inversion into the symmetric state $|S_1\rangle$, showing high fidelity at small separations ($d = 0.01\lambda_0$) compared to significantly lower efficiency at larger separations ($d = 0.1\lambda_0$).

**Paper structure.** The paper extends the SUPER excitation scheme from two emitters to a trimer, numerically investigating how state selectivity and preparation efficiency depend on inter-emitter spacing by solving the master equation with tailored pulses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Swing-UP of quantum EmitteR population (SUPER) scheme has recently been proposed as a deterministic method for the preparation of collective radiative states in two strongly dipole-coupled quantum emitters (Phys. Rev. Res. \textbf{8}, 013179 (2026)). Here, we extend this approach to an equilateral subwavelength triangular trimer of dipole-coupled two-level quantum emitters (QEs), loosely inspired by biological light-harvesting ring geometries. Using tailored, time-overlapping, red-detuned ultrashort SUPER pulses, we numerically investigate the selective preparation of collective target states. We find that both the state selectivity and the preparation efficiency depend strongly on the inter-emitter spacing. In particular, at deep-subwavelength separations, the symmetric collective state can be deterministically prepared with near-unity efficiency, whereas the inversion efficiency and state selectivity are significantly lower at larger inter-emitter separations. Furthermore, this state preparation technique inherits a certain degree of robustness against reasonable static position imperfections and on-site frequency inhomogeneities of the individual QEs. Our results demonstrate that deep-subwavelength triangular trimers and, more broadly, highly compact ring geometries are excellent candidates for the deterministic preparation of collective radiative states via SUPER excitation. These predictions could be realized with solid-state emitters and molecules. Our findings offer a route toward the direct probing of the `pure' electromagnetic layer of interaction in biological and bio-inspired synthetic nanophotonic ring configurations, with possible relevance in photonics, quantum information processing, and metrology.

</details>

**Summary.** The research introduces a novel cryogenic neutral-atom trap system that successfully resolves the conflict between achieving long atomic storage times and maintaining full optical access. By implementing advanced vacuum pumping techniques, they achieved record lifetimes of two hours with single atoms in an optical tweezer array. This breakthrough paves a viable path for scaling up quantum simulation experiments using thousands of qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical engineering bottleneck in quantum simulation: maintaining high coherence times while enabling complex optical addressing schemes necessary for scaling up qubit numbers.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Current neutral-atom quantum platforms face a fundamental trade-off between maintaining full optical accessibility for control and achieving long atomic storage times in optical potentials.

**Main result.** The authors report a novel cryogenic apparatus that overcomes this trade-off, demonstrating vacuum-limited single-atom trapping lifetimes of up to two hours while retaining full optical access.

**Method.** They utilized a cryogenically enhanced neutral-atom trap system with an optical tweezer array and employed rigorous vacuum techniques, including cold tips and bakeouts, to minimize gas-phase decoherence.

**Model / system.** The platform uses single $^{88}	ext{Sr}$ atoms trapped in optical tweezers within a cryostat. The key components include the atomic source, the tweezer array, and a 4 K cold tip used for pumping residual gases like hydrogen.

**Key observables.** Vacuum-limited trapping lifetimes ($	au$), survival probability vs. hold time, and dependence of lifetime on trap depth and oven temperature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trapping lifetime up to two hours (>7000 s); Tweezer spacing of $4\ \mu	ext{m}$; Cryogenic temperatures ($\sim 4$ K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The design does not shield atoms from room-temperature blackbody radiation, which is a noted limitation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the vacuum chamber setup (Fig. 1), the experimental sequence for lifetime measurements using various cooling protocols (Fig. 2), and show how regeneration cycles restore long lifetimes after gas desorption events (Fig. 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical apparatus, presents methods for measuring atomic decay rates under varying conditions (cooling/vacuum control), reports achieving record long lifetimes by mitigating vacuum limitations, and discusses scalability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neutral-atom quantum processors are rapidly scaling toward system sizes of more than ten thousand qubits, allowing for the realization of a new class of quantum computing algorithms and quantum simulation experiments. However, current neutral-atom platforms generally have to find a compromise between the optical accessibility and the storage time of atoms in optical potentials, limiting the available qubit numbers. Here we report on the operation of a novel, cryogenically enhanced, neutral-atom apparatus that overcomes these apparently conflicting requirements. We demonstrate vacuum-limited trapping lifetimes of up to two hours of single $^{88}\mathrm{Sr}$ atoms in an optical tweezer array while preserving full optical access and without the need for complex cryogenic enclosures. Our measurements show that exceptionally long single-atom lifetimes can be achieved with a relatively simple cryostat design. Our architecture can be straightforwardly ported to other atomic species and shows a viable path for scaling up to sorted arrays of tens of thousands of atoms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a spatial realization of Measurement-induced Phase Transitions (MIPTs) in a monitored quantum circuit by imposing a linear gradient on the measurement probability. By analyzing entanglement scaling across this gradient, the authors show that the transition is governed by a specific scaling form. This method allows for the controlled probing and direct calculation of critical exponents within a single steady state.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, controlled method—spatial inhomogeneity—to engineer and probe measurement-induced criticality within a single steady state, offering insights into non-equilibrium critical phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Measurement-induced Phase Transitions (MIPTs) by realizing the transition spatially rather than through uniform measurement probabilities or temporal driving.

**Main result.** It establishes that a deterministic spatial gradient in measurement probability creates coexisting volume-, critical, and area-law regions within a single steady state, allowing for the direct extraction of the correlation-length exponent $\nu$.

**Method.** The analysis involves scanning entanglement observables (like subsystem entropy) across the spatially varying measurement profile and performing scaling analyses.

**Model / system.** The system is a monitored Clifford chain (1+1)D random circuit model where local projective measurements are performed after each gate layer, controlled by a linear gradient $p(x)$.

**Key observables.** Subsystem entanglement entropy ($S_A$), ancilla entropy ($S_Q$), and tripartite mutual information ($I_3$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical measurement probability $p_c$, the spatial gradient parameter $R$, and the physical bounds $0 \le p(x) \le 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on truncating the linear gradient due to the physical constraints of probabilities ($0 \le p(x) \le 1$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the circuit and the resulting entanglement structure (Volume-law $\leftarrow$ Criticality $\rightarrow$ Area-law). Figure 3 verifies data collapse for scaling forms on both volume-law and area-law sides.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the spatial MIPT concept, analyzes the steady state containing coexisting regimes via entanglement observables, derives a spatial scaling form analogous to finite-time scaling but without Kibble-Zurek dynamics, and uses this structure to extract $\nu$ from the finite linear window.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measurement-induced phase transitions (MIPTs) in monitored quantum circuits are usually characterized by preparing steady states at different uniform measurement probabilities. Here we introduce a spatial realization of the MIPT by imposing a deterministic measurement gradient in a single monitored Clifford chain. The resulting steady state contains coexisting volume-law, critical, and area-law regions, with the point $p(x)=p_c$ acting as a spatial critical cut. By scanning entanglement observables across this profile, we show that the transition is organized by a spatial scaling form. Although this structure is analogous to finite-time scaling in temporally driven MIPT, the spatial protocol has no Kibble-Zurek dynamics. Instead, the physical bounds $0\le p\le 1$ impose a finite linear window, producing cutoff-controlled asymptotic regimes whose fitted exponents provide direct access to the correlation-length exponent $ν$. Our results establish spatially inhomogeneous measurements as a controlled route to engineer and probe measurement-induced criticality within a single steady state.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors experimentally demonstrate high-fidelity quantum state transfer across a spin chain using trapped-ion processors. They show that engineering the qubit couplings significantly outperforms simple nearest-neighbor interactions. Crucially, they also optimize the gate implementation by using parallel Trotterization, making Hamiltonian-based communication more practical for real quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges theoretical quantum information concepts (Hamiltonian dynamics, PST) with cutting-edge experimental hardware (trapped ions), providing concrete benchmarks for scalable quantum networking protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge addressed is achieving efficient, high-fidelity quantum communication between distant qubits necessary for scaling quantum processors.

**Main result.** Engineered coupling profiles significantly enhance the fidelity of quantum state transfer compared to uniform nearest-neighbor couplings. Furthermore, exploiting Hamiltonian commutation structure allows for a parallel Trotter decomposition that reduces circuit depth and execution time.

**Method.** The research combines exact numerical simulations with experimental measurements performed on a trapped-ion quantum processor using gate-based quantum simulation techniques (Trotterization).

**Model / system.** The system models a one-dimensional spin chain governed by an XY Hamiltonian, $H = \sum J_{ij} (X_i X_j + Y_i Y_j)$. The physical platform utilized is IonQ's trapped-ion quantum processor.

**Key observables.** State Transfer Fidelity ($F(t)$), coherence preservation ($\langle Z_{N-1} angle$), circuit depth, and execution time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths ($J_{ij}$), chain length ($N$), Trotter step size ($\Delta t$), and the comparison between uniform vs. engineered coupling profiles (e.g., $J_j \propto j(N-j)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations involve using the first-order Lie-Trotter product formula to simulate continuous time evolution on a discrete gate-based processor, and acknowledging that observed fidelity drops are due to hardware errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantum state transfer dynamics, comparing engineered vs. uniform couplings (Fig 4), showing population evolution for both single excitations and superpositions (Figs 6 & 7), and detailing the comparison between sequential and parallel Trotter implementations (Fig 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares different protocols: first, demonstrating state transfer using nearest-neighbor couplings; second, proving that engineered coupling profiles enhance fidelity; third, optimizing the implementation by showing that parallel decomposition reduces circuit depth compared to serial methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Efficient communication between distant qubits is one of the central challenges in scaling quantum processors. Although engineered spin chain protocols have been extensively investigated theoretically, their experimental realization has remained comparatively limited. Here, we experimentally realize engineered quantum communication protocols through digitally simulated spin Hamiltonian on IonQ's Forte 1/ Forte Enterprise 1 trapped-ion quantum processor. Combining exact numerical simulations with quantum hardware experiments, we benchmark uniform nearest-neighbour and engineered coupling profiles and demonstrate that engineered interactions significantly enhance the fidelity of quantum state transfer. We further show that exploiting the commutation structure of the spin Hamiltonian enables a parallel Trotter decomposition that more faithfully reproduces the target dynamics while substantially reducing the circuit depth and execution time compared to the conventional sequential implementations. Our results demonstrate that programmable quantum processors can effectively realize and efficiently implement quantum communication protocols, bringing Hamiltonian-based quantum communication closer to practical quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel constructive framework to model quantum emitters interacting with the complex electromagnetic modes of photonic crystals. By combining symmetry analysis with full Maxwell solver results, the authors derive minimal lattice Hamiltonians that overcome the limitations of existing simplified models. This allows for accurate simulation of polarization-dependent effects and non-Markovian dynamics beyond simple perturbative treatments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial computational bridge, allowing researchers to use advanced quantum many-body techniques (like those used for cavity QED) on complex, structured photonic environments where simple scalar models fail.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is bridging realistic, vectorial electromagnetic descriptions of light-matter interaction in photonic crystals with computationally tractable quantum many-body models.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a constructive framework yielding minimal, symmetry-enforced lattice Hamiltonians that accurately reproduce the full photonic dispersion while retaining essential position and polarization dependence.

**Method.** The method combines symmetry-constrained tight-binding constructions with numerically computed photonic band structures derived from Maxwell equations to build an effective bosonic Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum emitters coupled to a photonic crystal, modeled by deriving an effective bosonic lattice Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_B$) and coupling it to the emitter Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_S$).

**Key observables.** Polarization-dependent directional emission, non-Markovian light-matter dynamics, and entanglement entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model is analyzed in both perturbative (Born-Markov) and non-perturbative regimes; key parameters include the photonic band structure $\omega_\alpha(k)$ and coupling coefficients $g_{\alpha,k}(\mathbf{r}_i)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include the Born approximation, Markovian limit ($	au 	o 0$), and secular approximation when comparing to macroscopic QED results.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention simulations on a finite lattice using absorbing boundaries to study entanglement entropy and directional emission patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the modeling trade-off, then detailing the constructive framework combining symmetry analysis with band structure calculation to derive the minimal Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_B + \hat{H}_{int}$), validating this model against Green's function/macroscopic QED results in the perturbative limit, and finally demonstrating its utility for non-Markovian dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances are enabling quantum emitters to interact with photonic crystals, whose electromagnetic modes exhibit complex dispersion relations, spatial mode structure, and polarization textures. However, modeling light-matter behavior in these systems faces a persistent trade-off: electromagnetic approaches based on Maxwell-equation solvers provide realistic vectorial descriptions but are difficult to integrate with quantum many-body and non-perturbative methods, whereas simplified quantum-optical lattice models are tractable but typically rely on scalar and spatially independent light-matter couplings that miss essential features of these structured photonic environments. Here, we introduce a constructive framework to derive quantum-optical lattice descriptions that overcome this trade-off. Combining symmetry-constrained tight-binding constructions with numerically computed photonic band structures and field profiles, our method yields minimal, symmetry-enforced lattice Hamiltonians that reproduce the target photonic dispersion while retaining the mode-resolved (position- and polarization-dependent) structure of the light-matter coupling. We show that these models recover Green's-function-based emitter dynamics in the perturbative regime, while providing access to non-perturbative quantum dynamical simulations beyond emitter-only descriptions. As a proof of principle, we apply the framework to a two-dimensional photonic crystal and show that it captures polarization-dependent directional emission inaccessible to scalar models, while enabling the analysis of non-Markovian light-matter dynamics and entanglement. Our results provide a practical bridge between classical electromagnetic simulation tools and quantum-optical many-body and non-Markovian modeling in photonic crystal settings.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the theoretical limit of counting precision in many-body superradiant systems. It establishes that while uncontrolled dynamics fail to maintain optimal measurement precision, applying a tailored quantum feedback protocol successfully enforces Heisenberg scaling ($1/N^2$). This work demonstrates that controlled dissipation can be actively utilized to enhance quantum measurements beyond standard limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental limits on measurement precision in open quantum systems by showing how active feedback can convert inherent dissipation into a resource for achieving Heisenberg scaling, which is highly relevant to quantum metrology applications using cavity QED or atomic ensembles.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to determine if Heisenberg scaling ($1/N^2$) can be achieved for the estimation variance of counting observables in many-body quantum systems, and how this requires active control.

**Main result.** A quantum feedback protocol was established that successfully achieves Heisenberg scaling for the relative fluctuation of the counting observable by controlling dissipation. This demonstrates that collective dissipation can be harnessed as a resource for enhanced precision.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving many-body Kinetic Uncertainty Relations (KURs) under feedback control and solving mean-field equations derived from the feedback-modified master equation.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an ensemble of $N$ two-level systems exhibiting superradiance, analyzed using a quantum feedback protocol applied to its dynamics. The evolution is governed by a feedback-controlled master equation.

**Key observables.** Counting observable ($N_J(	au)$), relative fluctuation ($	ext{Var}[N_J(	au)] / \langle N_J(	au) angle^2$), and the lower bound set by the Kinetic Uncertainty Relation (KUR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Particle number $N$, time $	au$, feedback strength $
u = c/N$, and system parameters like decay rates ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the mean-field approximation for large-$N$ regimes, which simplifies the dynamics of collective spin operators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares the relative fluctuation (blue), KUR lower bound (red), and jump contribution (green) versus $N$ for cases with no feedback, optimal feedback, and feedback combined with precession.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of achieving Heisenberg scaling in counting observables. It then develops the theoretical framework using quantum feedback to modify the system dynamics and derive a corresponding many-body KUR. Finally, it presents analytical derivations and numerical simulations confirming the $1/N^2$ scaling under optimal control.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Precision is a central figure of merit for quantum devices, including quantum clocks whose performance is determined by the stability of counting events. Kinetic uncertainty relations set fundamental limits on the precision of such counting observables, showing that their fluctuations cannot be suppressed without increasing the activity of the system. While many-body effects offer a natural route to enhanced performance, it remains unclear how far they can enhance counting precision. In quantum metrology, Heisenberg scaling refers to the suppression of estimation variance as $1/N^2$ with the particle number $N$. This raises the question of whether the fluctuation of counting observables can exhibit an analogous Heisenberg-like $1/N^2$ scaling, but no protocol for achieving it has been established. We establish a protocol that achieves this scaling by applying quantum feedback to a superradiant spin ensemble. Because the superradiant enhancement of activity is transient, the scaling of counting precision becomes achievable only when it is controlled by feedback. We establish this result analytically through a many-body kinetic uncertainty relation and feedback-modified mean-field equations, and show it by numerical simulations. Our results demonstrate that feedback can turn collective dissipation into a resource for Heisenberg scaling of counting precision.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates scalable methods for generating remote entanglement between solid-state spin qubits ($	ext{SnV}$ centers) integrated into on-chip broadband waveguides. By circumventing the need for spectral matching to optical cavities, they achieve high-fidelity two-photon interference and heralded entanglement generation. This establishes a robust platform for building quantum network nodes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the physical implementation challenges for quantum networking nodes by replacing fragile cavity coupling with robust waveguide integration, a critical step for scaling solid-state quantum technologies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to demonstrate scalable quantum network links by achieving remote entanglement between solid-state spin qubits integrated into broadband waveguides, overcoming limitations of previous cavity-enhanced systems.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated heralded remote spin-spin entanglement using the waveguide platform, achieving high two-photon interference visibility comparable to cavity records without needing spectral matching.

**Method.** Entanglement is generated via a photon-mediated process (Barrett-Kok protocol) involving single-photon emission from diamond tin-vacancy ($	ext{SnV}$) centers and subsequent quantum interference measurements.

**Model / system.** The platform utilizes $	ext{SnV}$ spin qubits embedded in separate on-chip broadband waveguides fabricated on a high-purity diamond chip, operating at cryogenic temperatures. This system allows for intrinsic photon emission coupled to the waveguide mode.

**Key observables.** Entanglement fidelity ($F$), two-photon interference visibility ($V$), single-photon detection probability density, and spin coherence time ($T_{2	ext{DD}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperature of $1	ext{ K}$; high structural yield (99%); entanglement success probability limited by photon collection efficiency ($\approx 0.3\%$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that broadband waveguides provide sufficient coupling and low loss to enable scalable, high-yield integration compared to cavity methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the platform schematic (Fig. 1), qubit characterization (Rabi oscillations, coherence time scaling in Fig. 2), two-photon interference measurements showing visibility vs. time difference (Fig. 3), and the full heralded entanglement sequence (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by characterizing the fundamental properties of the $	ext{SnV}$ qubits (coherence, photon emission) in waveguides, demonstrating high-visibility two-photon interference without cavities, and culminating in the realization and characterization of heralded remote spin-spin entanglement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Solid-state spin-photon interfaces promise to scale quantum networks through on-chip photonic integration and multiplexed entanglement generation. To date, remote entanglement between integrated emitters has been realized only in cavity-enhanced systems, where fabrication yield and spectral matching remain major obstacles. Here we demonstrate heralded remote entanglement between diamond tin-vacancy spin qubits embedded in separate on-chip waveguides. Combining intrinsically efficient photon emission with a broadband waveguide architecture provides high device yield and obviates the need for spectral matching to cavity modes. We realize coherent optical and spin control and achieve high-visibility two-photon interference. By combining photon-mediated entanglement generation with real-time feedforward, we produce a consistent entangled state independent of the heralding pattern. These results establish waveguide-integrated tin-vacancy centers as a compelling platform for scalable quantum network nodes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that driven quantum junctions can act as programmable devices for controlling physical entropy flow. By comparing single and two-qubit architectures, the authors show multi-qubit systems offer thermodynamic advantages while revealing novel effects like coherent entropy currents and negative differential conductance. The methodology relies on advanced non-equilibrium quantum field theory techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium quantum thermodynamics by treating entropy transport as a measurable, controllable quantity in engineered quantum circuits, which is highly relevant for open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on achieving programmable control over physical entropy transport mediated by driven quantum junctions, moving beyond simple energy balance considerations.

**Main result.** Two-qubit junctions can enhance entropy transfer compared to single-qubit ones using lower driving power. The work also reveals coherent contributions and negative differential entropy conductance.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Keldysh diagrammatic approach and solves the Lindblad master equation under Born-Markov approximations to calculate nonlinear Rényi entropy flow.

**Model / system.** The system models $N$-qubit junctions coupled to multiple thermal reservoirs and a probe reservoir, described by an extended Hamiltonian including qubit interactions and external driving fields. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Entropy flow ($\mathcal{I}_\alpha$), entropy conductance, coherent contribution to entropy current, and negative differential entropy conductance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving frequency ($\omega_{dr}$), coupling strengths ($g_{ij}, J$), and the Rényi order $\alpha$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The weak coupling condition for the probe reservoir is assumed; calculations rely on Born-Markov, secular, and local master equation approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the general setup of the junctions coupled to baths. Specific figures show the enhancement factor ($R$) comparing two-qubit vs. single-qubit entropy conductance versus driving strength ($\Omega$), and energy spectra/anticrossings related to the Hamiltonian eigenstates.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework for nonlinear entropy flow, develops calculations using Keldysh formalism for both single and two-qubit junctions, compares the resulting entropy conductances, and analyzes specific non-intuitive effects like coherent contributions and negative differential conductance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that driven qubit junctions enable programmable control of physical entropy transport, with entropy conductance governed by quantum dynamics rather than by reservoir parameters alone. By comparing two simple quantum architectures -- a driven single-qubit junction and a driven two-qubit junction -- we find that the two-qubit junction enhances entropy transfer while requiring substantially lower driving power than its single-qubit counterpart. We further reveal two non-intuitive effects in both junctions: a sizable coherent contribution to the entropy current that emerges only under resonant driving, and negative differential entropy conductance, where increasing the thermal bias suppresses entropy flow into the probe reservoir. These results identify quantum logic architectures as programmable devices for entropy transport and suggest routes toward quantum feedback control, reservoir protection and refrigeration in driven quantum circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a protocol using time-dependent coupling (stirring) to activate passive quantum batteries, allowing for controlled energy extraction. It rigorously analyzes the thermodynamic trade-offs between work input, correlation generation, and information gain during this activation process. The results provide quantitative bounds on the maximum usable energy extractable from such systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium thermodynamics and quantum resource theory, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems, quantum heat engines, and understanding energy flow in complex quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a method to activate passive, thermally charged quantum batteries into an active state to enable controlled energy extraction.

**Main result.** The authors derive bounds on the maximal net extractable energy by explicitly accounting for the energetic cost of stirring, the entropic cost of correlation generation, and the activation time.

**Method.** The analysis uses thermodynamic principles, quantifying useful work via ergotropy and deriving upper bounds by relating it to non-equilibrium free energy differences.

**Model / system.** The model consists of a quantum battery ($\hat{H}_B$) and an ancillary activator ($\hat{H}_A$), coupled by a time-dependent interaction Hamiltonian $\hat{V}_I(t)$ during a 'stirring protocol.'

**Key observables.** Net extractable energy (net ergotropy), power output, work exchanged ($W_{	ext{stir}}$), and correlation build-up.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Activation Time ($	au$), relative temperatures ($T_A/T_B$), and frequency ratios ($\omega_A/\omega_B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes that environmental effects can be neglected during the short stirring time (timescale separation), and initial states are thermal Gibbs states.

**Figures summary.** Figure 7 illustrates three thermodynamic operating regimes (Heat Valve, Heat Engine, Refrigerator) defined by mathematical conditions on temperatures and frequencies, showing how ergotropy varies across these regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: defining the passive battery state $ightarrow$ introducing the time-dependent stirring protocol ($\hat{V}_I(t)$) $ightarrow$ deriving bounds for net extractable energy by accounting for costs $ightarrow$ applying this framework to an experimentally relevant waveguide-QED setup.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermally charged quantum batteries provide a stable and easy-to-have energy resource, but remain passive and therefore do not allow useful energy extraction on demand via unitary operations. We introduce a time-dependent stirring protocol that activates a thermally charged quantum battery through a time-dependent coupling to an auxiliary activator, thereby generating correlations that drive the battery into an active state. Accounting explicitly for the energetic cost of the stirring process, the entropic cost of correlation generation and the activation time, we derive bounds on the maximal net extractable energy. Incorporating projective measurement on the activator and exploiting the information gained through the measurement further enhances the extractable energy. As an experimentally relevant example, we analyze a waveguide-QED setup where a harmonic-oscillator battery (waveguide) is stirred and monitored by a two-level system. We characterize the performance of the protocol in terms of net extractable energy (net ergotropy) and power output.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors analyze a two-photon driven optomechanical system to study non-equilibrium phenomena. They demonstrate the existence of both first- and second-order dissipative phase transitions depending on detuning, while also showing that increasing pump power drives the system into chaotic regimes characterized by positive Lyapunov exponents. This establishes the platform as a rich testbed for quantum criticality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally accessible platform (optomechanics) to observe fundamental concepts like dissipative criticality, symmetry breaking, and quantum chaos simultaneously. The rigorous use of Liouvillian spectra is key for characterizing open system dynamics near critical points.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates nonequilibrium criticality and chaos within a driven-dissipative quantum optomechanical system.

**Main result.** The model successfully supports both first-order and second-order dissipative phase transitions, alongside the emergence of chaotic dynamics at strong driving.

**Method.** Analysis combines semiclassical stability analysis, exact Liouvillian spectral calculations, and stochastic quantum trajectory simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-photon driven optomechanical setup coupling an optical resonator to a mechanical oscillator. The dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation incorporating parametric driving and dissipation.

**Key observables.** Dissipative phase transitions (first/second order), cavity-parity symmetry breaking, Lyapunov exponents, and steady-state entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\Delta$), coupling strength ($G$), mechanical frequency ($\Omega_m$), and pump power (related to $G$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on semiclassical approximations for mean-field dynamics but extends to full quantum descriptions via Liouvillian spectra.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate stability diagrams showing continuous vs. discontinuous transitions, and plots of mean photon/phonon occupations across different pump strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system's behavior by first establishing the model, then using semiclassical methods to identify DPTs based on detuning signs, followed by spectral analysis to confirm transition orders, and finally examining high-power regimes for chaotic signatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate nonequilibrium criticality and chaos in a two-photon-driven optomechanical system. The parametric drive preserves a discrete $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry of the optical field, while radiation-pressure coupling transfers the resulting nonlinear dynamics to a mechanical oscillator. Combining semiclassical stability analysis, exact Liouvillian spectra, and stochastic quantum trajectories, we show that this driven-dissipative optomechanical model supports both first- and second-order dissipative phase transitions. At negative detuning a second-order transition yields spontaneous breaking of the cavity-parity symmetry in the thermodynamic limit. At positive detuning the same symmetry breaking coexists with a first-order transition, signaled by metastability and by an additional symmetric Liouvillian mode. At stronger pump power the mean-field dynamics loses all stable fixed points and develops limit cycles and chaotic attractors with positive Lyapunov exponent. Quantum trajectories in this regime display chaotic-like motion, enhanced steady-state entropy, and delocalization over many entropic Liouvillian modes. These results establish two-photon-driven optomechanics as a platform where dissipative criticality, symmetry breaking, and quantum signatures of chaos emerge within the same experimentally accessible setting.

</details>

### [Forked Physics-Informed Neural Networks for Non-Markovian Open Quantum Dynamics and Control](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12567v1)

**Authors:** Zhao-Wei Wang, Kai-Yu Yuan, Feng-Hua Ren, Zhao-Ming Wang

**Summary.** This paper introduces the Forked Physics-Informed Neural Network (FPINN) to solve the difficult problem of simulating and controlling open quantum systems under non-Markovian conditions. By selectively decoupling optimization objectives at the gradient level, FPINN overcomes convergence issues faced by standard PINNs in these complex environments. The framework demonstrates superior performance in state preparation fidelity compared to existing optimal control techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it provides an end-to-end differentiable framework for quantum control in realistic, non-Markovian settings, which are crucial for understanding decoherence in physical devices like superconducting qubits or trapped ions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is unifying quantum simulation and control for open quantum systems, specifically overcoming the failure of standard Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) when modeling non-Markovian dynamics.

**Main result.** The proposed Forked PINN (FPINN) successfully achieves joint optimization of simulation and control by decoupling objectives at the gradient level, outperforming established methods like GRAPE in fidelity for state preparation.

**Method.** The authors extend FPINNs by adding a dedicated control branch, utilizing selective gradient flow to transform an intractable multi-task optimization into a well-conditioned one.

**Model / system.** The work models open quantum dynamics using the Quantum State Diffusion (QSD) approach for non-Markovian environments. The system is tested on a two-qubit Heisenberg XXX model, governed by master equations involving bath and interaction Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Ground-state population evolution ($P_{00}(t)$), fidelity of state preparation, and the characteristic signatures of information backflow in non-Markovian dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The memory parameter ($\gamma$) characterizing non-Markovianity, coupling strengths ($\Gamma$), and time-dependent control field amplitudes/frequencies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard PINNs fail due to multi-objective optimization conflicts when applied to the coupled differential equations arising from non-Markovian dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the simulation of ground-state population ($P_{00}(t)$) using both RK4 (reference) and FPINN across varying dissipation strengths ($\Gamma$) and memory parameters ($\gamma$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the challenge of non-Markovian PINNs, details the FPINN architecture with its shared trunk and three independent branches, defines a composite loss function ($L_{mod} + L_{tar} + L_{pos}$), and validates the method through numerical simulations on the two-qubit model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) provide a pathway to reunify the simulation and control of quantum systems, in which these two tasks are typically decoupled in traditional strategies. However, most work remains confined to Markovian environments. When applied to non-Markovian systems, standard PINN architectures fail to converge reliably due to multi-objective optimization conflicts arising from the coupled differential equations. To address this fundamental limitation, we extend our previously proposed forked PINN (FPINN) by incorporating a dedicated control branch. By decoupling the optimization objectives at the gradient level via selective gradient flow, our method turns a previously intractable multi-task optimization into a well-conditioned one, allowing simulation and control to be optimized jointly without compromise. Numerical simulations on a two-qubit Heisenberg XXX model confirm that our framework faithfully reproduces the features of non-Markovian dynamics, including decoherence and information backflow. Taking a state-preparation task on the same model as an example, our FPINN achieves higher fidelity than gradient ascent pulse engineering, chopped random basis, and standard PINNs, with the advantage becoming more pronounced as the environment becomes more dissipative and more Markovian. The generated pulses are also noticeably smoother, which is advantageous for experimental implementation. Our framework thus provides a unified, end-to-end differentiable paradigm for simulation and control of open quantum systems, with potential implications for quantum computing, simulation, and control.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper explores the non-equilibrium thermodynamics of the quantum vacuum in acoustoelectric devices where charge carriers drift rapidly. By analyzing phonon-plasmon interactions using open quantum systems methods, the authors demonstrate that the vacuum fluctuations acquire a measurable thermal character. This establishes these solid-state systems as promising experimental platforms for studying fundamental quantum vacuum effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly connects concepts from quantum field theory (vacuum effects) to condensed matter systems (semiconductors/phonons) using advanced open quantum system formalism, which is highly relevant for AMO physics and quantum optics applications.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the quantum vacuum acquires thermal properties when subjected to non-equilibrium conditions created by charge drift in acoustoelectric systems.

**Main result.** The system's fluctuations acquire a thermal character, characterized by an effective temperature $T_{AE}$ proportional to the drift velocity and phonon wavevector, suggesting these platforms can model Unruh-like effects.

**Method.** The analysis employs open quantum systems techniques, calculating power spectra using the Wiener-Khinchin theorem and deriving non-equilibrium fluctuation-dissipation relations.

**Model / system.** The system models an electromechanically coupled mechanical oscillator interacting with plasmons and phonons in a semiconductor under a constant drift current. The dynamics are governed by coupling terms derived from the total Hamiltonian $H = H_0 + H_{int}$.

**Key observables.** Effective temperature ($T_{AE}$), power spectrum of mechanical fluctuations ($S_x(\omega)$), and non-equilibrium occupation number ($n_{AE}(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drift velocity ($v_d$), phonon wavevector ($q$), effective temperature ($\sim \hbar v_d q / k_B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the quasielectrostatic approximation and that plasmons are initially prepared in the quantum ground state for noise calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the mechanical resonator coupled to moving charge oscillations. Figure 2 compares occupation numbers and effective temperatures between ideal thermal and non-equilibrium acoustoelectric regimes. Figure 3 shows the normalized power spectrum comparison.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by analyzing the Hamiltonian, deriving equations of motion for the oscillator (Langevin approach), calculating two-time correlation functions in the frequency domain, and finally establishing a non-equilibrium fluctuation-dissipation relation that yields the effective temperature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum vacuum can assume thermal properties as a consequence of system kinematics, highlighting the nuance of our definition of particles in quantum field theory. Here, we explore this phenomenon in acoustoelectric systems, involving the interaction of phonons and plasmons, where the charge carriers drift at a constant velocity exceeding the speed of sound. Through an open quantum systems analysis, we show that the acoustoelectric quantum vacuum acquires a thermal character with a temperature defined by the drift velocity and the phonon wavevector. Realistic parameters yield effective temperatures of several Kelvin, establishing acoustoelectric systems as a promising platform for the investigation of quantum vacuum effects.

</details>

### [The Infraparticle Edge](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13001v1)

**Authors:** Soo-Jong Rey  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13001v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **4/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Kondo & dissipative impurity` **1/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2607.13001_figures/2607.13001_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: A stable particle contributes an isolated atom at s = m2. The charged QED sector begins at the same threshold and carries an integrable continuum edge. The unresolved photon environment transfers the atomic weight into the continuum.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a sophisticated open-system approach to study the spectral edge of charged particles interacting with soft photons in QED. By analyzing the energy marginals through projections and Laplace transforms, the author demonstrates that the interaction smears the sharp mass shell into an 'infraparticle edge.' This edge possesses a power-law singularity characteristic of unparticles, directly linking its exponent to the unresolved photon environment.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how environmental coupling (like soft photon emission) fundamentally alters particle spectral features, moving beyond simple mass-shell descriptions into continuum physics relevant to quantum field theory renormalization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives the spectral edge of charged particles by analyzing their interaction with unresolved (soft) photons within the framework of soft Quantum Electrodynamics (QED). It aims to determine how environmental coupling modifies the expected particle spectrum.

**Main result.** The resulting inclusive spectral measure exhibits an infraparticle edge characterized by a local power law, mirroring that of a gapped unparticle spectrum. This exponent is determined by the unresolved photon sector's soft intensity ($\eta_h$).

**Method.** The analysis employs open-quantum-system techniques, utilizing projections of the quantum instrument and Laplace transforms to analyze energy marginals in both real and imaginary time.

**Model / system.** The model is based on charged particles interacting with a soft photon bath (soft QED). The system is treated as an open quantum system where the environment causes dephasing, dissolving sharp spectral features.

**Key observables.** Spectral measure $ho_{	ext{inc}}(s)$, inclusive energy distribution $	ext{d}Ph(E)$, and various exponents ($\eta_h$, $\eta_{	ext{edge}}^a$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The infrared floor ($\mu$), the separation scale ($\Lambda$), and the soft intensity exponent ($\eta_h$) are crucial parameters determining the edge behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes a clear separation between hard (resolved) and soft (unresolved) photon contributions, allowing for the systematic calculation of exponents from each sector.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the theoretical transition from an isolated mass shell (stable particle) to a continuum edge due to unresolved photons in QED.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the soft QED quantum instrument, projecting it onto hard and inclusive channels, calculating the resulting spectral density using Laplace transforms, and finally deriving the power-law behavior of the infraparticle edge while separating bath and hard exponents.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

I derive the charged-particle spectral edge from the quantum instrument of soft QED. I use two projections of that instrument. Tracing over unresolved photons gives the reduced hard-sector channel. Pushing the outcomes to total energy gives the inclusive energy distribution. Its Laplace exponent is fixed by the diagonal soft intensity. For ${\rm d} N_h(ω)=η_h{\rm d}ω/ω+{\rm d} N_{h,\mathrm{reg}}(ω)$, I obtain $ρ_{\mathrm{inc}}(s)\sim Cθ(s-m^2)(s-m^2)^{-1+η_h}$. I retain the coherence kernel and derive hard-sector dephasing and the spectral edge from two contractions of one soft environment. The diagonal coefficient $κ_{aa}$ fixes the endpoint exponent, while $\frac12(κ_{aa}+κ_{bb}-2\operatorname{Re}κ_{ba})$ fixes the dephasing exponent between hard alternatives. I then classify infrared energy marginals, derive the finite-resolution residue $Z(μ)=(μ/Λ)^{η_h}$, prove stability under infrared-integrable perturbations, and separate the bath exponent from a hard threshold exponent. For the one-electron spectral measure, the hard threshold factor is regular. The resulting edge has the local power law of a gapped unparticle spectrum, while its exponent remains a response coefficient of the unresolved photon sector.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes Dynamical Quantum Phase Transitions (DQPTs) in finite systems quenched from thermal equilibrium using a pseudo-Hermitian framework. It demonstrates that temperature acts as a control parameter, leading to a universal threshold temperature above which the DQPTs are entirely suppressed for sufficiently large system dimensions ($N \ge 5$). This establishes a general principle for controlling quantum criticality via thermal occupation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful tool—the mixed-state Loschmidt amplitude in quasi-Hermitian settings—to study non-equilibrium critical phenomena (DQPTs) under realistic thermal conditions, linking quantum criticality directly to statistical mechanics principles.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how thermal effects suppress Dynamical Quantum Phase Transitions (DQPTs) when a finite-dimensional system is quenched from a thermal equilibrium state.

**Main result.** A dimensionality-dependent threshold temperature ($T_{th}$) exists for $N \ge 5$, above which the DQPTs are fully suppressed because the Loschmidt amplitude loses all real zeros. The quasi-Hermitian framework provides the rigorous foundation for this derivation.

**Method.** The authors construct and analyze a mixed-state Loschmidt amplitude using a metric-stationary pseudo-Hermitian framework, generalizing pure-state dynamics to thermal ensembles via purification.

**Model / system.** The model is an $N$-level system composed of a two-level sector coupled to $N-2$ spectator states. The analysis focuses on the quench dynamics governed by quasi-Hermitian Hamiltonians ($H^\dagger = \eta H \eta^{-1}$).

**Key observables.** Mixed-state Loschmidt amplitude $G(t)$, its zeros (indicating DQPTs), and the rate function $r(t, T)$ exhibiting cusps at critical times.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System dimension ($N$), temperature ($T$ or $eta$), and the metric operator ($\eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary analysis relies on the fixed-metric (metric-stationary) quench assumption ($\eta_i = \eta_f$), which simplifies the dynamics by ensuring equivalence with a Hermitian system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the motion of Fisher zeros in the complex time plane, showing coalescence at $T_{th}$ for finite $N$, and demonstrate how rate function cusps vanish above a critical temperature $T^*$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the mixed-state Loschmidt amplitude within the quasi-Hermitian framework. It then applies this to an N-level model, deriving explicit forms for $G(t)$ and identifying the condition for DQPTs based on thermal weights. Finally, it analyzes the temperature dependence leading to the finite threshold temperature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate dynamical quantum phase transitions (DQPTs) in finite-dimensional systems prepared in thermal equilibrium states and subjected to a sudden quench. A mixed-state Loschmidt amplitude is constructed from first principles within a metric-stationary pseudo-Hermitian framework, providing a self-contained derivation of the finite-temperature quench dynamics. Applying this framework to an $N$-level model consisting of a two-level sector coupled to $N-2$ spectator states, we find that temperature controls the DQPTs through the redistribution of thermal weights among the eigenstates. This mechanism leads to a dimensionality-dependent threshold temperature that becomes finite when the Hilbert-space dimension reaches five, above which the Loschmidt amplitude loses all real zeros and the DQPTs are fully suppressed. The thermal suppression mechanism suggests a general principle for controlling dynamical criticality through thermal occupation, while the quasi-Hermitian framework provides the self-consistent foundation for its rigorous derivation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work re-examines the Mpemba effect by treating the preparation of initial states as an optimal control problem for granular fluids. Using Pontryagin's principle, the authors show that the best protocols are 'one-bang,' and more significantly, they prove that stochastic thermostats impose fundamental limits on how far the system can deviate from equilibrium during cooling. This establishes a general link between non-equilibrium relaxation dynamics, optimal driving, and state accessibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical granular fluids, the methodology—linking anomalous relaxation rates (Mpemba effect) to optimal control theory and state constraints—provides a powerful general framework applicable to non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum systems, such as those involving engineered dissipation or measurement backaction.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study revisits the Mpemba effect by incorporating the explicit aging protocol used to prepare non-stationary initial conditions, rather than just selecting favorable states.

**Main result.** Optimal preparation protocols are found to be one-bang controls, and crucially, stochastic thermostats impose reachable-state constraints that limit the maximum attainable asymmetry in the relaxation rate (Mpemba effect).

**Method.** The analysis employs optimal control theory, specifically Pontryagin's Maximum Principle, applied to the evolution equations derived from the Boltzmann-Fokker-Planck equation.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a granular fluid of hard spheres undergoing inelastic collisions and subjected to a stochastic thermostat. The dynamics are analyzed using the first Sonine approximation for the Velocity Distribution Function (VDF).

**Key observables.** Excess kurtosis ($\kappa_2$), which controls the cooling rate; relaxation-rate asymmetry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Stochastic thermostat intensity ($\chi(t)$); restitution coefficient ($\alpha$); Homogeneous Cooling State (HCS) parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the first Sonine approximation of the Boltzmann-Fokker-Planck equation, and it assumes no singular arcs exist in the optimal control solution.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the focus is on deriving analytical results for optimal protocols and constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from formulating the aging stage as an optimal control problem to applying PMP, determining that the optimal protocol is one-bang, and finally establishing how state accessibility limits the magnitude of the Mpemba effect.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Mpemba effect, whereby an initially hotter system relaxes faster than a colder one towards a common final state, is often analysed within the kinetic framework by assuming non-stationary initial conditions that are selected a priori. Here, we revisit this viewpoint by explicitly incorporating the aging protocol used to prepare those states. Focusing on uniformly heated granular fluids, we formulate the preparation stage as an optimal-control problem in which the energy injection is tuned to generate the initial conditions that maximise or minimise the subsequent relaxation rate. Within the first Sonine approximation, this optimisation reduces to extremising the excess kurtosis of the velocity distribution function, the quantity controlling the cooling rate at fixed temperature. Applying Pontryagin's maximum principle, we show that the optimal preparation protocol is always a one-bang protocol and determine the corresponding extremal values of the excess kurtosis. Most importantly, we find that the stochastic thermostat imposes non-trivial reachable-state constraints: the accessible non-Gaussianities are bounded by those of the homogeneous cooling state, thereby limiting the relaxation-rate asymmetry and the maximum attainable Mpemba effect. These results demonstrate that the strength of the kinetic Mpemba effect cannot be disentangled from the accessibility of the underlying non-equilibrium states. More generally, our work establishes a connection between anomalous relaxation, optimal control, and state accessibility in non-equilibrium systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This review summarizes the application of ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy to study topological materials. It details how tracking electronic and spin relaxation on femtosecond timescales reveals fundamental coupling mechanisms like electron-phonon interactions. The work is crucial for advancing the understanding and practical control of quantum phases in next-generation spintronic and quantum computing devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it uses time-domain measurements (ultrafast optics) to probe non-equilibrium quantum states, which shares methodological overlap with open quantum systems studies involving rapid decoherence or thermalization dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to understand the nonequilibrium dynamics within topological materials by investigating various coupling mechanisms between charge, spin, orbital, and lattice degrees of freedom.

**Main result.** The study details how ultrafast spectroscopy can reveal distinct relaxation pathways—such as electron-phonon scattering and spin conversion—and even drive photoinduced topological phase transitions in these quantum systems.

**Method.** The primary method is ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy, which uses femtosecond pulses to excite the material into a non-equilibrium state and then monitors its subsequent relaxation dynamics.

**Model / system.** The focus is on topological materials, including insulators and semimetals (like $\text{Bi}_2(\text{Se}, \text{Sb})_3$), characterized by symmetry-protected band structures such as Dirac cones and Weyl nodes. The analysis covers both bulk and surface electronic states.

**Key observables.** Transient change in reflectivity ($\Delta R/R$), spin polarization dynamics, coherent phonon oscillations (ISRS vs DECP), and terahertz conductivity changes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Femtosecond to nanosecond time resolution; visible to near-infrared wavelengths for optical probing; temperature dependence is crucial for distinguishing bulk vs. surface effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation requires carefully disentangling contributions from the bulk material versus the topologically protected surface states, as conventional measurements might be dominated by the former.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic setups (OPOP), time evolution of coherent oscillations, and comparative plots showing distinct relaxation behaviors for surface vs. bulk electronic/spin states at different temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The review progresses from introducing ultrafast spectroscopy as a tool to summarize progress on various topological systems, detailing specific physical processes like electron-phonon coupling, spin dynamics, and culminating in future directions combining multi-dimensional spectroscopy with advanced theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological materials, characterized by symmetry-protected nontrivial band structures such as Dirac cones and Weyl nodes, host diverse quantum phenomena, with potential applications in quantum transport, spintronics, and nonlinear optics. Ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy has emerged as a powerful tool for exploring nonequilibrium dynamics in these systems. Its femtosecond resolution allows charge, spin, orbital, and lattice interactions to be tracked on their intrinsic timescales, thereby revealing key coupling mechanisms in topological phases. This review summarizes progress in ultrafast spectroscopic studies of topological insulators, topological semimetals, and magnetic topological materials. We first discuss the relaxation pathways of photoexcited surface and bulk electronic states, emphasizing electron-phonon scattering, surface-bulk charge transfer, and ultrafast spin conversion. We then examine population inversion in Dirac and Weyl semimetals, spin-polarization dynamics associated with tilted Weyl bands, and the effects of magnetic order on topological states, including coherent phonon and magnon excitations, magnetically driven topological transitions, and terahertz emission. We further review photoinduced topological phase transitions driven by electronic correlations, lattice distortions, and magnetic order under intense optical excitation, highlighting routes toward nonthermal control of quantum phases. Finally, we outline future directions that combine multidimensional ultrafast spectroscopy with temporal, energy, momentum, and spin resolution and advanced theoretical modeling to establish a unified picture of nonequilibrium topological states. This review aims to provide a useful reference for ultrafast studies of topological quantum materials and to advance their applications in high-speed, low-power information processing, spintronics, and quantum technologies.

</details>

**Authors:** Shuai Zeng

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that imposing a strong non-Abelian conservation law on a spin chain allows local thermal noise to generate system-size-diverging distillable entanglement. Using advanced techniques from representation theory and QBP, the authors prove this scaling holds exactly for certain models like the dimer chain. This shows that symmetry can stabilize quantum correlations against thermal decoherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical understanding of how symmetry constraints can fundamentally alter thermal physics, turning expectedly finite entanglement into an unbounded resource relevant for quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether local thermal fluctuations can generate system-size-diverging distillable entanglement when the thermalization process is constrained by a strong non-Abelian conservation law.

**Main result.** Strong non-Abelian symmetry allows local thermal noise to activate an operational resource, leading to distillable entanglement scaling as $E_{\mathrm{D}} \sim rac{1}{2} \log_2 N$.

**Method.** The authors employ representation-space protocols and advanced techniques like Quantum Belief Propagation (QBP) combined with saddle-point approximations to derive asymptotic bounds on entanglement.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on finite-range SU(2)-invariant spin chains, particularly using the exactly solvable dimer chain model. The system is analyzed under thermal evolution governed by $e^{-eta H}$.

**Key observables.** Distillable Entanglement ($E_{\mathrm{D}}$), lower bound entanglement measure ($Y_N$), and the twisted partition function $f_{n,eta}(g)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$ or $T>0$), system size ($N$), and the crossover scale $T^*(N) \sim \Delta / \ln N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on bounding errors using techniques like Haar averaging approximations in QBP, and establishing asymptotic limits for large systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that entanglement ($	ext{ED}$) grows logarithmically with $N$ at fixed $T>0$, confirming the predicted scaling $rac{1}{2} \log_2 N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing a lower bound on entanglement using representation theory, then applying QBP and saddle-point methods to derive asymptotic bounds for the partition function, culminating in exact results for the dimer chain.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermal noise ordinarily suppresses quantum entanglement. We show that a strong non-Abelian conservation law can convert local thermal fluctuations into an unbounded operational resource. For a broad class of finite-range $SU(2)$-invariant spin chains restricted to the global-singlet sector, an explicit representation-space protocol yields $Y_N=\frac12\log_2 N+O_β(1)$, and hence $E_{\mathrm{D}}\geq Y_N$, throughout a finite high-temperature interval. Local thermal fluctuations produce subsystem spins $j\sim\sqrt N$, whose globally locked irreducible representations contain $\log_2(2j+1)\sim\frac12\log_2N$ ebits. An exactly solvable dimer chain exhibits a sharper effect: its zero-temperature state is unentangled across the cut, whereas every fixed $T>0$ produces $E_{\mathrm{D}}=\frac{1}{2}\log_2 N+C(T)+o(1)$, with crossover scale $T_*(N)\simΔ/\ln N$. Exact diagonalization of a nonintegrable chain is consistent with the predicted scaling. Thus heating can activate system-size-diverging distillable entanglement across a macroscopic bipartition when thermalization is confined by a non-Abelian conservation law.

</details>
