**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that time-averaged entangling power serves as an effective tool to diagnose symmetry structures in light-matter models like the Quantum Rabi Model. They show that this diagnostic reveals a distinct signature—a peak—at points corresponding to hidden symmetries (AQRM), contrasting with the response seen at manifest symmetries (JC model). This highlights the utility of entanglement measures for probing fundamental quantum symmetries.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, non-standard operator diagnostic that moves beyond simple energy level analysis to probe the deep structural properties (hidden vs. manifest symmetries) of quantum Hamiltonians in open or interacting systems relevant to cavity QED and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To use time-averaged entangling power as an operator diagnostic tool to distinguish between hidden symmetries (like those in the AQRM) and manifest symmetries (like $U(1)$ in the JC model) within light-matter interaction systems.

**Main result.** The time-averaged entangling power exhibits distinct spectral responses: sharp peaks at integer biases for the hidden symmetry of the AQRM, contrasting with a weak dip observed at the manifest $U(1)$ point of the JC model.

**Method.** The diagnostic involves calculating the time-averaged entangling power ($ar{e}_p$) by analyzing how the system's spectral expansion is reorganized by different symmetries. Calculations are performed using finite input ensembles (Haar or coherent state averages) due to the infinite dimensionality of the oscillator space.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the Quantum Rabi Model (QRM), which describes a qubit coupled to a single harmonic oscillator. Key regimes analyzed include the Jaynes-Cummings (JC) model (with manifest $U(1)$ symmetry) and the Asymmetric Quantum Rabi Model (AQRM), which possesses a parameter-dependent 'hidden' symmetry at integer biases ($\epsilon/\omega \in \mathbb{Z}$).

**Key observables.** Time-averaged entangling power ($ar{e}_p$), reduced purity ($	ext{Tr}ho_A^2$), and the spectral structure of energy differences ($\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias parameter $\epsilon$, coupling strength $g$, oscillator frequency $\omega$, and the integer ratio $\epsilon/\omega = k \in \mathbb{Z}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space using either Fock-space truncation (Haar average) or a fixed mean occupation ($ar{n}$) coherent-state average.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare spectral features, showing crossings in symmetric limits and avoided crossings when bias is introduced. A key figure compares results from different input ensembles (fixed-radius vs. Gaussian coherent sampling), confirming peak locations at integer biases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the QRM Hamiltonian and its limiting cases (JC/AQRM). It then introduces time-averaged entangling power as a diagnostic, showing how it responds differently to manifest versus hidden symmetries by analyzing spectral reorganization effects. Finally, it compares finite ensemble calculations for robustness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum Rabi model is a standard effective Hamiltonian in studies of light-matter interaction, capturing the simplest nontrivial setting in which a qubit couples to a single harmonic oscillator. Within the broader Rabi family, we focus on two special cases: the Jaynes-Cummings (JC) model, which carries an explicit $U(1)$ symmetry, and the asymmetric quantum Rabi model (AQRM), which possesses a parameter-dependent "hidden'' symmetry that appears only at integer bias, $\varepsilon/ω\in\mathbb{Z}$, and is not manifest in the Hamiltonian. We use the time-averaged entangling power as an operator-level diagnostic of these symmetry structures. Since the oscillator Hilbert space is infinite-dimensional, we compare two finite input ensembles: a Haar average after Fock-space truncation and a coherent-state average at fixed mean occupation $\bar{n}$. Both diagnostics show peaks at the integer-bias points of the AQRM, where the hidden symmetry resides. In contrast, the manifest $U(1)$ symmetry at the JC point instead gives a weak dip. Thus, the time-averaged entangling power responds to both the hidden symmetry and the $U(1)$ symmetry in the Rabi family, with the sign of the response indicating how the symmetry reorganizes the spectral expansion. These results demonstrate that the entangling power can serve as an operator diagnostic to reveal the presence and properties of hidden and manifest symmetries in light-matter systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive continuum model using the Onsager variational principle to explain cellular engulfment. It uniquely posits that entropic depletion forces provide the necessary initial 'push' before specific receptor binding sustains the process. The resulting kinetic phase diagram offers quantitative predictions for successful viral uptake, linking fundamental physics principles to virology and nanomedicine.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biophysics, the rigorous application of variational principles (Onsager) to couple multiple dissipative/restoring forces (viscoelasticity, entropy) provides a powerful theoretical template for analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics in complex soft matter systems relevant to AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental, unanswered question of the initial driving force required for ligands and receptors to approach each other during receptor-mediated endocytosis.

**Main result.** It predicts a kinetic phase diagram defining critical biomolecule concentrations and optimal particle sizes (e.g., 30–60 nm) necessary for successful cellular engulfment.

**Method.** The authors develop a unified continuum model rooted in the Onsager variational principle to derive the kinetics from a total free energy landscape incorporating multiple physical contributions.

**Model / system.** The system models viral or nanoparticle uptake by a cell membrane, treating the process as having distinct phases: initial entropy-driven approach (depletion forces), followed by sustained binding and viscoelastic cytoskeletal deformation.

**Key observables.** Kinetic phase diagram, critical biomolecule concentration ($c_{crit}$), optimal virus radius ($R_{opt}$), and engulfment time $t(h)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Membrane tension ($\gamma$), Young's moduli ($E_v, E_m$), viscosity coefficients ($\mu, \eta_m$), and biomolecule concentration ($c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the use of the Onsager variational principle for dynamics, employs the Helfrich-Canham Hamiltonian for membrane bending, and models cytoskeleton viscoelasticity using a linear solid approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate depletion-driven engulfment mechanisms, showing how entropic forces stabilize uptake mediated by the cytoskeleton across different stages of contact.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the initiation problem to developing a unified free energy functional (summing entropy, binding, membrane, and cytoskeleton terms). It then applies the Onsager principle to derive the kinetic equation, culminating in phase diagram predictions and size optimization criteria.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A fundamental question in receptor-mediated endocytosis remains unanswered: what initial driving force brings ligands and receptors into close proximity? While previous models assume pre-existing contact and overlook this initiation problem, we propose that entropic forces from nanoscale biomolecules in crowded cellular environments provide the essential driving mechanism. We develop a unified continuum model rooted in the Onsager variational principle, where engulfment depth serves as the generalized coordinate and the driving force derives from a free energy landscape of entropic, binding, membrane, and cytoskeleton contributions. The framework naturally incorporates: (i) entropy-driven adhesion as initiation; (ii) ligand-receptor binding as the sustaining force; (iii) membrane deformation via the Helfrich-Canham Hamiltonian; and (iv) cytoskeleton viscoelasticity through the elastic-viscoelastic correspondence principle. The kinetic phase diagram predicts a critical biomolecule concentration for initiation, a lower bound of ligand density for complete engulfment, a finite size window for engulfable particles, and an optimal virus radius of 30--60 nm that decreases with increasing binding energy. The Onsager solubility condition naturally yields the phase boundaries. The model exhibits asymptotic consistency with the classic Asakura-Oosawa result in the large-particle flat-surface limit. Stiffer cells lead to longer engulfment times and narrower size windows. Strikingly, the optimal size matches HIV-1 dimensions under physiologically realistic parameters. This work provides a variational foundation for cellular uptake with implications for virology, nanotechnology, and drug delivery.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how to maintain the complex 'random walking shock' structure of interacting particle models like ASEP within a finite volume by engineering specific boundary conditions. By deriving necessary constraints on these boundary rates, the authors characterize non-trivial stationary distributions for systems exhibiting non-equilibrium dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools for handling non-equilibrium steady states in interacting particle systems, which is foundational knowledge for understanding driven quantum many-body systems or open quantum dynamics where boundaries play a critical role.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to find boundary mechanisms for interacting particle systems that preserve a 'random walking shock' structure within a finite volume, while allowing ordinary particles to pass freely but reflecting the shocks.

**Main result.** The authors successfully identify semi-open boundary conditions that confine the random walking shocks and allow for the characterization of non-trivial two-species, non-reversible stationary distributions in finite volumes.

**Method.** The analysis involves extending known infinite volume results to finite volumes by defining specific boundary jump rates ($p_0, q_0, p_K, q_K$) that maintain conservation laws related to 'second-class particles.'

**Model / system.** The study focuses on one-dimensional interacting particle systems, including the Asymmetric Simple Exclusion Process (ASEP) and a generalized Bricklayers process (BLP), modeled by coupled dynamics of particle occupations ($\omega_i$) and second-class particles ($\zeta_i$).

**Key observables.** Stationary distributions $
u_{\varrho, m}$ for the coupled pair $(\omega, \zeta)$, hydrodynamic flux/velocity, and the conservation of the sum of second-class particles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Boundary rates ($p_0, q_0, p_K, q_K$), system size $K$, and parameters governing asymmetry (e.g., $p, q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The boundary mechanism must be open for first-class particles but reflective for second-class particles to maintain conservation laws; the analysis relies on treating $\varphi$ as an arbitrary cylinder function.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically extends infinite volume results by defining boundary generators ($L_{	ext{bry}}$) and deriving necessary identities (setting $E[L\varphi]=0$) that constrain the boundary rates for both ASEP and BLP to ensure stationary states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-stationary time evolution of interacting particle systems is in general a rather difficult topic however, exceptional examples are known where hidden processes within the model make the description manageable. These hidden processes often take the form of a finite number of interacting random walks and in some cases, rather than being hidden, were very explicitly revealed as second class particles associated with the model. The examples of asymmetric exclusion and exponential bricklayers model are known in this context, where such distributional structure was demonstrated in infinite volume. Here we find boundary mechanisms that save this remarkable structure in finite volume of the model with semi-open boundaries that let ordinary particles, but not the shocks, through. This finding also allows to characterise nontrivial two-species, non-reversible stationary distributions subject to our special boundary rates.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using trapped fermionic atoms as ultra-sensitive quantum sensors to search for Axion Like Particles via their spin-dependent interactions. It develops a sophisticated multi-probe measurement technique that overcomes theoretical limitations in parameter estimation. This method allows setting stringent new upper bounds on the APL coupling constants, advancing dark matter searches.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies advanced concepts from quantum metrology (QFIM, CRB) to a physical system involving ultracold atoms in optical lattices, making it highly relevant for AMO physicists studying precision sensing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to constrain the unknown mass ($m_a$) and coupling constants ($g_e g_n$) of Axion Like Particles (APLs) by detecting exotic pseudoscalar spin-spin interactions.

**Main result.** By employing a multi-probe detection strategy, the authors successfully circumvent the singularity in the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix, achieving exclusion limits down to $10^{-7}$ for coupling constants when $m_a \le 10^{-3} 	ext{ eV}$.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum metrology principles, specifically calculating the ultimate sensitivity limit via the inverse of the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) derived from multi-probe measurements.

**Model / system.** The system involves fermionic atoms trapped in an optical lattice, whose spin interactions are mediated by APLs. The physics is modeled using a Fermi-Hubbard Hamiltonian incorporating axion-mediated background fields ($h_{\perp}, h_{\parallel}$).

**Key observables.** Observed particle counts ($\{n_k\}$) from the sensor spins interacting with the source spins.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Axion mass ($m_a$), coupling constants ($g_e g_n$), lattice tunneling rate ($J$), and on-site interaction ($U$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The key methodological assumption is that using multiple, orthogonally positioned probes resolves the singularity inherent in estimating multiple parameters from a single observable.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup involving source/sensor spins (Fig. 2) and demonstrate how multi-probe measurements resolve singularities in posterior probability distributions compared to single-probe analysis (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical interaction via a Yukawa potential, models the system using a Fermi-Hubbard Hamiltonian, develops the quantum estimation theory framework using QFIMs, and finally proposes and analyzes the multi-probe detection strategy to achieve parameter constraints.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The search for the axion like particles (APLs)-one of the deepest puzzles in modern cosmology-may hold the key to understanding dark matter and dark energy. In this work, we introduce a setup taking advantage of the quantum metrology techniques to constrain the hypothetical mass and coupling constants of APLs by employing exotic pseudoscalar spin-spin interactions between fermions mediated by ALPs. A key advantage of our approach is the exploitation of position-dependent spin sensor results to the high sensitivity of the probe. To simultaneously investigate the axion mass and coupling constants, we invoke a multi-probe detection strategy. Through this strategy, we circumvent the singularity of the quantum Fisher information matrix as an ultimate upper bound on the sensitivity of any probe. For the axion masses in the range of $m_a {\le} 10^{-3} \text{eV}$, this setup can exclude values of axion coupling constants $g^e_pg^n_p$ down to $10^{-7}$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a method to rapidly create massive Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) entangled states using atomic nuclear-spin qubits. It proposes a novel Quantum Ferromagnetic Gate (QFG) realized via Rydberg interactions in alkaline-earth atoms. By iteratively applying this gate through a 'gluing circuit,' the authors demonstrate a scalable pathway toward generating highly entangled quantum resources for quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum information theory (GHZ states) with advanced AMO physics techniques (Rydberg blockade on nuclear spins), providing a concrete, scalable platform proposal for quantum computation using neutral atoms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is the fast, high-fidelity generation of large-scale Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states.

**Main result.** A protocol using a Quantum Ferromagnetic Gate (QFG) and repetitive 'gluing circuits' allows for the theoretical realization of GHZ states up to 243 qubits using nuclear-spin qubits in alkaline-earth atoms.

**Method.** The state generation relies on a measurement-based quantum computation approach involving a specific QFG gate, single-qubit rotations, and projective measurements on an ancillary qubit.

**Model / system.** The system uses atomic nuclear-spin qubits of alkaline-earth-like atoms trapped in arrays. The interactions are mediated by Rydberg blockade effects, which govern the dynamics described by a complex Hamiltonian involving single-atom and Rydberg interaction terms.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F$), QFG realization time ($6\pi/\Omega_m$), and error sources like Rydberg-state decay and blockade leakage.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rydberg Rabi frequency ($\Omega_m$), detuning ($\Delta$), blockade strength ($V$), and atomic position fluctuations ($\sigma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes the feasibility of assembling large-scale atomic arrays and relies on approximations for error analysis, such as ignoring states with more than a few Rydberg excitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate state evolution under the QFG mechanism, showing phase profiles and fidelity comparisons between different pulse engineering techniques used to mitigate blockade errors.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of large-scale GHZ generation, proposes the QFG gate using nuclear spins, details the 'gluing circuit' for scaling, analyzes the underlying Hamiltonian dynamics including Rydberg interactions, and discusses error sources from decay and position fluctuations in appendices.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large-scale Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger~(GHZ) state is useful for quantum technologies but difficult to be prepared. Here, we propose fast measurement-based preparation of large-scale GHZ states by a four-qubit quantum phase gate with nuclear-spin qubits of alkaline-earth-like atoms, which is named as quantum ferromagnetic gate~(QFG) due to its analogy to the alignment of molecular magnetic moments in a classical magnet. A high-fidelity Rydberg-mediated QFG can be realized in a time of $6π/Ω_{\text{m}}$ with $Ω_{\text{m}}$ the maximal Rydberg Rabi frequency. From a product state of three data atom and one ancilla atom, a gluing circuit with one QFG, two single-qubit gates, and a projective measurement of the ancilla can generate a 3-qubit GHZ state, and repetition of this gluing circuit can lead to 9, 27, 81, 243, $\cdots$-qubit GHZ states. Analyses based on currently available techniques show that a 243-qubit GHZ state is realizable, and more qubits can be entangled with higher detection fidelity.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers addressed the challenge of decoherence in polar molecule quantum systems caused by thermal motion within optical tweezer arrays. They developed sophisticated control schemes, including geometric engineering and programmable echo sequences, which successfully suppressed sensitivity to positional jitter. These methods enabled the generation of high-fidelity entanglement ($\mathcal{F} \approx 0.976$), pushing the boundaries for molecular quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant experimental demonstration of active quantum error mitigation using mechanical degrees of freedom. The ability to use controlled molecular motion to refocus environmental noise is highly relevant for building scalable quantum simulators and quantum memories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The coherence of quantum interactions between polar molecules trapped in optical tweezers is degraded by thermal motion and positional fluctuations, limiting entanglement fidelity.

**Main result.** By employing dynamic geometric control and programmable molecular motion (geometric echo), the authors achieved a high two-molecule Bell state fidelity of $\mathcal{F}= 0.976^{+0.008}_{-0.011}$ in directly laser-cooled molecules.

**Method.** The study uses optical tweezer arrays controlled by SLMs and AODs to engineer the dipolar interaction geometry, combined with advanced quantum control techniques like dynamic decoupling and echo protocols.

**Model / system.** The system involves polar molecules (e.g., $	ext{CaF}$) trapped in 2D optical tweezers, whose coherent dynamics are governed by a dipolar coupling Hamiltonian $J$. The methodology focuses on minimizing the sensitivity of this interaction to motional states and positional noise.

**Key observables.** Entanglement fidelity ($\mathcal{F}$), Dipolar coherence quality factor ($Q$), Single-body coherence time ($	au_{sb}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trap position fluctuations (down to 10 nm scale), Geometric parameters ($	heta, z_0$), Molecular temperature/motional occupation ($ar{n}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations are identified as residual axial positional disorder ($\sigma_z$) and the need for complex site-dependent calibrations across the array.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry control using SLM/AODs, show how geometric engineering minimizes motional sensitivity ($S$), and demonstrate the improvement in coherence quality factor ($Q$) achieved by implementing echo protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying thermal motion as the primary decoherence source to proposing static geometric solutions (magic angles), then advancing to dynamic control via programmable molecular motion (echo protocol) to suppress positional noise, culminating in high-fidelity entanglement measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum information systems made of optical tweezer arrays of ultracold molecules, thermal motion of molecules degrades the coherence of their interactions, which limits entanglement fidelity and the concomitant scientific applicability of these systems. We show that by controlling the geometry of the dipolar interaction, even when a molecule occupies many motional states in the tweezer, coherence can be preserved. We characterize several geometries that suppress sensitivity to thermal fluctuations. We further use programmable, coherence-preserving motion of the molecules during entanglement to refocus dephasing from relative positional jitter of the tweezers, which is relevant even on the 10 nm scale. These methods yield substantially improved dipolar coherence and enable generation of two-molecule entanglement with a Bell state fidelity of $\mathcal{F}= 0.976^{+0.008}_{-0.011}$ in directly laser-cooled molecules.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a geometric framework to map the entire space of possible non-equilibrium responses in Markov jump processes. It shows that these responses are constrained by the topology of the underlying state graph via 'uniquely constructable' tree families. This provides powerful, mathematically rigorous bounds for designing systems with specific sensitivity profiles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical structure (polytope geometry) to non-equilibrium dynamics, offering rigorous bounds on how systems can respond to external stimuli, which is highly relevant for designing synthetic biological or engineered quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To systematically characterize the full range of possible non-equilibrium response behaviors achievable by a system, moving beyond existing theoretical bounds.

**Main result.** The entire space of achievable responses is conjectured to be structured as a polytope whose vertices are determined by 'uniquely-constructable' tree families derived from the graph topology.

**Method.** The authors use graph theory (rooted spanning trees) and geometric arguments, showing that physical realizability restricts the response space to a convex combination of contributions from these unique topological structures.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a continuous-time Markov jump process on a graph G with N nodes. The dynamics are governed by a master equation, and the focus is on calculating the rate sensitivity ($\partial_\lambda \langle Q angle$) of steady-state observables to external control parameters $\lambda$.

**Key observables.** Steady-state distribution ($\pi_j$), response sensitivity vector components ($\partial_\lambda \ln \pi_j$), and the expected value of an observable $\langle Q angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The topology of the graph G, kinetic rates $W_{ij}$, and the control parameter $\lambda$ that tunes the rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the graph is strongly connected to ensure a unique steady state. Crucially, it distinguishes between 'multi-constructable' (non-physical) and 'uniquely-constructable' tree families.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of different types of rooted spanning trees on small graphs (like 3 states), contrasting those that are uniquely constructible (leading to nested or twisted hysteresis motifs) versus those that fail due to structural contradictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the general problem of response characterization, establishing the role of tree families in bounding the response space via convex combinations, and finally applying these topological constraints to specific physical models like molecular binding on a 3-state graph.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonequilibrium systems can exploit energy to amplify their sensitivity to external stimuli, allowing them to be harnessed for a variety of functions in both engineered devices and living organisms. An assortment of theoretical results capture different facets of this nonequilibrium amplification, including fluctuation-response relations as well as bounds and constraints that limit the potential behavior. Here, we take a broader perspective, aiming for a systematic characterization of the full range of possible response behaviors. For a wide class of nonequilibrium dynamics, we identify a collection of optimally sensitive models whose behavior is determined by the topology of the state space. We further conjecture that every response can be written as a convex combination of these optimal models, thereby structuring the entire space of responses. We use this geometric perspective to put sensitivity limits on nonmonotonic biochemical input-output functions, and identify all optimal kinetic schemes for the unordered binding of molecules among three sites.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how to maintain optimal Heisenberg-limited precision in quantum measurements when the system suffers from complex non-Markovian noise and controls are not instantaneous. By comparing measurement-only, active recovery, and dynamical decoupling protocols, the authors show that while all can theoretically achieve ideal scaling, practical limitations dictate which method performs best at finite control rates.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete, quantitative comparisons of different control strategies for overcoming noise in quantum sensing, directly impacting how robust quantum sensors can be designed under realistic physical constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is achieving Heisenberg-limited quantum estimation scaling ($	ext{QFI} \propto t^2$) when the probe is subjected to non-Markovian noise and control operations are restricted to a finite rate.

**Main result.** Protocols relying purely on measurements show poor scaling, whereas active recovery or dynamical decoupling protocols achieve superior convergence rates in the finite-rate regime.

**Method.** The work analyzes three distinct quantum control schemes (measurement-only, active recovery, and dynamical decoupling) by establishing connections to the quantum Zeno effect and analyzing the resulting Quantum Fisher Information scaling.

**Model / system.** The system involves a quantum probe coupled to an environment, modeled via total Hamiltonians. The analysis compares performance metrics across different noise models (Markovian vs. non-Markovian).

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information ($	ext{QFI}$), fidelity of the state evolution, and convergence rates as control frequency approaches zero.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite control rate ($\delta t$), time $t$, and the structure of the noise generators $\{E_k\}$ relative to the signal generator $G$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis rigorously compares performance in the finite-rate limit, contrasting with theoretical results derived assuming infinitely fast (Zeno) controls.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the three control schemes and show that AQEC and DD maintain high fidelity compared to AQED, especially as measurement intervals decrease.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a connection between optimal metrology protocols and the quantum Zeno effect. It then rigorously analyzes the performance of measurement-only, active recovery, and dynamical decoupling methods when controls are limited to finite rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, it has been shown that protocols utilizing infinitely fast controls, such as quantum error correction, can in principle restore Heisenberg-limited frequency estimation in the presence of a broad class of non-Markovian noise models arising from coupling to finite-dimensional environments. However, these controls differ substantially from those used to address Markovian noise, and their underlying physical mechanism remains unclear. In this work, we establish a direct connection between these protocols and the quantum Zeno effect, and extend the framework to infinite-dimensional environments. We delineate three types of constructions: (a) protocols that rely solely on measurements, (b) protocols using an active recovery after measurements and (c) protocols using dynamical decoupling and rigorously analyze the performance of each when controls can be applied at only a finite rate. While protocols relying purely on measurements can be engineered for noise models where active recoveries are fundamentally impossible, they result in the quantum Fisher information exhibiting a quadratically worse dependence on the control frequency. Surprisingly, while dynamical decoupling protocols are possible whenever protocols relying only on measurements can be engineered, the quantum Fisher information has the same dependence on control frequency as the active recovery protocols. Numerical simulations suggest that the improvement offered by dynamical decoupling may work in regimes beyond the perturbative setting where our rigorous theorems apply.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates solitary waves formed by attractively interacting SU(N) fermions on a lattice. It establishes that these bound states exhibit dynamics analogous to quantum walks, mirroring classical soliton behavior. The study also quantifies how disorder affects this confinement and derives effective hopping parameters for the composite excitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects concepts from integrable many-body physics (Bethe ansatz) with observable quantum transport phenomena (quantum walks), providing a detailed theoretical framework for understanding emergent collective excitations in strongly interacting fermionic systems relevant to ultracold atomic gases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the formation, dynamics, and disorder robustness of bound states (solitary waves) in attractively interacting SU(N) fermions on a 1D lattice.

**Main result.** Bound states propagate as quantum walks, exhibiting a dynamical signature analogous to classical soliton motion. Furthermore, their confinement is shown to be enhanced by increasing the component number N under disorder.

**Method.** The analysis employs exact diagonalization in fixed-momentum sectors and utilizes Bethe ansatz results for the dilute limit. Dynamics are probed via quench protocols and correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The system models attractively interacting SU(N) fermions on a 1D ring lattice, governed by an extended Fermi-Hubbard type Hamiltonian. The continuum limit maps this to the Gaudin–Yang–Sutherland (GYS) model.

**Key observables.** Energy spectrum bands, density-density correlation $G(r)$, N-body correlator $T_N(r)$, and the Hellinger distance ($d_H$) comparing dynamics to single-particle quantum walks.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Attractive interaction strength $|U|$, hopping amplitude $J$, disorder strength $W$, and component number $N$ (SU(2), SU(3), etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the lattice problem to integrable models in the dilute limit, and uses perturbative expansions for effective hopping in the strongly coupled regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy spectra showing distinct bound bands (Fig. 1, Fig. 6), density evolution confirming quantum walk behavior after rescaling time (Fig. 2), and correlation length decay under disorder (Fig. 3, Fig. 8).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the lattice Hamiltonian and analyzing the energy spectrum via Bethe ansatz solutions to characterizing the dynamics using quench protocols and correlations. It then explores the continuum limit mapping and finally examines the robustness of these bound states against disorder.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the formation, dynamics, and disorder robustness of bound states in attractively interacting SU(N) fermions on a one-dimensional ring lattice. Using exact diagonalization in fixed-momentum sectors and Bethe ansatz exact results as a guide, we resolve the many-body spectrum into bands related to the possible partitions of the particles into bound composites, and characterize their internal structure also through density-density and N-body correlations. A pinning quench protocol reveals a transition from dispersive spreading to dynamical localization as the attractive interaction increases relative to the single-particle hopping. We find that the bound state dynamics for one-particle per component occurs as a many-body quantum walk similar to that of a single particle with a re-normalized effective mass. Such a property, that is the quantum version of the shape-preserving motion of classical solitons, can provide the dynamical signature of the fermionic solitary waves. We probe the robustness of the fermionic bound-state dynamics under on-site disorder.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically maps the complex finite-temperature phase diagram of a Josephson-coupled superconducting bilayer under an in-plane magnetic field. It distinguishes between two types of transitions: the Pokrovsky–Talapov transition driven by soliton entry, and Berezinskii–Kosterlitz–Thouless melting governed by vortex unbinding. The analysis uses advanced RG techniques to characterize these competing critical lines.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how competing topological defects (solitons vs. vortices) dictate phase transitions in superconducting systems, which has parallels with critical phenomena studied in quantum many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates finite-temperature phase transitions (Pokrovsky–Talapov and Berezinskii–Kosterlitz–Thouless) in a Josephson-coupled bilayer superconducting film subjected to an in-plane magnetic field.

**Main result.** The phase diagram is characterized by three distinct boundaries: the PT soliton-entry line separating C/FF from IC/Bloch SC, and two BKT-like thermal melting lines (C–L and IC–L).

**Method.** The authors employ Renormalization Group (RG) analysis on a continuum action derived from the Ginzburg-Landau functional, supplemented by Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a Josephson-coupled superconducting bilayer ($\psi_1, \psi_2$) placed in an in-plane magnetic field ($H$). The effective theory involves phase dynamics governed by relative and common phase sectors.

**Key observables.** Correlation exponents (expected to approach 1/4 at BKT transitions), helicity moduli ($\Upsilon$), and the soliton density $ho_{	ext{sol}}$ determining the C–IC boundary.

**Important parameters / regimes.** In-plane magnetic field parameter $k_0 = 2\pi H a / \Phi_0$, temperature $T$, and Josephson coupling strength $K$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on weak-fugacity RG schemes and assumes that the C–IC transition is governed by soliton entry, distinct from thermal vortex unbinding.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing the coexistence of PT (soliton) and BKT (thermal melting) boundaries, and show finite-size scaling diagnostics confirming the expected critical exponents for the thermal transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops an effective continuum action, applies RG techniques to track vortex fugacities and stiffnesses, determines the zero-temperature PT boundary via fixed-sector free energy crossings, and analyzes the finite-temperature BKT boundaries using correlation function decay and helicity modulus criteria.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study finite-temperature phase transitions in a Josephson-coupled bilayer superconducting film with compact layer phases under an in-plane magnetic field. At zero temperature, where thermally excited layer vortices are absent, the relative-phase sector undergoes a Pokrovsky--Talapov (PT) commensurate--incommensurate (C--IC) transition from a commensurate Fulde--Ferrell (C/FF) state to an incommensurate Bloch superconducting (IC/Bloch SC) state. At finite temperature, compactness separates two distinct defect mechanisms. The C--IC boundary remains a PT soliton-entry line: interlayer Josephson vortex--antivortex-pair solitons enter with the square-root onset $ρ_{\rm sol}\propto [k_0-k_0^c(T)]^{1/2}$. Thermal melting is instead Berezinskii--Kosterlitz--Thouless (BKT)-like, with correlation exponent $η=1/4$ at the boundary, but the active vortex channel changes across the phase diagram. Josephson locking suppresses elementary layer vortices in the C/FF state and selects a same-vorticity layer-pair BKT channel, whereas elementary layer vortices control melting of the IC/Bloch SC state.

</details>

**Summary.** This roadmap details the development of Quantum Entanglement PET (QE-PET), which aims to improve standard PET imaging by measuring the quantum entanglement between annihilation photons. By characterizing this entanglement, researchers propose new diagnostic biomarkers related to tissue oxygenation and pH mapping. While promising, translating these sophisticated quantum measurements into clinical scanners remains a significant ongoing challenge.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum optics/information theory directly into applied medical physics. The ability to use fundamental quantum properties like entanglement as a measurable biomarker for biological processes is highly relevant to open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to advance Positron Emission Tomography (PET) by leveraging the quantum entanglement inherent in annihilation photons, leading to a novel modality called Quantum Entanglement PET (QE-PET). This aims to overcome limitations like random coincidences and improve diagnostic capabilities.

**Main result.** The study establishes that quantum correlation is preserved even when one photon undergoes Compton scattering, allowing the degree of quantum entanglement ($	ext{CQE}$) to be measured as a potential diagnostic biomarker for tissue pathology or oxygenation.

**Method.** The research combines theoretical modeling of entangled photon states with descriptions of advanced detector systems (e.g., crystal scintillators, semiconductors) capable of measuring polarization and Compton scattering angles simultaneously.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the annihilation of an electron-positron pair into two back-to-back photons ($	ext{511 keV}$). The theoretical framework models the quantum state using entanglement measures like $	ext{CQE}$ and analyzes how this correlation changes upon interaction with matter (Compton scattering).

**Key observables.** Degree of Quantum Entanglement ($	ext{CQE}$), angular correlations ($C(	heta_S)$), relative angle ($\varphi$), and the energy spectrum of scattered photons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scattering angles ($	heta_S$), coincidence rates, and the specific physics of annihilation mechanisms (direct vs. positronium).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary open question is the successful translation of entanglement-based imaging into routine clinical practice due to low detection sensitivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate event reconstruction in prototype scanners, polarization/Compton scattering planes, various annihilation processes, and plots showing how $	ext{CQE}$ and correlation measures depend on the Compton scattering angle ($	heta_S$).

**Paper structure.** The paper functions as a roadmap, first establishing the physics background (entanglement preservation during scattering), detailing necessary detector technologies, presenting theoretical observables ($	ext{CQE}$, $C(	heta_S)$), and concluding with the potential diagnostic applications (oxygenation mapping, pH imaging).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Annihilation photons are quantum entangled in their polarization, a property that is not accessible in state-of-the-art clinical Positron Emission Tomography (PET). This roadmap describes the current status of research in the emerging field of quantum entanglement applications involving these photons for medical diagnosis. It outlines the underlying physics phenomena and the development of detector systems that can serve as a foundation for future Quantum Entanglement PET (QE-PET) scanners. These scanners will be capable of utilizing the entanglement between annihilation photons by measuring their Compton scattering on electrons. This roadmap comprises up-to-date experimental results on the study of quantum entanglement and the decoherence of annihilation photons, alongside the current theoretical understanding of these phenomena. The methods and detector technologies described herein are being developed (i) in order to enhance standard PET imaging by suppressing random coincidences in the reconstruction of annihilation site density distributions, (ii) in order to establish the degree of quantum entanglement as a diagnostic biomarker for tissue pathology and oxygenation, and (iii) in order to elaborate a method for pH imaging. Whether entanglement-based imaging and pH mapping can be successfully translated into clinical practice remains an open question and a subject of exciting ongoing research. This roadmap serves as an invitation to the scientific community to join this burgeoning field.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an enhanced Standard Basis Operator method to calculate the properties of the Bose-Hubbard model. It improves upon prior work by correctly accounting for more on-site states at finite temperatures and predicts complex phase transitions in multi-component systems. The methodology is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated quantum gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools (improved SBO) to study interacting quantum gases, offering quantitative improvements over previous approximations by systematically handling local Hilbert space truncation effects.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors aim to develop an improved theoretical method for calculating both ground-state and finite-temperature properties of the Bose-Hubbard model.

**Main result.** They successfully enhanced predictions by accounting for a necessary number of on-site states, while also predicting non-trivial phase diagram deformations in the two-component case.

**Method.** The core method is an improved Standard Basis Operator (SBO) approach formulated within a nonperturbative Green's function framework, utilizing Random Phase Approximation (RPA).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the single and two-component Bose-Hubbard model in three dimensions, governed by a Hamiltonian involving hopping ($J$), on-site interaction ($U$), and chemical potential ($\mu$).

**Key observables.** Phase boundary, excitation spectra, boson occupancies (D_alpha), critical temperature (T_c/J).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength to hopping ratio (U/J), chemical potential ($\mu/U$), and temperature ($T/J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the RPA for decoupling higher-order Green's functions, which is noted to violate algebraic rules in multi-component systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare critical lines (SBO vs. QRA vs. TNS), show lobe structures at different temperatures, and illustrate how increasing the number of retained local Fock states improves accuracy.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the SBO method using Green's functions, applies RPA to solve for these functions in momentum space, derives self-consistency equations from spectral theorems, and discusses limitations regarding multi-component extensions and numerical stability near phase transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate an improved standard basis operator (SBO) method for the single and two-component Bose-Hubbard model in three dimensions. In the first case, nonzero temperature predictions are qualitatively and quantitatively enhanced by taking into account necessary number of on-site states, not just three as in previous works. Performance of the final numerical calculations is also improved by asymptotic analysis of the self-consistent equations near the critical line. Obtained results are compared with Monte-Carlo, tensor networks, Quantum Rotor Approach and experimental data. In the two-component case, SBO generalizes rather poorly, being able to account for intra-species thermal and quantum fluctuations, but not the inter-species ones. Deeper reasons for this situation are discussed. Still however, non-trivial deformation of the phase diagrams is predicted, together with first-order phase transitions steered by the changes in chemical potential.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the use of active phase stabilization techniques, borrowed from quantum communications, to enable long baseline optical interferometry using fiber connections. By successfully stabilizing an interferometer over 170km, the authors demonstrate that source sizes can be resolved far below the diffraction limit. This significantly advances the feasibility of building full-scale, high-resolution astronomical instruments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it bridges advanced quantum optics (phase stabilization, quantum estimation) directly into a major area of astrophysics/quantum sensing, providing concrete engineering pathways for next-generation astronomical instruments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to overcome the diffraction limit in astronomical imaging by implementing long baseline optical interferometry, which requires mitigating severe phase noise accumulated over long fiber links.

**Main result.** By applying quantum communication-inspired active phase stabilization techniques, the authors successfully demonstrated measurements on a 170km pseudo-baseline that allowed for source size estimation significantly below the system's diffraction limit.

**Method.** The work combines experimental implementation of interferometry with theoretical analysis using quantum optimal measurement estimators to extract source parameters from noisy fringe visibility data.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a long baseline optical interferometer utilizing single-mode fibers over baselines up to 170km, emulating a pseudo-thermal astronomical source. The system incorporates active phase stabilization derived from quantum communication principles.

**Key observables.** Fringe visibility values, Complex Degree of Coherence (CDC), and the estimated angular size of the celestial source.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Baselines up to 350km are targeted; sub microarcsecond resolution is the ultimate goal. Phase noise reduction across long fiber links is critical.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that advances in low-loss fibers will enable full-scale deployment, and the quantum estimator approach outperforms classical methods for source size determination.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup schematic, phase spectral density comparisons showing noise reduction via stabilization, and fringe visibility measurements confirming successful signal recovery.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for long baseline interferometry to detailing the experimental implementation using fiber links. It then focuses on demonstrating that quantum-inspired phase stabilization techniques are necessary and sufficient to recover usable interference fringes and estimate source sizes accurately.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Astronomical observations allow us to better our understanding of the universe. As we observe smaller and more distance features, we run into the diffraction limit of our observation system. This limit is a function of the wavelength observed and the size of the primary aperture used. We can synthesize a larger primary aperture by implementing interferometry. Long baseline optical interferometry would lead to significant improvements in astronomical imaging resolution. Building optical interferometers with free space baselines becomes significantly more difficult as the baseline increases. A promising alternative is to use optical fiber to connect telescopes. Fiber-based interferometers are much more susceptible to phase noise than their free-space counterparts due to inhomogeneities in the fiber medium. This leads to significant degradation of interferometric signals used for astronomical measurements. We implement phase stabilization techniques used in quantum communications to stabilize an optical interferometer with a 170km pseudo-baseline. We use a quantum optimal measurement technique with this interferometer to resolve the extent of a source four times smaller than the diffraction limit of the system within 1.5% error. These results bring the potential for a full-scale on-sky long baseline interferometer significantly closer. A 350km baseline optical interferometer at 1550nm would allow us to resolve sub microarcsecond features in the universe.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs an exact, local, SU(2)-invariant parent Hamiltonian using spin-1 moments on frustrated lattices. It demonstrates that the ground state manifold naturally encodes loop constraints characteristic of valence-bond physics. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding quantum magnetism in 2D and 3D systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a rigorous, microscopic starting point (spin-1) for studying complex constrained quantum phases like valence-bond solids, bridging concepts from spin models, dimer coverings, and gauge theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if constrained valence-bond loop manifolds can arise exactly as the ground-state space of a local, microscopic SU(2)-invariant spin Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The construction provides an exact spin-1 parent Hamiltonian for these frustrated systems, showing that loop constraints emerge naturally from the zero-energy manifold.

**Method.** The authors use an AKLT-inspired fractionalization procedure and project symmetry-allowed perturbations into the resulting ground-state manifold to derive low-energy dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model is a spin-1 Hamiltonian defined on checkerboard and pyrochlore lattices, constructed using projectors acting on triangular faces. It aims to capture physics related to valence-bond loops in frustrated magnets.

**Key observables.** Spin correlations within the ground state manifold (determined by loop connectivity) and effective low-energy pseudospin dynamics (e.g., emergent XY model terms).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling constant J, and the lattice structure (checkerboard vs. pyrochlore).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that projecting perturbations into the ground state manifold captures the low-energy physics, and specific mathematical lemmas are used to establish the local kernel structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the checkerboard and pyrochlore lattices, showing where the projector acts on triangular faces. Other figures detail the AKLT-like construction using virtual spin-1/2 singlets.

**Paper structure.** The paper first constructs the microscopic Hamiltonian ($H_0$) via projectors. It then analyzes the zero-energy manifold using fractionalization techniques and proves its connection to loop states. Finally, it calculates matrix elements of perturbations within this manifold to derive effective low-energy Hamiltonians for different lattice types.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct an exact spin-$1$ parent Hamiltonian for constrained valence-bond loop manifolds on the checkerboard and pyrochlore lattices. The Hamiltonian is local, SU(2)- and time-reversal-invariant, and built from positive-semidefinite projectors acting on triangular faces. Each projector removes only the maximally polarized state of a triangle, so the model is frustration-free. Its zero-energy states are generated by an AKLT-like construction in which each spin-$1$ moment is resolved into two virtual spin-$\tfrac12$ degrees of freedom, singlets are formed inside every crossed plaquette or tetrahedron, and the physical spin-$1$ Hilbert space is recovered by projection. The resulting ground states are fully packed singlet-loop states on the corner-sharing lattice. Thus, a loop or dimer constraint, usually introduced as part of an effective Rokhsar--Kivelson description, appears here as the exact zero-energy manifold of a microscopic spin Hamiltonian. We analyze spin correlations within this manifold and show that, for a fixed loop covering, they are determined by loop connectivity. We also project symmetry-allowed perturbations into the ground-state manifold and derive the resulting low-energy pseudospin dynamics. The checkerboard and pyrochlore cases differ sharply. On the pyrochlore lattice, tetrahedral symmetry removes simple local bias terms, and the leading nontrivial next-nearest-neighbour Heisenberg perturbation gives an emergent spin-$\tfrac12$ XY model on the diamond lattice of tetrahedron centers. These results give an exact spin-$1$ microscopic starting point for constrained valence-bond physics in two and three dimensions, and show how loop, dimer, and gauge-theoretic descriptions can be approached from a small-spin, SU(2)-invariant frustrated magnet.

</details>

**Summary.** This research investigates $	ext{NdMgAl}_{11}	ext{O}_{19}$ as a next-generation cryogenic magnetocaloric material. By demonstrating superior cooling performance down to $113 	ext{ mK}$ using only a small magnetic field change, the authors establish it as an excellent candidate for low-field adiabatic demagnetization refrigeration. The underlying physics is attributed to the QSL-like ground state arising from geometric frustration in its triangular lattice structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on achieving extreme cooling temperatures through quantum fluctuations in frustrated magnets directly relates to understanding exotic ground states and magnetic entropy management, which has parallels with many-body physics studied in AMO contexts.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing high-performance magnetic refrigerants for sub-Kelvin cooling regimes by maximizing retained magnetic entropy at low temperatures while suppressing long-range magnetic ordering.

**Main result.** The material $	ext{NdMgAl}_{11}	ext{O}_{19}$ exhibits outstanding refrigeration performance, achieving cooling down to $113 	ext{ mK}$ using only a small field change of $2 	ext{ T}$. This is attributed to its Quantum Spin Liquid (QSL)-like ground state.

**Method.** The study employed magnetic susceptibility, specific heat, and quasi-adiabatic demagnetization cooling measurements on single crystals.

**Model / system.** The system is the hexaaluminate $	ext{NdMgAl}_{11}	ext{O}_{19}$, where $	ext{Nd}^{3+}$ ions form a triangular lattice structure. The physics is modeled around the concept of Quantum Spin Liquids (QSLs) to achieve high magnetic entropy retention.

**Key observables.** Magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), specific heat ($C_m$), retained magnetic entropy ($S_m$), and minimum attainable temperature ($	ext{T}_{\min}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low-field regime (e.g., $\Delta H = 2 	ext{ T}$), sub-Kelvin temperatures (down to $113 	ext{ mK}$), and the effective spin-1/2 ground state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The outstanding performance is linked to frustration combined with strong spin-orbit coupling and crystal-electric-field effects, which suppress magnetic ordering down to very low temperatures.

**Figures summary.** Figures show structural details (triangular lattice), temperature dependence of magnetic properties ($\chi$), and cooling curves demonstrating the magnetocaloric effect.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes the crystal structure via XRD, analyzes magnetic interactions using susceptibility and specific heat measurements to confirm QSL-like behavior, and culminates in quantitative assessment of its performance as a low-field adiabatic demagnetization refrigerator (ADR).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic refrigeration in the sub-Kelvin regime requires refrigerant materials to retain a large magnetic entropy at low temperatures by suppressing magnetic ordering. Quantum spin liquids (QSLs), which evade long-range magnetic ordering while retaining strong quantum fluctuations to the lowest temperatures, therefore provide a promising platform for realizing high-performance magnetic refrigerants. Here, we investigate the magnetic ground state and the magnetocaloric effect of the hexaaluminate, NdMgAl$_{11}$O$_{19}$, in which the Nd$^{3+}$ ions form a network of triangular lattices. Magnetic susceptibility and specific heat measurements indicate a magnetically dynamic state down to 50~mK, consistent with a QSL state. Specific heat measurements further reveal substantial magnetic entropy retained below 50~mK. Quasi-adiabatic demagnetization measurements demonstrate a superior cooling performance of NdMgAl$_{11}$O$_{19}$, which can be cooled to 113~mK from 1.9~K by only a small magnetic field change of 2~T. The outstanding refrigeration performance is attributed to the persistent spin fluctuations associated with the QSL-like ground state, together with a large effective \textit{g} factor and the smallness of the exchange interactions along the easy-axis direction. This study demonstrates that frustration, combined with strong spin-orbit coupling and crystal-electric-field effect in the rare earth magnets provides a promising design principle for next-generation cryogenic magnetic refrigerants.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel quantum metrology technique for non-destructive testing that reads out mid-infrared sample information using visible/near-IR photons via correlated photon pairs generated by SPDC. By operating at ultra-low powers and achieving shot-noise-limited performance, this method offers a potential paradigm shift compared to expensive classical mid-IR instrumentation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems as it demonstrates a practical application of quantum correlations (entanglement) in sensing, moving beyond fundamental demonstrations into routine NDT tools.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop quantum metrology techniques for non-destructive testing (NDT) by reading out information about mid-infrared (mid-IR) samples using visible/near-IR photons.

**Main result.** The technique demonstrates ultra-low power impingement ($	ext{60 pW}$) and achieves shot-noise-limited performance, potentially surpassing classical methods for applications like QFTIR spectroscopy and OCT.

**Method.** It utilizes a nonlinear interferometer based on spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) to generate correlated signal (detection) and idler (probing) photon pairs.

**Model / system.** The experimental platform is a nonlinear Michelson-type interferometer pumped by a visible laser through a nonlinear crystal (e.g., ppKTP). The quantum correlation allows the mid-IR interaction to modulate the near-IR interference pattern.

**Key observables.** Signal photon interference visibility, Power Spectral Densities (PSDs), and B-scans from OCT imaging.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultra-low probe powers ($	ext{60 pW}$), shot-noise limit performance, and the energy conservation relation $\omega_p = \omega_s + \omega_i$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique assumes that quantum correlations can effectively transfer mid-IR sample information to the detectable visible/near-IR signal photons.

**Figures summary.** Figures show simulated PSDs, time-domain and Fourier-domain interferograms comparing open vs. blocked idler arms, and an experimental setup diagram using a HeNe laser reference.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of undetected photon metrology, details the nonlinear interferometer setup for QFTIR/OCT, presents benchmarking against classical systems (showing superior SNR scaling), and concludes with future outlooks on increasing source brightness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Metrology with undetected photons is an emerging technique that leverages quantum effects and photon correlations (entanglement) to retrieve valuable information in a target spectral range (e.g., mid-infrared, mid-IR) using measurements in an easily accessible domain (e.g., visible, near-IR). The underlying quantum process of spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) is utilized to generate non-degenerate correlated signal and idler photons to serve as detection and probing photons, respectively. Sensing with undetected photons enables important advantages, such as ultra-low probe powers, room-temperature operation, and shot-noise-limited detection. In this contribution, we apply a quantum nonlinear interferometer based on an SPDC source to perform applied mid-IR spectroscopy, mid-IR microscopy, and mid-IR optical coherence tomography (OCT) as among the most promising techniques for quantum-based routine non-destructive testing. Moreover, we characterize the system, benchmark it against classical systems, and provide a prospective outlook for this new technology.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the critical challenge of implementing quantum error correction on noisy superconducting qubit arrays containing physical defects. By developing and testing active defect exclusion strategies, the authors demonstrated that proactively bypassing faulty components yields substantial improvements in logical fidelity compared to standard or passive decoding methods. This proves a vital principle for scaling up solid-state quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete experimental evidence for the necessity of hardware-aware error correction techniques, which is crucial for scaling up any solid-state quantum computation platform.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing quantum error correction for solid-state superconducting qubit arrays that inevitably contain defective or underperforming components due to fabrication imperfections.

**Main result.** Actively excluding underperforming components leads to a dramatic reduction in logical error probability, showing up to 2.8X improvement over standard defect-ignorant approaches.

**Method.** The authors implemented and compared strategies—including active defect exclusion via super-stabilizer reconstruction—to operate distance-5 surface codes on physical hardware.

**Model / system.** The system is a superconducting qubit processor arranged in a square lattice (e.g., 120 qubits), utilizing the surface code for error correction.

**Key observables.** Logical error probability per round, failure rate suppression factor ($\Gamma$), and mutual information between qubits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance (d=5), qubit coherence times ($T_1, T_2 < 80\ \mu s$ for defect criteria), and measured error rates of couplers/qubits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on specific processor limitations, such as the unavailability of mid-circuit resets, necessitating 'reset-free syndrome extraction'.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare logical error per round across various defect handling strategies (uninformed vs. noise-informed decoding) and show performance improvements when excluding defective components.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares multiple QEC protocols on a physical chip, detailing defect identification criteria, implementing active exclusion circuits using super-stabilizers, and benchmarking performance via memory and stability experiments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A solid-state quantum-computing architecture will require the fabrication of arrays of many coupled qubits. It is inevitable that this process will produce qubits and couplers with varying performance, with some components underperforming due to imperfect fabrication. Quantum error-correction requires high-performing components and hence these defects must be dealt with, either by adapting the code to exclude the defects, or by informing the decoder to accommodate defects in post-processing. Here we implement and compare strategies to operate distance-5 surface codes on a quantum processor consisting of a square-lattice array of 120 superconducting qubits. We demonstrate a dramatic reduction in the probability of a logical error in a memory experiment by excluding underperforming components, compared with both a standard approach of ignoring defects, and a defect-aware decoding approach. We observe up to 2.8X improvement in logical errors per round when excluding defects compared with the standard defect-ignorant approach (1.62% compared to 4.49%). In contrast, defect-aware decoding gives only modest gains. Defects are also expected to be particularly harmful for measurement-based logical operations. Using a stability experiment we show that excluding defects resurrects measurement-based logic gate performance, observing a 6.3% per-round suppression of failure rate when excluding defects, compared to zero suppression otherwise. Furthermore, we show a further substantial decrease in logical errors when using leakage post-selection in combination with our defect exclusion strategies, resulting in a distance-5 code outperforming the best distance-3 in one basis. Our experiments therefore give a proof-of-principle demonstration of the essential utility of defect exclusion methods in the scale-up of solid-state quantum computing approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an experimental demonstration of a hybrid quantum blockchain designed for practical use in supply chain management. By deploying protocols like Quantum Byzantine Agreement over commercial fiber optics, the system achieves superior fault tolerance and high throughput. It establishes a viable blueprint for secure, scalable, and decentralized information processing in the emerging quantum era.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a direct engineering demonstration of applying advanced quantum information concepts (QKD, QBA) to solve a complex distributed systems problem (blockchain), which is highly relevant to the physical realization of quantum networks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is ensuring that blockchain technology maintains its cryptographic integrity and decentralized fault-tolerant resilience when operating in the quantum computing era.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a fully operational hybrid quantum blockchain architecture achieving fault tolerance approaching one-half, surpassing classical limits, with a practical throughput of 500 transactions per second for food traceability.

**Method.** They proposed and experimentally implemented a four-layer hybrid architecture combining Quantum Byzantine Agreement (QBA) protocols with photonic integrated circuits deployed over commercial fiber networks.

**Model / system.** The system is built on a five-user network in Nanning, China, utilizing photonic integrated circuits for the quantum physical layer. It integrates classical blockchain frameworks (Hyperledger Fabric) with quantum primitives like BB84 QKD and OTUH-QDS signatures.

**Key observables.** Fault tolerance approaching one-half; throughput of approximately 500 transactions per second; secure key rates up to 273.49 kbps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Five nodes (N=5) tolerating two malicious participants (f=2); link loss of 13.2 dB over an average length of 13.1 km.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the feasibility of integrating quantum advantages into existing, commercially available classical telecommunications infrastructure for real-world deployment.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the four-layer architecture workflow; Figure 2 shows the star-like physical network topology in Nanning; Figure 3 details the six stages of the food traceability application flow from input to viewing results.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the quantum blockchain challenge, proposes the layered hybrid architecture (Application $ightarrow$ Quantum Consensus $ightarrow$ Classical Logical $ightarrow$ Quantum Physical), details the implementation using QKD and QBA protocols over a metropolitan fiber network, validates it with an end-to-end food traceability application, and concludes by establishing its foundation for practical quantum data processing.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Popularized by the Bitcoin cryptocurrency, blockchain technology establishes a decentralized digital framework that utilizes cryptographic and consensus protocols to secure data against unauthorized modification. Consequently, blockchain has found broad adoption across diverse fields, including finance, data management, healthcare, and digital asset governance. In the quantum computing era, a paramount objective for blockchain is to preserve its foundational advantages of cryptographic integrity and decentralized fault-tolerant resilience. In principle, quantum digital signatures and quantum Byzantine agreement protocols offer foundational security guarantees and tolerate up to one-half of malicious nodes for blockchain. However, the practical realization of such a quantum-enhanced blockchain remains a significant and multifaceted challenge. Here, we propose and experimentally demonstrate a fully operational hybrid quantum blockchain architecture built on photonic integrated circuits and deployed over commercially available classical telecommunications infrastructure. The system achieves a fault tolerance of nearly one-half, surpassing the classical limit, while reaching consensus on a timescale of seconds. A deployed food traceability application validates the practicality of the proposed architecture, achieving a throughput of approximately 500 transactions per second. This work establishes a foundation for practical quantum blockchains, enabling secure, scalable, and decentralized information processing in the emerging quantum era.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive, noise-aware quantum workflow designed to estimate Credit Valuation Adjustments (CVA) using Quantum Amplitude Estimation. The authors introduce CABIQAE, an algorithm that adapts Bayesian inference by incorporating measured hardware contrast loss. While demonstrating improved performance over naive methods on current devices, the work highlights that practical utility is currently constrained by circuit depth and discretization limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on finance, the core methodology—developing robust quantum algorithms that explicitly account for noise-induced contrast loss during amplitude estimation—is highly relevant to any field using NISQ devices for complex simulation or optimization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the practical viability of using quantum amplitude estimation (QAE) to accelerate Credit Valuation Adjustment (CVA) calculations on real, noisy quantum hardware.

**Main result.** A novel noise-aware algorithm, CABIQAE, was shown to exploit limited quantum amplification more effectively than noise-agnostic methods on current devices. However, the overall CVA oracle remains fundamentally limited by circuit depth and discretization resolution.

**Method.** The authors developed an end-to-end workflow incorporating a specialized Bayesian iterative QAE (CABIQAE) that incorporates experimentally calibrated Grover-contrast loss into its inference process.

**Model / system.** The financial model involves calculating CVA for correlated two-asset exposures, encoded using a QCBM-based joint time-market distribution. Experiments are performed on real quantum hardware (e.g., IBM Quantum).

**Key observables.** CVA error decomposition (statistical, encoding, discretisation, hardware), final relative error of the estimator, and circuit resource counts (Logical vs. ISA gates/depth).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth, discretization resolution ($n$), query cost ($N_q$), and experimentally calibrated contrast loss parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that a quantum advantage can be extracted despite noise; the work explicitly models hardware limitations rather than assuming ideal coherence.

**Figures summary.** Figures characterize Grover amplification for toy and CVA circuits, showing empirical contrast decay. Other figures compare the final relative error and runtime of various amplitude estimation techniques under hardware constraints versus ideal theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for quantum acceleration in CVA to developing a noise-aware workflow (CABIQAE). It details the financial encoding, presents theoretical guarantees (ideal scaling), validates the approach on real hardware by analyzing resource scaling and error accumulation, and concludes with an error decomposition analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Credit Valuation Adjustment (CVA) requires repeated risk-neutral expectation estimation, making it a natural test bed for quantum amplitude estimation, whose coherent amplification can in principle reduce Monte Carlo sampling cost. Whether this advantage survives realistic financial encoding and noisy hardware remains open. We develop an end-to-end, noise-aware quantum workflow for CVA, covering market calibration, discretisation, oracle construction, hardware execution and error-budget analysis. The model combines a correlated two-asset exposure with discount and default factors, encoded through a QCBM-based joint time-market distribution and controlled payoff rotations. We introduce contrast-aware Bayesian iterative quantum amplitude estimation (CABIQAE), which incorporates experimentally calibrated Grover-contrast loss into Bayesian inference and circuit-depth selection. Hardware-calibrated experiments show that CABIQAE exploits the limited amplification available on current devices more effectively than noise-agnostic alternatives and achieves a much lower classical post-processing runtime than the noise-aware BAE baseline. The analysis further decomposes the total CVA error into statistical, encoding, discretisation and hardware contributions. The full CVA oracle remains limited by circuit depth and discretisation resolution.

</details>
