**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental demonstration of a scalable quantum blockchain protocol designed to overcome the inherent limitations of previous high-fault-tolerance schemes. By utilizing weak coherent states and a novel circular Quantum Byzantine Agreement mechanism, they significantly reduce communication complexity. This breakthrough enables practical, high-throughput quantum-secured applications like token exchanges on existing fiber infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete experimental validation of scaling solutions for distributed quantum computing primitives (like consensus), which is highly relevant for building practical quantum networks beyond simple QKD links.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing high-fault-tolerant quantum blockchain protocols face a scalability challenge, known as the 'blockchain trilemma,' requiring either exponential communication complexity or demanding multipartite entanglement.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrated a scalable quantum blockchain protocol that achieves an exponential reduction in quantum communication complexity while maintaining information-theoretic security.

**Method.** They employed a circular Quantum Byzantine Agreement (QBA) mechanism based on weak coherent states, which avoids complex multipartite entanglement requirements.

**Model / system.** The protocol was implemented on a photonic integrated circuit platform, successfully realizing a six-node network over commercially available telecommunication infrastructure to secure a token exchange application.

**Key observables.** Sustained throughput of 805.3 transactions per second with zero failures; less than 4% quantum communication resource usage compared to prior schemes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The protocol scales as $O(N^2)$ for the semi-decentralized structure, and it is secure if at least two participants are honest ($N > f+1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system assumes a layered architecture integrating quantum consensus with application-layer smart contracts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the token exchange workflow, the hierarchical two-layer architecture (quantum consensus + application), and detailed comparisons of communication complexity vs. node count for different QBA protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework by proposing the circular QBA protocol, demonstrates its implementation on a photonic platform, validates performance metrics like key rates and throughput, and applies it to a quantum-secured token exchange workflow.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

To secure modern distributed digital infrastructures, quantum blockchains exploit quantum resources to achieve information-theoretic security and surpass the classical one-third fault-tolerance bound. However, existing high-fault-tolerant protocols face a fundamental scalability challenge: the blockchain trilemma imposes either exponential communication complexity or experimentally demanding multipartite entanglement. Here, we experimentally demonstrate a scalable quantum blockchain protocol based on weak coherent states that achieves an exponential reduction in quantum communication complexity. The protocol employs a circular quantum Byzantine agreement mechanism that preserves information-theoretic security while avoiding multipartite entanglement. We implement this protocol on a photonic integrated circuit platform, realizing a six-node network over commercially available telecommunication infrastructure. Compared with previous schemes, the protocol requires less than 4% of the quantum communication resources. Leveraging this advantage, we further demonstrate a quantum-secured token exchange application achieving a throughput of 805.3 transactions per second with zero failures. These results establish a practical pathway toward scalable quantum blockchain.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces symmetry-twisted multi-entropies as nonlocal order parameters capable of classifying 2D Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phases. By analyzing expectation values involving multiple system replicas and partial symmetries, the authors derive specific quantities whose quantized phases encode the topological invariants associated with these phases. This provides a robust theoretical tool to probe entanglement structure beyond simple bipartite measures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, purely entanglement-based diagnostic tool for characterizing topological order, which is highly relevant to understanding exotic phases of matter studied in AMO physics and condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop nonlocal order parameters capable of detecting and distinguishing all bosonic Symmetry-Protected Topological (SPT) phases in two dimensions, overcoming limitations associated with local correlators.

**Main result.** The authors propose symmetry-twisted multi-entropies ($\Lambda_r^g$ and $\Omega_{g,h,k}$) that serve as order parameters whose quantized phases specify the topological invariants classifying 2D Abelian SPTs based on braiding statistics.

**Method.** The method involves defining these order parameters using expectation values of partial symmetry and permutation operations acting on multiple replicas of the system state in finite spatial regions.

**Model / system.** The focus is on bosonic SPT phases in two dimensions protected by internal discrete, Abelian unitary symmetries ($G = \prod_i \mathbb{Z}_{N_i}$). The analysis uses wavefunction expectation values rather than explicit flux insertion into the Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Nonlocal order parameters $\Lambda_r^g(\psi)$ and $\Omega_{g,h,k}(\psi)$, whose quantized phases ($\vartheta$) correspond to topological invariants (Type-I, II, and III SPT classes).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The symmetry group $G$, the number of replicas ($R$), and the structure of the Abelian symmetries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that these multi-entropy quantities effectively simulate the path integral on topologically non-trivial manifolds, allowing extraction of topological invariants purely from wavefunction expectation values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the replica structures and partial symmetry actions for the proposed order parameters ($\Lambda_r^g$ and $\Omega_{g,h,k}$), showing how they are constructed using multiple copies of the system state.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the need for nonlocal probes (Chunk 1). It then formalizes the general multi-entropy structure using replica techniques (Chunk 2). The core mathematical development involves calculating and simplifying the phase factors ($	ilde{\Omega}_{g,h,k}$) associated with plaquette boundaries on a lattice (Chunks 3 & 4), culminating in identifying this factor as a TQFT partition function.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Although symmetry-protected topological phases (SPTs) can be distinguished by their entanglement properties, it has been unclear how to extract this information directly from expectation values beyond the 1D case. Here, we close this gap and propose a pair of nonlocal order parameters that can detect and distinguish all bosonic SPTs in 2D protected by internal discrete, Abelian unitary symmetries. The desired topological invariants are extracted by these quantities by effectively simulating the SPT path integral on topologically non-trivial spacetime manifolds. Our order parameters are defined in terms of expectation values of partial symmetry and permutation operations acting on fixed numbers of replicas of the system in finite spatial regions. These expectation values correspond to symmetry-twisted versions of multipartite entanglement quantities known as multi-entropies. We show explicitly that our two order parameters detect symmetry-protected four-party and six-party entanglement, respectively, and we constrain possible "spurious" contributions. We analytically test our proposal in fixed-point lattice models. Our results suggest multipartite entanglement to be a defining feature of SPTs; indeed, we expect our methods to generalize to fermionic and higher-dimensional systems.

</details>

**Authors:** Keming He, Ziao Tang, Zetong Li, Ge Bai, Xin Wang  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12948v1>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically classifies Hypergraph Product (HGP) codes that minimize overhead during logical entanglement gate implementation by requiring the use of physical qubit permutations. The unique resulting family is then rigorously benchmarked against surface codes in simulating complex quantum circuits, demonstrating concrete performance advantages for low-overhead fault tolerance on hardware like neutral-atom arrays.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct comparison with neutral-atom arrays as a natural hardware setting, which supports nonlocal qLDPC operations while enabling the required local CNOTs via permutations, makes this highly relevant for experimental AMO physicists working on scalable quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is reducing physical spacetime overhead in fault-tolerant quantum computation by finding efficient ways to implement logical entangling gates.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the only Hypergraph Product (HGP) codes satisfying the 'phantom' condition are derived from a simplex code and a repetition code, which outperform surface codes in benchmark simulations.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical classification of CSS HGP codes based on permutation automorphisms with extensive circuit-level benchmarking using Monte Carlo simulations for GHZ state preparation and many-body simulation.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Quantum Error Correction (QEC) using binary CSS Hypergraph Product (HGP) codes, specifically the simplex–repetition family. These codes are evaluated in the context of implementing logical CNOT gates via physical qubit permutations.

**Key observables.** Logical infidelity ($1 - F$), which measures the average mismatch probability per target logical observable; code parameters like distance ($d$) and number of qubits ($n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rates ($p = 10^{-3}, 5 	imes 10^{-4}$); system sizes (up to $K=64$ logical qubits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the physical realization of the HGP structure and uses a specific noise model for circuit simulations; it also notes that the results are up to natural equivalences.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare logical GHZ-state preparation fidelity and Trotterized many-body simulation fidelity between the proposed HGP code and rotated surface codes, showing consistent outperformance of the HGP code.

**Paper structure.** The paper first theoretically classifies the unique structure of phantom CSS HGP codes (Theorem 2), then derives the specific simplex–repetition family using weight analysis (Theorem 3), and finally validates this family by benchmarking its performance against surface codes in two distinct quantum simulation tasks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Logical entangling gates are a major source of physical spacetime overhead in fault-tolerant quantum computation. Phantom codes reduce this cost by implementing every ordered in-block logical CNOT through physical qubit permutations and Pauli-frame updates. Whether this mechanism can coexist with the low-weight stabilizer structure of qLDPC codes is a central question for low-overhead fault-tolerant architectures. We give a deterministic answer within binary CSS hypergraph product (HGP) codes. Up to natural equivalences, the simplex-repetition family is the unique HGP family satisfying the phantom condition. We then evaluate this family under circuit-level noise in logical GHZ-state preparation and Trotterized many-body quantum simulation. The codes retain low-weight stabilizer checks and yield concrete advantages over rotated surface-code baselines in both benchmarks. Reconfigurable neutral-atom arrays offer a natural setting for this approach, supporting nonlocal qLDPC operations while enabling in-block logical CNOTs without additional physical operations. Together, these results make precise how permutation-based logical entangling constrains code design within the HGP framework, demonstrate the circuit-level benefits of the unique family, and guide the search for phantom qLDPC families with better asymptotic parameters for low-overhead fault tolerance on neutral-atom hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically demonstrates the Nonreciprocal Quantum Mpemba Effect (NQME) in open quantum systems coupled to biased reservoirs. By analyzing how swapping reservoir parameters affects the system's dynamics, the authors show that directional asymmetry can induce anomalous relaxation speedups without altering the underlying energy spectrum. This mechanism is crucial for understanding controlled dissipation and non-Hermitian physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how directional biases (nonreciprocity) can induce anomalous relaxation phenomena like the Mpemba effect in quantum systems, which is highly relevant for designing quantum thermal machines or controlling decoherence rates.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates a nonreciprocal quantum Mpemba effect (NQME) by analyzing how reservoir bias asymmetry affects anomalous relaxation dynamics in open quantum systems.

**Main result.** The NQME is proven to exist when the system's evolution exhibits directional asymmetry under parameter swapping, even though the underlying spectrum remains invariant due to structural symmetries.

**Method.** The analysis employs perturbation theory (Feshbach projection method) to derive an effective Liouvillian superoperator for the slow subspace dynamics. The nonreciprocity is diagnosed by comparing decoupling conditions ($\chi \cdot a = 0$) under swapped reservoir parameters.

**Model / system.** The system consists of an open quantum system coupled to two isomorphic, biased reservoirs (Left and Right). Dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation, analyzed in regimes where spectral gaps allow for effective low-dimensional modeling.

**Key observables.** The slow component of the deviation from steady state ($\deltaho_{	ext{slow}}(t)$), the decoupling vector $\chi$, and the relaxation rate asymmetry (speedup ratio $R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perturbation strength $\lambda$ and direction parameter $\eta \in (-1, 1)$; the spectral gap $\Delta$; and operating at a Liouvillian exceptional point (EP).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a substantial spectral gap ($\Delta$) to separate slow and fast modes, allowing for first-order perturbation theory approximations in $\eta\lambda$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates how swapping reservoir parameters turns the QME ON or OFF. Figure 2 shows time evolution confirming NQME by comparing decay rates under forward ($\eta=1$) versus reverse ($\eta=-1$) bias.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework using the Lindblad master equation and introduces the swap operation. It then uses perturbation theory to derive an effective low-dimensional generator, proving that nonreciprocity is carried entirely by eigenvector rotation while eigenvalues are pinned. Finally, it applies this analysis to demonstrate NQME in both generic and exceptional point regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate a nonreciprocal quantum Mpemba effect. Consider a broad class of open quantum systems, each coupled to two isomorphic reservoirs through symmetric ports. Interchanging the parameters of the two reservoirs -- a discrete operation we call the swap -- turns the quantum Mpemba effect on or off without changing the initial states. The swap modifies the Liouvillian, yet a structural symmetry pins the eigenvalues while rotating only the eigenvectors. The nonreciprocity therefore leaves no trace in the spectrum and is carried entirely by the eigenvectors. Concretely, the swap alters the far state's projection onto the slowest mode, switching whether it bypasses the slowest relaxation channel. At a Liouvillian exceptional point, the far state's relaxation switches from bypassing the slowest mode to avoiding the critical slowing, with the on--off contrast intact. There the spectrum-independent mechanism takes its purest form.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how periodically removing individuals from a stochastic population based on their trait value (harvesting) fundamentally reshapes the system's probability density. It demonstrates that this external selection process forces the system toward predictable, universal quasi-steady states or imparts constant directional drifts. This suggests a mechanism for actively manipulating complex non-equilibrium dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical population dynamics, the concept of external selection interventions leading to predictable steady states mirrors non-equilibrium steady state physics relevant in open quantum systems or driven many-body models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how periodic removal (harvesting) of individuals above or below a trait threshold reshapes the probability density of heterogeneous populations without altering their underlying stochastic dynamics.

**Main result.** Backward reshaping drives the system to a universal quasi-steady state dependent only on the harvesting frequency and threshold, while forward reshaping generates a constant effective drift exceeding unharvested rates.

**Method.** The authors use mathematical derivations based on instantaneous density clipping (reshaping) applied to solutions of stochastic processes like Brownian motion, CTRW, and Lévy walks.

**Model / system.** The model involves tracking the probability density $\rho(x, t)$ of a population whose trait value $x$ is governed by various anomalous diffusion processes on $[0, \infty)$.

**Key observables.** The shape of the probability density function $\rho(x, n\tau)$ after multiple reshaping events; the evolution of the median position $m(\tau)$; and the effective drift rate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Harvesting threshold ($x_c$), harvesting frequency ($\tau$), and process parameters defining diffusion (e.g., anomalous exponents $\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reshaping removal is treated as an instantaneous event, and convergence arguments assume that initial condition effects vanish after sufficient repetitions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the density evolution for various random walks (Brownian, CTRW, Lévy) showing convergence to a fixed shape under backward reshaping, alongside median tracking plots.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theory by first defining the mathematical framework of stochastic reshaping, then deriving analytical results for specific processes (like Brownian motion), and finally generalizing these findings to anomalous diffusion types using asymptotic arguments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Harvesting -- the periodic removal of individuals above or below a threshold trait value -- reshapes heterogeneous populations without altering their underlying stochastic dynamics. We study how repeated harvesting events steer the evolution of probability densities for classes of stochastic processes exhibiting both normal and anomalous dynamics, as well as a prototypical predator-prey model. Removal of the upper portion of the density drives the system to a quasi-steady state when viewed at the ``harvesting clock''. This state depends only on the harvesting threshold and frequency but not on the initial conditions. Removal of the lower portion of the density fixes its shape while generating a constant effective drift that exceeds that of the unharvested mean. Our results suggest the possibility of manipulating the dynamics of stochastic populations through external selection interventions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores Strong-to-Weak Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking (SWSSB) using holographic models dual to black hole physics. By calculating specific correlation functions like the Rényi-2 correlator, the authors demonstrate that SWSSB occurs when comparing different thermal ensembles. This leads to a novel Swampland conjecture connecting symmetry breaking to fundamental constraints in quantum gravity theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While deeply rooted in quantum gravity/holography, the core concept—symmetry breaking diagnosed via correlation functions in mixed states—is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Strong-to-Weak Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking (SWSSB) at finite temperature within the framework of holography.

**Main result.** Non-zero Rényi-2 and Wightman correlators in the large distance limit demonstrate SWSSB, which is linked to the emergence of weak symmetries via ensemble averaging. The authors propose a new Swampland conjecture regarding this phenomenon in quantum gravity.

**Method.** The analysis employs holographic methods using the AdS/CFT correspondence, calculating correlation functions (Rényi-2 and Wightman) by implementing projection operators through twisted boundary conditions on bulk fields.

**Model / system.** The model involves a 2D CFT dual to an Einstein-Chern-Simons theory coupled with probe matter in the bulk geometry of a BTZ black hole, analyzed at finite temperature.

**Key observables.** Rényi-2 correlator ($R^{(2)}$) and Wightman correlator ($C_W$), which are used to diagnose the presence or absence of symmetry breaking.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite temperature ($eta=1/T$), large distance limit ($|x-y|	o\infty$), and the chemical potential $\mu$ associated with charge conservation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on large-$N$ factorization, specific limiting procedures for correlation functions, and the assumption that SWSSB is diagnosed by these correlators.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-contractible cycle wrapping the BTZ horizon and show how different field types (neutral vs. charged) interact with boundaries in the bulk geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining SWSSB and setting up the holographic model, calculating correlation functions using twisted boundary conditions, demonstrating the non-zero nature of $R^{(2)}$ and $C_W$, and concluding by formulating a Swampland conjecture based on these findings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SWSSB) at finite temperature and its implications in holography. SWSSB is a novel critical phenomenon that arises only in mixed states. We demonstrate SWSSB with the BTZ black hole and show that, in the bulk, it is realized through wormhole geometries connecting the two copies in the thermofield-double construction. We point out that SWSSB can be recast as an emergence of global symmetries under ensemble averaging. We further examine the interplay between SWSSB and the swampland program, and propose a new swampland conjecture for quantum-gravity theories involving ensemble averages.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a classical analogue of Krylov complexity using phase space methods to study quantum chaos. By applying this framework to spin models like LMG, the authors show that microcanonical analysis resolves ambiguities in complexity measurements caused by saddle points or spectral instabilities. This provides a robust tool for characterizing early-time chaotic dynamics across different energy regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, mathematically rigorous tool (classicalization of K-complexity) to probe early-time chaotic dynamics in many-body systems without needing full quantum simulation, which is highly relevant for understanding thermalization and scrambling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work develops a classical counterpart for Krylov complexity, allowing the study of quantum chaos measures using phase space techniques.

**Main result.** Classical Krylov complexity accurately approximates quantum complexity at early times in semiclassical systems, and a microcanonical approach resolves ambiguities arising from dynamical instabilities like saddle points.

**Method.** It employs the classical Lanczos algorithm in phase space, utilizing Poisson brackets for dynamics and phase-space integrals for the inner product, alongside an energy-resolved (microcanonical) analysis.

**Model / system.** The study is applied to collective spin systems, specifically the Lipkin-Meshkov-Glick (LMG) and Feingold-Peres (FP) models, which are known to exhibit classical behavior in the thermodynamic limit.

**Key observables.** Krylov complexity (K-complexity), Classical Krylov complexity, Krylov-Ehrenfest time ($t^*$), Microcanonical Krylov complexities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The semiclassical parameter $\hbar$, energy shell structure, and system coupling constants defining the LMG/FP models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence of a well-defined semiclassical limit ($\hbar 	o 0$) for the quantum mechanical systems under study.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of microcanonical K-complexity for the LMG model, comparing regimes with and without saddle points in phase space.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the formal link between quantum and classical Krylov dynamics. It then introduces the energy-resolved (microcanonical) framework to analyze complexity growth in specific models like LMG and FP, resolving issues related to spectral chaos versus localized instabilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we develop and study the classical Lanczos algorithm allowing us to define Krylov complexity using the symplectic structure of phase space: Poisson brackets take on the role of the quantum commutators and phase-space integrals furnish the inner product needed to define the Lanczos recursion. We show, using general methods of quantum mechanics in phase space, that the $\hbar \to 0$ limit of the usual quantum mechanical Krylov framework smoothly goes over into the classical one. In theories with well-defined semiclassical limits, we show that classical Krylov complexity accurately approximates quantum complexity at early enough times, and thus is a useful characteristic of early-time chaotic dynamics. We define a Krylov-Ehrenfest time, which quantifies the eventual divergence of classical and quantum complexities, corresponding to a characteristic depth of the Krylov chain, $n\sim n_*(\hbar)$, which in the time domain translates to the well-known scale, $t_*\simλ_K^{-1}\log(1/\hbar)$, in generic chaotic systems. We additionally define microcanonical Krylov complexities, both in the classical and quantum setting, which allows one a fine-grained study of complexity, energy shell by energy shell. We apply this framework to the Lipkin-Meshkov-Glick (LMG) and Feingold-Peres (FP) models, which are collective spin systems known to classicalize in the thermodynamic limit. In particular, while the FP model features spectral chaos for some range of coupling values, the LMG model is known to exhibit early-time saddle-dominated scrambling. Our analysis shows that the instability in LMG is resolved by the microcanonical Krylov complexity, which is controlled by the integrable structure of the Hamiltonian in spectral windows away from the instability, both at early and late times.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how chirality affects phase separation in self-propelled active matter. Using sophisticated continuum models and multi-scale analysis, the authors distinguish between bulk and microphase separation regimes. The key finding is that chirality introduces novel dynamic features, such as interface traveling waves and droplet splitting, even when fluid flows are absent.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical active matter, the use of advanced non-equilibrium statistical mechanics tools like multiple-scale expansion and linear stability analysis mirrors techniques used to study pattern formation and phase transitions in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how chirality influences phase separation—specifically distinguishing between bulk and microphase regimes—in active particle systems driven by quorum-sensing interactions.

**Main result.** Chirality is shown to induce traveling waves at the interface and can cause elongated droplets to split even without fluid flows, a phenomenon distinct from achiral systems.

**Method.** The analysis employs both Diffusion-Drift (DD) approximation and advanced Multiple-Scales (MS) coarse-graining techniques applied to continuum models of active particles.

**Model / system.** The system involves Chiral Quorum-Sensing Active Particles (cQSAPs) modeled by particle dynamics and a continuum description called chiral Active Model B (cAMB).

**Key observables.** Phase separation types (bulk vs. microphase), coarsening law ($\ell_{typ} \propto t^{1/3}$), interfacial tensions, and the growth rate $r(q)$ from linear stability analysis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chirality parameter ($\omega$), translational diffusivity ($DT$), and density ($ho_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DD approximation is insufficient, necessitating the use of MS theory for accurate prediction of microphase separation; the MS expansion itself has limitations near instability thresholds.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing stability predictions (DD vs. MS), time evolution of coarsening ($\ell_{typ} \propto t^{1/3}$), and simulation snapshots demonstrating droplet splitting.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model (cQSAPs) to comparing theoretical approaches (DD vs. MS theory) for stability analysis, culminating in showing how chirality modifies dynamics like interface wave propagation and droplet breakup.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many active particles phase-separate due to quorum-sensing interactions, and their self-propulsion mechanisms often break chiral symmetry. Using particle and continuum models, we uncover the role of chirality in inducing bulk or microphase separation, including a chiral phase formed of vapor bubbles. Analytical predictions for the emergence of these phases require a coarse-graining technique based on multiple-scale analysis. Further, introducing a minimal active field theory, we show that, in the bulk phase separation regime, chirality does not alter the diffusive $t^{1/3}$ coarsening law nor the dynamical exponent associated with capillary waves, but induces traveling waves at the interface. We finally demonstrate that, even in the absence of fluid flows, chirality can cause the breakup of elongated droplets, resembling phenomena previously observed experimentally.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces 'subspace restart,' a novel periodic reset mechanism for quantum walks that selectively erases history while preserving spatial location. This process forces the walker's dynamics into a predictable drifted-diffusion regime, allowing researchers to engineer transport coefficients like drift and diffusivity with high control. It provides a powerful tool for studying and controlling the fundamental crossover from coherent quantum mechanics to classical diffusion.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, tunable protocol—subspace restart—to engineer macroscopic transport properties in quantum systems, offering an analogy to controlled decoherence or measurement-induced localization relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to control the transition from quantum wave-like transport to classical diffusive behavior in quantum walks.

**Main result.** Subspace restart drives the quantum walk into an engineered drifted-diffusion regime, allowing precise tuning of both drift and diffusivity coefficients.

**Method.** The analysis uses a source picture decomposition (Huygens-Fresnel mechanism) to separate the robust classical backbone from residual short-range quantum interference.

**Model / system.** The model is a discrete-time quantum walk on an infinite 1D lattice, governed by periodic subspace restarts that reset only the internal coin degrees of freedom while preserving spatial probability.

**Key observables.** Mean displacement (drift), Mean Squared Displacement ($\langle x^2(n) angle$), effective drift velocity ($v_{	ext{deff}}$), and diffusion coefficient ($D_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The restart period ($r$) acts as a tunable knob, controlling the degree of classicalization; the initial coin orientation sets the geometric bias.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations, particularly treating the incoherent sum of source intensities (diagonal approximation), which is valid in certain limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the probability distribution $P_r(x, n)$ for different restart periods ($r=1$ vs. $r=25$), showing a clean Gaussian core overlaid with short-range interference fringes, and plot the accumulation of statistical quantities like $\sigma_n^2$ over time steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces subspace restart as a mechanism to control quantum-to-classical crossover in DTQWs. It develops the source picture description, analyzes the asymptotic Gaussian behavior (incoherent sum), and derives explicit formulas for the effective drift and diffusion coefficients based on the restart period $r$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Restart of a quantum process is typically modeled as a global reinitialization that erases the system's entire history. Here we introduce subspace restart, a protocol that periodically resets only the internal degrees of freedom while preserving the spatial distribution, as a tunable knob for the quantum-to-classical crossover. Using the discrete-time quantum walk as an example, we show that this selective reset drives the walker into an engineered drifted-diffusion regime. This phenomenon can be understood by a Huygens-Fresnel mechanism, where each restart fragments the wave function into a set of independent secondary sources to screen long-range correlations and isolate a robust classical backbone, whose drift and diffusivity are set by the geometric orientation of the initial coin and the restart period. Residual quantum interference, confined to effective light cones, survives only as a short-range correction that renormalizes these coefficients and imprints periodic modulations on the cumulants. Our results establish subspace restart as a route to controlling the quantum-to-classical crossover in synthetic lattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper re-examines Bose-Einstein condensation in the Grand Canonical Ensemble, focusing on systems where condensate number fluctuations are macroscopic. By using a phase-density decomposition, the authors derive that the coherence observed is suppressed relative to the total density by a factor of $\pi/4$, providing a key theoretical signature for photon BECs.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework to reconcile phase coherence (suggested by $\langle \hat{\psi}_0 angle$) with large number fluctuations ($ho_0$), which is crucial for understanding real-world quantum optical experiments like photon BECs.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses Bose-Einstein condensation (BEC) in the Grand Canonical Ensemble (GCE) when macroscopic condensate number fluctuations are significant, challenging standard mean-field descriptions.

**Main result.** For an ideal Bose gas in GCE, the ratio of the anomalous average squared to the total density is predicted to be $|\langle \hat{\psi}_0 angle|^2 / ho_0 = \pi/4$, indicating that fluctuations contribute significantly beyond the simple mean-field prediction.

**Method.** The authors employ a phase-density decomposition ($\hat{\psi}_0 ightarrow e^{i	heta} \sqrt{ho_0}$) and analyze correlation functions by determining their dependence solely on the statistics of the condensate density operator $\hat{ho}_0$.

**Model / system.** The system is an ideal gas of bosons, specifically applied to BEC in photon microcavities. The analysis focuses on the zero-momentum mode operator $\hat{\psi}_0$ and its associated density fluctuations.

**Key observables.** Condensate density ($ho_0$), anomalous average squared ($|\langle \hat{\psi}_0 angle|^2$), and the ratio $R = |\langle \hat{\psi}_0 angle|^2 / \langle \hat{\psi}^\dagger_0 \hat{\psi}_0 angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The GCE limit, which leads to the characteristic prediction $R=\pi/4$, and the comparison between coherent (canonical) and fluctuating (grand canonical) regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a well-defined phase ($	heta$) while allowing the condensate density ($ho_0$) to fluctuate macroscopically, which is necessary because standard Bogoliubov approaches fail in this regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the phase-density decomposition, deriving how correlation functions depend only on density statistics. It then calculates specific ratios like $R$ for the ideal gas in GCE, contrasting these results with canonical and coherent state expectations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bose-Einstein condensation in the grand canonical ensemble admits a formulation in terms of a phase-density decomposition of the condensate mode operator $\hatψ_{\bf 0}$. In the presence of macroscopic condensate number fluctuations this representation presents nontrivial implications. In particular, we show that, for the ideal gas, under the assumption of a well-defined phase and a fluctuating condensate density, the full hierarchy of correlation functions is determined by the statistics of the density. Within this framework, the modulus squared of the anomalous average $\langle \hatψ_{\bf 0}\rangle$ can provide only a fraction of the whole condensate density $ρ_{\bf 0}$ and for the grand canonical statistics of the ideal Bose gas one obtains the value $|\langle {\hat ψ}_{\bf 0}\rangle|^2 =(π/4) ρ_{\bf 0}$. The remaining part is supplemented by the (macroscopic) fluctuations of $\hatψ_{\bf 0}$, which become a distinctive feature of the BEC in this setting. This provides a transparent physical picture of a condensate of photons with a well-defined phase but large number fluctuations, as observed in dye-filled microcavity photon experiments. We also propose a way to access the square modulus of the anomalous average to test theoretical predictions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Oscillatory Active Brownian Motion (OABM), a refined model for sperm movement that accounts for periodic flagellar beating. By fitting this model to experimental tracking data, the authors successfully characterize how intrinsic oscillations govern overall cell transport properties across different physiological states like hyperactivation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biophysics, the use of minimal extensions to standard diffusion models (ABM) and the rigorous statistical inference from complex time-series data mirrors techniques used in modeling non-equilibrium dynamics in disordered or interacting quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard Active Brownian Motion (ABM) fails to account for the intrinsic periodic oscillations present in sperm motility, which are crucial for understanding their transport dynamics.

**Main result.** The Oscillatory Active Brownian Motion (OABM) model successfully reproduces single-cell and population dynamics for both control and hyperactivated sperm by linking measurable flagellar kinematics to coarse-grained transport.

**Method.** The study combines analytical derivations of observables (like MSD and VACF) with fitting procedures, including Maximum Likelihood Estimation (MLE), to infer OABM parameters from experimental tracking data.

**Model / system.** The system is mammalian sperm exhibiting active motion. The model used is Oscillatory Active Brownian Motion (OABM), an extension of ABM that incorporates a periodic angular modulation ($\psi(t) = A \cos(\Omega t + \phi)$) into the swimmer's orientation dynamics.

**Key observables.** Time-averaged mean-squared displacement (TAMSD), velocity autocorrelation function (VACF), transverse excursion amplitude, straight-line velocity (VSL), and curvilinear velocity (VCL).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Swimming velocity ($V$), oscillation amplitude ($A$), beat frequency ($\Omega$), and rotational diffusion coefficient ($D_R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that in the relevant experimental regime, averages over oscillatory modulation can be treated as cycle averages; furthermore, the distribution of $D_R$ was found to be independent of hyperactivation state.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely show scatter plots and correlation matrices ($ho$) for fitted OABM parameters ($V, A, \Omega, D_R$) comparing control and hyperactivated groups, demonstrating strong correlations between velocity and amplitude.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the limitations of standard ABM. It then develops the OABM framework by deriving analytical expressions for key observables. The methodology involves fitting these derived quantities to experimental sperm trajectory data (control vs. hyperactivated). Finally, it analyzes parameter distributions to distinguish physiological states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active biological microswimmers typically combine persistent self-propulsion with cyclic motion generated by flagellar or ciliary beating. However, standard active Brownian motion (ABM) does not explicitly account for these intrinsic oscillations. This limitation is particularly relevant for sperm cells, whose transport depends not only on directional persistence and stochastic reorientation but also on periodic head motion. Here we introduce oscillatory active Brownian motion (OABM), a minimal extension of ABM in which the swimmer orientation undergoes directional diffusion while being modulated by a periodic angular drive. The model yields analytical expressions for experimentally relevant observables, including the time-averaged mean-squared displacement, velocity autocorrelation function, and transverse excursion amplitude. A central prediction is a crossover between two ballistic regimes: short-time motion governed by the swimming velocity and an intermediate regime with a reduced, oscillation-averaged effective velocity determined by the angular beat amplitude. Using trajectories of human sperm, we infer model parameters from standard motility measures. The model quantitatively reproduces both single-cell trajectories and population-level dynamics under control conditions and following induction of hyperactivation. Overall, OABM provides a compact active-matter framework that links measurable flagellar kinematics to coarse-grained transport, enabling a description of sperm motility across different physiological states.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper argues that assuming ideal reference frames when calculating probabilities leads to an oversimplification. By analyzing measurements relative to finite-resource Quantum Reference Frames, the authors demonstrate that the resulting relative frequencies are fundamentally uncertain, violating Bell inequalities in the large repetition limit. This suggests a necessary revision of the quantum state concept for regimes constrained by physical resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts foundational quantum theory, suggesting that resource constraints fundamentally alter the probabilistic structure of quantum mechanics, which is highly relevant for understanding measurement processes near gravitational boundaries.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how quantum probabilities behave when measurements are performed relative to non-ideal, finite-resource Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs), challenging the assumption that standard Born rules hold universally.

**Main result.** When reference frames are resource-limited, the relative frequencies themselves become fundamentally indefinite, violating Bell-type inequalities even in the large repetition limit.

**Method.** The authors develop mathematical tools using relative frequency operators and construct CHSH-type correlators to test for compatibility with local hidden variable models.

**Model / system.** The model involves a system measured repeatedly (i.i.d. copies) whose measurement context is defined by an auxiliary, physical QRF. This framework is proposed to be implemented using pulsed continuous-variable quantum optics and homodyne detection.

**Key observables.** Relative frequencies ($
u$), Relational Relative Frequency Operators ($F_N(P)$), and CHSH-type correlators ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The large number of repetitions limit ($N 	o \infty$) is crucial, as the breakdown of assumptions occurs in this regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard notion of a quantum state breaks down when macroscopic reference frames are unavailable; the analysis relies on taking the $N 	o \infty$ limit operationally.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes, but the structure implies showing the violation of Bell inequalities in the large-N limit for both discrete and continuous variable settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the problem with ideal reference frames to introducing relative frequency operators. It then develops constructions (POVM vs PVM) and culminates by applying these tools to show violations of Bell inequalities, particularly in the continuous variable setting using a CHSH-type correlator.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum theory challenges the view that individual measurement outcomes are predefined and independent of the measurement context. Yet the quantum state itself -- the catalogue of probabilities for all possible measurements -- is usually assumed to be well defined. We argue that this assumption tacitly relies on measurements being performed relative to ideal, infinitely-resourceful reference frames. We show that, when measurements are made relative to non-ideal quantum reference frames, the probabilities themselves become indefinite: even in the limit of arbitrarily large number of runs, the relative frequencies may remain uncertain. The uncertainty is irreducible in a quantum-mechanical sense, as we show by proving a Bell-type theorem for relative frequencies. We further propose a quantum-optical implementation of these relational measurements based on pulsed homodyne detection. Our findings motivate an extension of the notion of the quantum state to regimes constrained by finite resources. We expect them to be especially relevant at the interface between quantum theory and general relativity, where the resources and information available in a bounded region of spacetime are fundamentally limited.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the theory of Active Quantum Nematics by embedding continuum descriptions of active matter into a quantum mechanical framework using a complex wavefunction ($\psi$). It successfully models diverse biological phenomena—from bacterial swimming to heart beating—by predicting quantized behaviors and deriving force laws based on wave mechanics principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel theoretical bridge between classical active matter physics (hydrodynamics/soft matter) and fundamental quantum mechanics formalism. The use of wavefunctions to describe non-equilibrium, self-propelled systems is highly relevant for open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to formalize Active Nematics (AN) and Liquid Crystal theory within a quantum mechanical framework, creating 'Active Quantum Nematics' (AQN). This unifies concepts from active matter physics with quantum field formalism.

**Main result.** The introduction of a complex Nematic Wavefunction ($\psi$) allows the system to exhibit quantized features, predicting relationships like Planck's energy-frequency relation for micro-swimmers and characterizing biological activity via wave mechanics.

**Method.** The methodology involves extending continuum mechanics equations by introducing quantum operators and utilizing analogies to the Dirac equation structure. Key techniques include expectation value calculations and Helmholtz decomposition of velocity fields.

**Model / system.** The model describes active nematic continua, which can represent biological systems like peristaltic worms or beating hearts. The dynamics are governed by coupled equations involving a complex wavefunction $\psi$ and various potential fields ($\phi$, $\phi_S$).

**Key observables.** The Nematic Wavefunction ($\psi$), its conserved probability density ($|\psi|^2$), the total power eigenvalue ($E = \hbar_A\omega$), and derived active forces ($f_{\phi u}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quantum action scale ($\hbar_A$), activity coefficients, and parameters governing chemotaxis/biosignal diffusion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that the complex phase angle ($\phi$) dictates the evolution of $\psi$, and it often simplifies by ignoring passive elastic stresses or assuming constant drag coefficients.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate peristaltic swimmers vs. shaker creatures, and depict predator-prey chemotaxis using biosignal fields ($\phi_S$) coupled to internal activity potentials.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the standard AN equations by introducing $\psi$ (analogous to Dirac structure), deriving quantum interaction terms via potential transformations, and finally applying this framework to specific biological examples like predator-prey dynamics and cardiac pulsation analysis using harmonic expansion.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nematic symmetry entails conserved quantized quantities such as number of topological defects and vorticity cells. Correspondingly, countless quantum analogies have been found in Active Nematics. We formalize Active Nematics and Liquid Crystal theory into the framework of Quantum Mechanics by introducing a complex valued Nematic Wavefunction to the Beris Edward equations, thus splitting spatiotemporally varying nematic systems into quantized states. We obtain the Planck's energy-frequency relationship for active micro-swimmers such as peristaltic worms and bacterium as a consequence of local complex phase-symmetry of the governing equations, similar to the gauge formulation of Electromagnetism. For organisms operating on diffusive chemotaxis, we obtain predator-prey dynamics that evolve to maximize/minimize pheromones field gradient overlap. Furthermore, when quantizing beating hearts, similar to the orbitals of hydrogen atoms, the state-function allows us to characterize hearts not only through the rhythm, but also the spaciotemporal distribution of contractile activity of various harmonics among healthy and unhealthy hearts.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous quantum theory for spontaneous Raman scattering, treating it as an entangled-photon generation process. Using advanced techniques like the cumulant expansion, the authors demonstrate that the interaction leads to unique chiral couplings causing optical nonreciprocity. The findings provide a theoretical blueprint for developing quantum photonic protocols based on enhanced and controlled Raman spectroscopy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO/Quantum Optics as it applies advanced open quantum system techniques (master equations, cumulant expansion) to a fundamental spectroscopic process (Raman scattering), yielding insights into generating nonclassical light sources and understanding dissipation effects on quantum correlations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a microscopic quantum theory for spontaneous Raman scattering that connects naturally to entangled-photon generation models while correctly accounting for the resonant and dissipative nature of the process.

**Main result.** The theory establishes that Raman scattering induces chiral couplings between Stokes and anti-Stokes sidebands, leading to optical nonreciprocity. Crucially, it shows that while entanglement can be generated, true squeezing below the vacuum noise limit is not achieved in this resonant process.

**Method.** The analysis employs a cumulant-expansion approach applied to the quantum evolution map derived from the interaction Hamiltonian, resulting in a cascaded master equation (Lindblad form).

**Model / system.** The system is spontaneous Raman scattering involving Stokes and anti-Stokes sidebands driven by a pump field. The dynamics are modeled using an effective two-photon operator formalism within a quantum map framework.

**Key observables.** Logarithmic negativity (entanglement measure), squeezing factor, mean photon number ($\langle \hat{n}_{St} angle$), and the nonreciprocal amplification characteristics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phonon occupation number ($ar{n}$), detuning ($\Delta$), conversion strength ($\Gamma_{cw}$), and phase mismatch ($\Delta k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The pump field is treated classically (undepleted-pump approximation), and the phonon bath is assumed to be in a thermal state, which limits the description of full quantum dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate collinear setups for Stokes-anti-Stokes scattering; plots show mean Stokes photon number enhancement vs. detuning/coupling strength, and logarithmic negativity trends following this enhancement.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the fundamental Hamiltonian and using cumulant expansion to derive a master equation. It then analyzes different excitation regimes (cw vs. ultrafast) and incorporates propagation effects via phase matching factors before concluding with diagnostic measurements of entanglement and nonreciprocity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inelastic light scattering is a central tool for sample characterization and label-free imaging across the physical and life sciences. Recent work has suggested that scattered light can also exhibit nonclassical correlations. Here, we develop a microscopic theory of Raman scattering that connects naturally to established descriptions of entangled-photon generation in three- and four-wave mixing, while capturing essential differences arising from the resonant, dissipative character of Raman scattering. Using a cumulant-expansion approach, we analyze the entanglement structure of the scattered sidebands and identify signatures that distinguish Raman-mediated correlations from those generated in conventional off-resonant nonlinear wave mixing. In particular, we show that Raman scattering induces chiral couplings between Stokes and anti-Stokes sidebands, leading to nonreciprocal amplification of coherent seed fields. These results establish a theoretical framework for Raman-based quantum photonic protocols and suggest routes toward quantum-enhanced Raman spectroscopy and imaging.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper calculates how quantum effects modify the electrical conductivity of a black brane at very low temperatures using holographic methods. It derives a non-monotonic temperature dependence for this conductivity by comparing results from an effective 2D action against direct one-loop gravitational calculations. The findings highlight that quantum corrections significantly alter the expected low-temperature behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on gravity/condensed matter duality, the analysis of strongly coupled low-temperature transport coefficients and quantum corrections mirrors problems in strongly correlated electron systems where thermal fluctuations are suppressed.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study revisits holographic electrical conductivity at very low temperatures where quantum fluctuations near a black brane throat are strongly coupled.

**Main result.** The quantum-corrected conductivity exhibits a non-monotonic temperature dependence, decreasing to a minimum before growing as $1/\sqrt{CT}$ in the deep low-temperature limit.

**Method.** The authors employ two complementary theoretical approaches: using an effective two-dimensional action capturing Schwarzian modes, and calculating the one-loop contribution directly from the four-dimensional gravitational path integral.

**Model / system.** The model involves a near-extremal Reissner-Nordström (RN) black brane in AdS$_4$ spacetime, analyzed via holographic methods derived from the AdS/CFT correspondence.

**Key observables.** Holographic electrical conductivity ($\sigma_{DC}$), quantum corrections to transport coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and the dimensionless parameter $CT$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating quantum fluctuations via effective 2D actions or performing one-loop calculations in the gravitational path integral, assuming validity in specific temperature regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 shows a function $g_T(k)$ with a maximum increasing rapidly with $T$; Figure 8 compares the two-dimensional Wightman function numerically to analytical estimates.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews standard DC conductivity calculations using linearized fluctuations, then develops and compares results from an effective 2D Schwarzian mode action against direct 4D gravitational path integral methods for quantum corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit holographic electrical conductivity in the regime of very low temperatures where quantum fluctuations in the throat of the near-extremal, asymptotically AdS$_4$ dual black brane are strongly coupled. We treat the fluctuations via an effective two-dimensional action capturing the effects of the Schwarzian modes at the scale $1/C$. Our main result is a non-monotonic temperature dependence: the quantum-corrected conductivity decreases as the temperature is lowered, reaches a minimum at $(CT_{\mathrm{min}}\simeq 0.023)$, and grows as $(1/\sqrt{CT})$ for $(CT\ll 1)$. We further estimate the one-loop contribution to the conductivity directly from the four-dimensional gravitational path integral and find qualitative agreement with the effective two-dimensional description in the regime $(CT\gg 1)$. We find that quantum corrections substantially modify the low-temperature behavior of the holographic conductivity in both approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper calculates how quantum fluctuations modify transport properties like viscosity and conductivity near extremal black branes using the AdS/CFT correspondence. It finds universal low-temperature enhancements, predicting non-monotonic temperature dependence similar to that observed in real correlated materials. This work provides holographic signatures of quantum gravity effects on condensed matter transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of non-monotonic temperature dependence with a minimum, analogous to behavior seen in correlated materials (like strange metals), provides holographic evidence for quantum gravity effects influencing transport in strongly interacting systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study determines quantum corrections to transport properties, such as shear viscosity and electrical conductivity, at low temperatures in holographic models that incorporate momentum relaxation.

**Main result.** Quantum fluctuations lead to a universal low-temperature enhancement of transport; specifically, for certain operators, this manifests as a non-monotonic temperature dependence with a minimum at $T_{m min} \propto C^{-1}$.

**Method.** The analysis employs the AdS/CFT correspondence, calculating transport coefficients via Kubo formulas applied to retarded Green's functions in the near-extremal black brane limit.

**Model / system.** The model uses holographic gravity coupled to matter fields (e.g., an isotropic linear axion model) describing a black brane geometry with momentum relaxation mechanisms built into the bulk action.

**Key observables.** Shear viscosity ($\eta$) and DC electrical conductivity ($\sigma_{DC}$), analyzed through retarded Green's functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature limit ($T 	o 0$), extremal black brane geometry, momentum relaxation scale ($\alpha$), and the Schwarzian scaling dimension ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the holographic duality (AdS/CFT) and approximations for quantum corrections in the near-horizon region.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the necessity of quantum corrections due to thermodynamic inconsistencies, then calculating $\eta$ and $\sigma_{DC}$ using advanced techniques involving matched asymptotic expansions and analyzing the order dependence of limits in the Kubo formula application.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We determine the quantum corrections to transport arising from fluctuations of the near-AdS${}_2$ throat of near-extremal black branes in holographic models with momentum relaxation. By computing the shear viscosity and electrical conductivity at both zero and finite chemical potential, we uncover a universal low-temperature enhancement of transport generated by Schwarzian quantum fluctuations. Specifically, transport coefficients extracted from retarded Green's functions increase throughout the regime $C T \ll 1$. For operators with Schwarzian scaling dimension $Δ>1$, this enhancement is preceded by a universal minimum at a characteristic temperature $T_{\rm min} \propto C^{-1}$, leading to a non-monotonic temperature dependence strikingly similar to that observed in many correlated materials. In contrast, for the case $Δ=1$, relevant for the electrical conductivity, the transport coefficient evolves monotonically toward a constant value. Our results identify universal signatures of near-horizon quantum gravity in the transport properties of holographic quantum matter.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes that supernova neutrinos can act as a source for the initial seeds of life's chirality. By coupling parity-violating neutrino interactions into autocatalytic chemical reactions within interstellar clouds, the authors show that tiny directional biases are amplified by non-equilibrium dynamics to produce significant homochirality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work models non-equilibrium chemical dynamics driven by an external, weak quantum field effect (neutrinos) acting on chiral systems, which is a prime example of open quantum system dynamics in a far-from-equilibrium setting.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether parity-violating interactions from supernova neutrinos can provide an astrophysical mechanism to generate the necessary homochirality in interstellar molecular clouds.

**Main result.** The combination of neutrino-induced directional bias, autocatalysis, and stochastic fluctuations allows for an amplification mechanism that predicts an enantiomeric excess greater than 10%, consistent with meteorite analyses.

**Method.** The authors solve stochastic differential equations (in the Itô sense) derived from a chemical reaction scheme incorporating parity-violating energy differences ($\Delta E_{PV}$).

**Model / system.** The model describes autocatalytic chemical reactions in an Interstellar Molecular Cloud near a supernova, where neutrino interactions introduce a directional bias term into the system dynamics.

**Key observables.** Enantiomeric excess (ee) of chiral molecules ($\omega$), and the probability distribution of this excess over time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Neutrino flux ($10^{33}-10^{35} 	ext{ cm}^{-2}	ext{s}^{-1}$), Supernova Luminosity ($\sim 10^{51} - 10^{53} 	ext{ erg/s}$), and the Parity Violation Energy Difference ($\Delta E_{PV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The neutrino interaction term is treated as a deterministic component of the chemical scheme, and approximations are made for calculating $\Delta E_{PV}$ using DFT methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the chemical reaction schemes (including autocatalytic, decay, and neutrino-induced steps) and show time evolution plots detailing how the mean enantiomeric excess ($\langle \omega angle$) changes with temperature or time.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the physical context (neutrino interaction Hamiltonian), developing the stochastic chemical model using Master/Fokker-Planck equations, and finally simulating the thermal evolution to demonstrate how symmetry breaking leads to high enantiomeric excess.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the role of parity-violating interactions between supernova neutrinos and chiral molecules in nearby interstellar molecular clouds as a potential source of biomolecular homochirality. We introduce neutrino interactions into the autocatalytic chemical reactions in a far-from-equilibrium noise-induced system. These interactions create a directional bias between L and D enantiomers in the racemization reactions, which is amplified by autocatalysis and stochastic fluctuations. We solve the stochastic equations within the Ito sense to obtain the dynamics of the probability distribution of the enantiomeric excesses, offering an astrophysical scenario for the delivery of homochirality seeds to Earth by meteorites. In spite of the weak interactions of supernova neutrinos, our framework introduces an amplification mechanism to yield a considerable enantiomeric excess of more than $10\%$, in agreement with the latest chemical analysis of the meteorites. Moreover, we scan over the parameter space of the model, inferring from the observational values in order to explore the window of opportunity to generate the initial seeds of homochiral states in interstellar molecular clouds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a coupled model linking agent swarming (spatial motion) to opinion dynamics. It shows that parameters like the confidence threshold control how many distinct opinion groups form, and these opinions, in turn, govern whether those groups physically merge or stay separated. The framework is useful for understanding collective decision-making.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not quantum in nature, the study of emergent collective behavior, pattern formation, and phase transitions in interacting many-body systems shares deep mathematical parallels with concepts studied in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and complex quantum dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates how coherent group-level patterns emerge from simple interactions when agents possess both spatial motion (swarming) and internal states like opinions.

**Main result.** The confidence threshold controls the number of opinion clusters, while the strength of opinion-dependent attraction dictates whether these clusters merge or remain spatially separated. An analytical expression for the consensus swarm radius is also derived.

**Method.** The authors propose and analyze a coupled model involving differential equations governing both spatial dynamics (attraction-repulsion) and opinion evolution (Deffuant-type model).

**Model / system.** It models N agents in 2D space with opinions O_i, where spatial movement depends on the difference between opinions. The opinion update follows a bounded-confidence rule.

**Key observables.** Number of opinion clusters (N_g), Overlap (I) and Distance (d) metrics for cluster separation, Average Swarm Velocity ($\langle V(t) angle$), and the radius ($R$) of the stationary consensus swarm distribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Confidence threshold ($d$), Opinion-dependent attraction strength parameters ($J, \lambda$), Attraction/Repulsion strengths ($A, B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on numerical simulations and, for analytical results, assumes the continuum limit (N -> infinity) or specific functional forms for kernels.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how varying $d$ controls cluster number and separation; changing $\lambda$ affects spatial grouping; and plots compare simulated vs. analytically derived consensus radii ($R_c$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the coupled framework, details the model equations (spatial and opinion dynamics), presents numerical results showing parameter effects, derives an analytical expression for the consensus radius, and concludes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Collective dynamics in multi-agent systems provide a powerful framework for understanding how coherent group-level patterns can emerge from simple interactions between individuals. Such phenomena are observed in many natural and artificial systems, including animal groups, robotic swarms, and distributed decision-making processes. In many situations, agents are not only characterized by their spatial motion, but also by internal states, e.g., opinions or preferences, which evolve through interactions with peers. Understanding how these internal states influence collective motion, and how spatial organization in turn affects internal dynamics, remains an important challenge. In this work, we propose a model of coupled collective motion and opinion dynamics. The spatial dynamics are governed by attraction--repulsion interactions, while the internal dynamics are described by a Deffuant-type opinion model. Our results show that the confidence threshold of the opinion dynamics plays a key role in controlling the number of opinion clusters, whereas the strength of the opinion-dependent spatial attraction determines whether these clusters spatially merge or remain separated. In addition, for the full-consensus state, we derive the expression for the radius of the stationary swarm distribution when a nonlinear attraction kernel is used, using a semi-analytical approach. The proposed framework may be useful for studying collective decision-making, animal group behavior, and coordination strategies in swarm robotics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper validates the use of a quantum polariton Hamiltonian by showing its equivalence to the standard Floquet theory approach when calculating High-Harmonic Generation spectra. By comparing results from both QED and Floquet formalisms on an atomic system, the authors confirm that the two methods yield identical predictions under appropriate approximations for strong laser fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it rigorously connects semi-classical descriptions (Floquet) with fully quantized treatments (QED) for strong-field light-matter interactions, which is central to advanced quantum optics and AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is defining a quantum Hamiltonian that accurately reproduces the High-Harmonic Generation (HHG) spectra predicted by Floquet theory when an atom interacts with intense laser fields.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that the mean-acceleration HGS derived from the semi-classical Floquet approach is equivalent to the spectrum obtained using a full Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) polariton Hamiltonian under specific physical conditions.

**Method.** The authors employ both Floquet theory and QED perturbation theory, comparing the resulting spectra by analyzing the difference between the two formalisms' Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an atom (illustrated with a Xenon potential) driven by intense Infrared (IR) pump laser fields while emitting Ultraviolet (UV) high harmonics. The comparison involves constructing and diagonalizing both the Floquet Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_{	ext{Floquet}}$) and the QED polariton Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_{	ext{QED}}$).

**Key observables.** High-Harmonic Generation Spectrum (HGS), specifically $|A(	ext{NHG})|^2$, and the comparison of energy expectation values $\langle \hat{H} angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regime where the number of IR pump photons is much larger than the number of emitted UV photons ($\omega_{	ext{UV}} = N_{	ext{HG}}\omega_{	ext{IR}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** A critical assumption is that the intensity of the emitted high harmonics is many orders of magnitude smaller than the intense IR laser field, allowing for approximations in coupling terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare eigenvectors and HGS plots derived from both Floquet and QED matrices across varying numbers of absorbed IR photons, showing strong numerical agreement.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by defining two theoretical frameworks (Floquet vs. QED). It then proceeds to calculate observables like the HGS using methods specific to each framework, culminating in a direct comparison and validation that supports the use of the quantum polariton Hamiltonian.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This manuscript investigates the possibility of defining a quantum Hamiltonian that leads to the same high-harmonic generation (HHG) spectra as those predicted by Floquet theory in the regime where the number of infrared (IR) pump photons is much larger than the number of emitted UV photons. The key assumption underlying our derivation is that the intensity of the emitted high harmonics is many orders of magnitude smaller than that of the IR laser, and that the emission of the Nnt harmonic results from the absorption of N IR photons.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an exact Gaussian-Remainder Hierarchy to accurately approximate the probability density of sums of random variables with heavy tails. By separating the sum into a Gaussian bulk and residual jump contributions, the authors derive a simple first-order formula that captures the entire behavior—center, crossover, and far tail—in one expression. This provides powerful tools for analyzing rare events in complex statistical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical machinery developed for approximating complex multi-variable probability distributions is highly relevant for analyzing rare events or non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum systems (e.g., tunneling rates, escape probabilities).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop an accurate method for approximating the probability density function of a sum of many independent, identically distributed random variables, especially in regimes exhibiting complex crossover behavior between Gaussian and rare-event tails.

**Main result.** The authors introduce an exact Gaussian-remainder hierarchy whose first-order approximation successfully captures the central Gaussian bulk, the crossover region, and the far-tail big-jump statistics simultaneously for various distributions.

**Method.** They utilize a specialized convolution decomposition (Gaussian-Remainder Hierarchy) to separate the sum density into a known Gaussian background contribution and residual terms that capture non-Gaussian features like rare jumps.

**Model / system.** The model consists of sums of $N$ independent, identically distributed random variables ($X = \sum x_i$) whose single-summand densities can be subexponential (e.g., stretched-exponential) or follow power laws.

**Key observables.** Probability density function $P_N(X)$, anomalous large-deviation rate function $I(\xi)$, and the ratio of approximate to exact probabilities ($R_{	ext{app}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of summands ($N$), the tail behavior of the summand (subexponential vs. power-law), and the order of approximation used in the hierarchy.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes finite variance for the single-summand density to define the Gaussian background, though extensions are discussed for cases where moments diverge.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare various approximations ($P^{(1)}_N$, $P^{(2)}_N$) against the exact result across logarithmic scales, demonstrating convergence in both bulk and tail regions; rate function plots show finite-$N$ rates converging to the asymptotic anomalous rate function.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Gaussian-Remainder Hierarchy for $P_N(X)$, derives the first-order approximation ($P^{(1)}_N$), demonstrates its accuracy across various distributions (stretched-exponential, power-law), and finally applies this framework to derive and verify the anomalous large-deviation rate function.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop an exact Gaussian-remainder hierarchy for the probability density of the sum of $N$ independent, identically distributed random variables with broad, finite-variance distribution for the summands. The hierarchy separates the Gaussian fixed-point contribution from residual sectors that retain the original single-summand density. For subexponential densities, the first nontrivial truncation yields a simple finite-$N$ approximation that involves one convolution with a Gaussian background and a subtraction that removes Gaussian overcounting. This approximation captures the Gaussian center, the crossover region, and the big-jump tail within a single expression, as demonstrated numerically for stretched-exponential and finite-variance power-law examples. The same first-order approximation reproduces the known asymptotic anomalous rate function for sums of stretched-exponential random variables and also provides an accurate approximation to the corresponding finite-$N$ rate function.

</details>
