**Summary.** This work details a side-channel attack where an untrusted error correction decoder can deduce the underlying quantum algorithm by analyzing patterns (gate fingerprints) hidden within the syndrome measurement data. The authors demonstrate that these leakage channels exist even in fault-tolerant settings, necessitating that decoder systems be secured or treated as trusted components.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, information-theoretic security vulnerability in the control/readout layer of quantum computation, which is highly relevant for anyone designing or simulating large-scale quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper identifies a novel side-channel attack vector against fault-tolerant quantum computers where an untrusted decoder can infer the executed logical computation by analyzing syndrome data.

**Main result.** Distinct logical operations leave detectable statistical patterns, called 'gate fingerprints,' in the spacetime syndrome data processed by the decoder.

**Method.** The analysis formalizes this leakage by defining gate fingerprints as conditional distributions of the spacetime syndrome given a hidden logical gate, using surface code models.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computing (FTQC) utilizing surface codes and Clifford+T gates. The attack targets the decoder which processes syndrome measurements derived from stabilizer checks.

**Key observables.** Spacetime syndrome data, gate fingerprints (statistical patterns in syndrome outcomes), and reconstructed logical gate sequences/ratios over time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The type of computation (e.g., Amplitude Amplification, HHL) and the level of decoder knowledge (qubit-aware vs. qubit-unaware).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The decoder is assumed to be correct in its decoding function but untrusted regarding confidentiality; leakage occurs through syndrome data.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the threat model flow, show rolling average gate counts for specific algorithms (AA, HHL, QFT), and demonstrate circuit reconstruction via majority voting on repeating patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by formalizing the syndrome leakage setting, defining gate fingerprints using surface code analysis of Clifford+T gates, showing how adversaries combine these fingerprints to infer circuits, and concluding with security recommendations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum computing emerges as an applied technology, there is a growing need to protect quantum computers against information security attacks. This work identifies a new class of side-channel attacks against fault-tolerant quantum computers, in which the syndrome data that is sent to the decoder system is used to infer which computation (logical circuit) is taking place on the quantum computer. Our work introduces the concept of gate fingerprints, which describes those patterns present in syndrome data that indicate which logical operation took place on the quantum computer. We show different effects by which logical operations produce gate fingerprints by focusing on Clifford+T computation in the surface code. We then explore how gate fingerprint information can be used to make inferences about the circuits or algorithms run on a quantum computer. Our findings indicate that decoder systems can be a vector for side-channel attacks and thus to prevent this, decoder systems should either be secured or built by a trusted party.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies Quantum Machine Learning (QML) techniques, specifically QSVM and VQC, to detect phishing attacks on Ethereum transaction networks. The authors introduce a novel cascaded QRAC encoding scheme that improves performance over standard methods. They propose a hybrid quantum-classical ensemble framework, demonstrating its effectiveness while also analyzing the resilience of these models when run on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on finance, the methodology involves applying quantum feature mapping and kernel estimation to complex, structured data, which shares conceptual links with how entanglement or correlation structures are analyzed in many-body physics simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is detecting anomalies, specifically phishing activity, within real-world Ethereum financial transaction networks.

**Main result.** A hybrid quantum-classical ensemble framework combining QSVM and GCN showed strong performance, with QRAC encoding consistently improving results over standard feature maps on both simulators and hardware.

**Method.** The authors employ Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models like QSVM and VQC, enhanced by novel cascaded Quantum Random Access Coding (QRAC) schemes for data encoding.

**Model / system.** The work is tested on quantum computing platforms, including IBM statevector simulators and the physical IBM Heron quantum processor. The core models are QSVM and VQC applied to Ethereum transaction graph data.

**Key observables.** False negatives count, Recall rates, Macro average of Precision/Recall/F1 score, and performance degradation comparing simulator vs. hardware results.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dataset is highly imbalanced (2500:1 ratio); encoding schemes include ZZ feature map versus cascaded QRACs; quantum volume is a key hardware metric.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Performance improvements are demonstrated despite current NISQ hardware limitations, and the authors assume that hybrid classical-quantum approaches can bridge the gap to deep learning techniques.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of QRAC states on the Bloch sphere (e.g., corners of a square/cube) and detail the feature encoding scheme using orthogonal planes ($	ext{xy, yz, xz}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the anomaly detection problem to proposing QML models (QSVM/VQC), detailing advanced data encoding techniques (QRAC vs. ZZ map), benchmarking these methods on both simulators and hardware, and finally presenting an ensemble framework for robust results.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This article explores the potential of Quantum Machine Learning (QML), specifically assessing a Quantum Support Vector Machine (QSVM) and a Variational Quantum Classifier (VQC) for detecting anomalies in real-world financial transaction data. While these QML methods outperform statistical methods, they fall short of cutting-edge deep learning techniques. To bridge this gap, we propose a hybrid quantum-classical ensemble framework that leverages the strengths of both domains. We demonstrate its effectiveness in detecting phishing in Ethereum transaction networks by combining complementary algorithms. The QSVM, whether used individually or in an ensemble, consistently delivered the lowest false negatives and higher recall rates, that are crucial for anomaly detection. To enhance individual models, we encoded the data using novel cascaded Quantum Random Access Coding (QRAC) schemes and compared it with the popular encoding ZZ feature map on both simulators and the IBM Heron quantum processor. For both QSVM and VQC, we consistently observed improvements (13% for QRAC-VQC and 3% for QRAC-QSVM) of QRAC over the ZZ feature map. Notably, certain QML algorithms exhibit remarkable resilience on the IBM Heron quantum processor, approaching simulator-level performance on devices with high quantum volume. This observation underscores the promise of QML despite hardware limitations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously examines the impact of logarithmic corrections on the critical behavior of a 3D quantum Heisenberg antiferromagnet at its surface. By combining large-scale Quantum Monte Carlo simulations with Boundary Renormalization Group theory, the authors characterize three distinct boundary regimes (ordinary, special, extraordinary). The work provides new theoretical predictions and quantitative numerical evidence for how these logarithmic terms govern finite-size scaling near the quantum critical point.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed study of boundary critical phenomena, especially incorporating higher-order logarithmic corrections beyond mean-field theory, provides crucial insights into how dimensionality reduction or boundaries modify fundamental quantum phase transitions relevant to strongly correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how logarithmic corrections modify critical behavior at both the bulk and surface levels of a quantum antiferromagnet near its upper critical dimension.

**Main result.** The authors derive new, quantitatively supported logarithmic correction exponents for boundary correlations in ordinary and special transition regimes, while also confirming long-range surface order in the extraordinary regime.

**Method.** The research combines large-scale Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations with rigorous Boundary Renormalization Group (RG) analysis to analyze finite-size scaling.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1/2 columnar-dimerized quantum Heisenberg antiferromagnet realizing a (3+1)D O(3) quantum critical point. The Hamiltonian includes bulk and surface coupling terms ($J_1, J_2, J_s$).

**Key observables.** Bulk staggered moment ($\langle M_z^q angle$), surface staggered moment ($\langle M_z^{q,s} angle$), susceptibility ($\chi(q)$), correlation length ($\xi$), and the surface Binder ratio ($R_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis distinguishes between three critical regimes based on the surface coupling $J_s$: ordinary, special, and extraordinary transitions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending known mean-field results to include multiplicative logarithmic corrections in finite-size scaling forms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the lattice structure (Fig 1), show bulk observables confirming expected logarithmic scaling at the QCP (Fig 2), and plot the surface Binder ratio ($R_2$) vs. $J_s$ to map out the different critical regimes (Fig 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework for logarithmic corrections in the bulk, then uses QMC simulations to verify these effects. It proceeds by analyzing the surface criticality across three distinct regimes—ordinary, special, and extraordinary—deriving specific scaling forms and exponents for each.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

At the bulk upper critical dimension, marginally irrelevant interactions generate multiplicative logarithmic corrections to mean-field scaling. While these corrections are well understood for bulk observables, their consequences for boundary criticality, particularly for finite-size scaling, remain much less explored. Here we combine large-scale quantum Monte Carlo simulations with boundary renormalization-group analysis to study a (3 + 1)D O(3) quantum critical point. After verifying the known logarithmically modified bulk finite-size scaling, including the correlation-length scaling governed by the logarithmic finite-size exponent \hat{\coppa}, we tune the surface coupling to identify ordinary, special, and extraordinary boundary regimes. For the ordinary and special transitions, we derive logarithmic correction exponents and \hat{\coppa}-dependent finite-size scaling forms for boundary correlations, including results that have not been systematically established before. These predictions are quantitatively supported by Monte Carlo data. In the extraordinary regime, we find long-range surface magnetic order and a logarithmically enhanced surface-bulk correlation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper creates a reproducible benchmark by applying various quantum solvers to the 1-D heat equation. It systematically compares performance across different algorithmic paradigms under controlled conditions. The key finding is that measuring only compact observables drastically reduces resource requirements compared to reconstructing the entire field, guiding future research in practical quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, application-driven framework for benchmarking quantum algorithms applied to continuous physical systems, which is crucial for advancing quantum simulation beyond toy models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a reproducible benchmark for quantum PDE solvers due to inconsistencies across existing literature regarding discretization, readout models, and hardware assumptions.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that compact observable measurements (like total energy) require significantly fewer shots than full-field reconstruction, providing a practical guide for selecting appropriate quantum algorithms.

**Method.** A comprehensive benchmark is constructed by applying multiple quantum kernels—including HHL, QSVT, QSM, and various time-evolution methods—to the 1-D Dirichlet heat equation under controlled parameters.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the 1-D Dirichlet heat equation ($\partial u/\partial t = \alpha \partial^2 u/\partial x^2$), discretized on a grid of size $N=2^n$ with fixed boundary conditions.

**Key observables.** Relative $\ell_2$ error, reconstruction normalization artifacts (norm-mismatch), and the shot count required for different types of observables (full field vs. compact).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Grid sizes $N \in \{16, 32, 64, 128\}$ ($n=4$ to $7$), CFL-like ratio $r \approx 0.4$, and final time $T=1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between algorithmic error (statevector backend) and sampling/reconstruction errors; it assumes the heat equation structure is representative for benchmarking.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the text refers to tables comparing resource terms and performance across different kernels and observables.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares five paradigms of quantum solvers (linear inversion, variational dynamics, Hamiltonian simulation, spectral methods) against a common benchmark problem instance (the heat equation), culminating in a guide for practical solver selection based on the required output observable.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum PDE solvers are difficult to evaluate in practice because published studies use different discretizations, output models, reconstruction rules, and hardware assumptions. We present a reproducible, application-driven benchmark for the 1-D Dirichlet heat equation that compares eleven kernels under the same problem instances and readout contract. The benchmark covers coherent linear solvers (HHL, QSVT, and QLS-Fourier), VQLS, imaginary-time methods (QITE, var-QITE, and AVQDS), real-time Hamiltonian simulation and unitary dilations (Hamiltonian simulation, Schade-Hamiltonian, and Schr"odingerisation), and the spectral quantum simulation method (QSM). We use three initial conditions, four grid sizes from $n=4$ to $7$ qubits ($N=16$ to $128$), a CFL-like ratio $r\approx0.4$, and final time $T=1$. Statevector, ideal-shot ($10^5$ shots per step), and noisy Aer backends separate algorithmic, sampling, and device-noise errors. On statevector, QSM and Schade-Hamiltonian reproduce the semi-discrete reference to floating-point precision, Schr"odingerisation reaches approximately $10^{-4}$ error, and QITE is the strongest non-transform method for smooth data. Under the fixed-shot setting, HHL degrades to approximately $0.79$ relative $\ell_2$ error, while several low-depth or postselected methods become readout-limited. A norm-mismatch ablation attributes 23--29% of the $n=7$ smooth-initial-condition error of Hamiltonian simulation, AVQDS, and QLS-Fourier to reconstruction normalization. Compact observables, including total thermal energy and individual Fourier-mode weights, require 1--3 orders of magnitude fewer shots than full-field reconstruction. The resulting public benchmark provides a practical guide for selecting quantum PDE solvers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores using quantum annealing to solve track reconstruction in gaseous detectors by mapping the pattern recognition challenge into two distinct QUBO formulations. The results show that while classical methods like Kalman filters yield slightly better numerical resolution, the quantum approach is a viable proof-of-concept for local decision-making. This work establishes a framework for hybrid quantum-classical computing in complex detector environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on particle physics instrumentation, the core methodology—mapping a complex combinatorial optimization problem onto a QUBO solvable by quantum annealing—is directly relevant to developing algorithms for solving hard problems in quantum chemistry or materials science simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses track reconstruction in gaseous detectors, which is formulated as a complex combinatorial optimization problem due to overlapping hits.

**Main result.** Quantum annealing approaches using QUBO formulations successfully reproduce local reconstruction decisions comparable to established Kalman filter methods for low pileup environments.

**Method.** The core method involves formulating the hit selection and track association problems into Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problems, which are then solved on a Quantum Processing Unit (QPU).

**Model / system.** The physical system is simulated using DAMSA detector events from a beam dump setup. The optimization uses QUBO formulations to select single hits or associate cluster triplets across multiple detector layers.

**Key observables.** Position and angular resolutions ($\sigma_{	ext{full}}$, 68% width), Triplet recovery fraction ($\epsilon_{	ext{triplet}}$), and end-to-end latency ($T_{	ext{total}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Penalty strengths (e.g., $A_{	ext{seg}} = 35.0$), Straightness reward strength ($B_{	ext{line}} = 10.0$), and the low pileup regime of the simulated events.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The current demonstration is a proof-of-principle limited to low pileup samples; future work must address high occupancy and complex noise scenarios.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare position/angular residual distributions (Kalman vs. QPU), show the resource usage of QUBO embedding, and deconstruct the total operational latency for the quantum processing unit.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, formulates two distinct QUBO subproblems (single hit selection and cluster association), tests these formulations on simulated data, compares results statistically against classical methods, and analyzes computational overheads.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This study investigates the use of quantum annealing for particle track reconstruction in strip-type gaseous detectors. In such detectors, ghost hits and multiple hit combinations can turn pattern recognition into a combinatorial optimization problem. We formulate two reconstruction subproblems as quadratic unconstrained binary optimization problems. The first subproblem selects detector hits associated with a single photon track inside a localized candidate region. The second subproblem selects cluster triplets from different detector layers so that multiple track candidates can be handled within a single quantum processing unit(QPU) submission. The proposed formulations are tested using simulated DAMSA detector events. For the single track hit selection task, the QPU based reconstruction gives position and angular resolutions close to those obtained with a Kalman based reconstruction. In the simultaneous association task, valid cluster triplets are first extracted from the QPU samples and then connected using an association rule based on graph connectivity to construct track candidates. The DAMSA event topology studied here has low pileup and is dominated by the two photon signal from axion-like particle(ALP) decay. In this setting, the results show that the QUBO formulations can reproduce local reconstruction decisions. This provides a practical basis for further studies of reconstruction methods that combine quantum and classical computing in more complex tracking environments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously compares four different parameterized quantum circuits (ansatze)—including a native 2D pattern—to assess their suitability for variational quantum algorithms. By measuring expressibility via KL divergence and frame potential, and trainability via gradient variance, the authors show that the 2D ansatz offers superior performance at shallow depths by respecting physical hardware connectivity constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the crucial engineering challenge of mapping theoretical quantum algorithms onto realistic, geometrically constrained hardware architectures like planar superconducting chips.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study compares the expressibility and trainability of a native two-dimensional quantum circuit ansatz against various one-dimensional ansatze for Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs). The goal is to determine which connectivity pattern best suits modern, planar quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The 2D pairwise ansatz consistently demonstrated superior performance compared to 1D alternatives at shallow layer depths, showing lower KL divergence and smaller gradient variance. However, all ansatze converged to similar performance metrics as the circuit depth increased.

**Method.** Expressibility is quantified using Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence and the second-order frame potential ($F^{(2)}$). Trainability is assessed by measuring the gradient variance of an expectation value with respect to a circuit parameter.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses a fixed 16-qubit system modeled on superconducting qubits, specifically comparing nearest-neighbor interactions on a planar $4 	imes 4$ grid (2D) against linear or circular chains (1D).

**Key observables.** Kullback–Leibler divergence ($D_{KL}$), second-order frame potential ($\hat{F}^{(2)}$), and gradient variance of the Pauli-Z string expectation value.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of ansatz layers ($L$), system size ($N=16$ qubits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison is performed by matching the number of repeated ansatz layers ($L$) rather than matching physical resource counts like gate depth or total gates. The gradient variance measures signal strength, not overall optimization success.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare KL divergence and frame potential across different ansatze vs. layer depth (L), while other figures plot the gradient variance of a specific Pauli-Z string expectation value against L for all four ansatz types.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces PQCs and the need to match hardware connectivity, defines the 2D and 1D ansatzes, and then systematically compares them using three metrics (KL divergence, frame potential, gradient variance) across increasing circuit depths ($L$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Parameterized quantum circuits~(PQCs) constitute a central building block of variational quantum algorithms~(VQAs) and quantum machine learning~(QML) methods. Existing ansatz designs often adopt hardware-agnostic or simplified 1D chain/ring entanglement patterns. However, as quantum hardware continues to develop, native 2D connectivity patterns, such as planar superconducting-qubit architectures, are becoming increasingly important. Inspired by this hardware structure, we construct a native 2D pairwise ansatz and compare its expressibility and trainability with representative 1D ansatze at identical layer depths, despite their different circuit depths. For the fixed 16-qubit system, the 2D ansatz has the smallest KL divergence at $L=1$ and $2$, and its second-order frame potential approaches the theoretical lower bound more rapidly at shallow layer counts than the frame potentials of the three 1D ansatze. We also evaluate the gradient variance of the Pauli-$Z$-string expectation value $\langle Z_0\otimes\cdots\otimes Z_{15}\rangle$ with respect to the first $R_y$ angle. For this Pauli-$Z$ string and fixed parameter, the gradient variance is smaller for the 2D circuit at $L=1$--$4$. The differences narrow at $L=5$, and the four ansatze yield statistically compatible variances at $L=6$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel framework using categorical tensor-graph semantics to analyze quantum algorithms structurally. It reinterprets key protocols like Bernstein-Vazirani and generalizes them to higher dimensions (qutrits) within this topological language. By linking computation to Frobenius structures, it provides a powerful diagrammatic toolkit for circuit optimization and understanding entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the development of a diagrammatic toolkit based on category theory offers a powerful, potentially automated way to optimize quantum circuits by revealing underlying mathematical symmetries beyond standard gate decomposition.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a structural, intuitive understanding of quantum algorithms by reinterpreting them using categorical tensor-graph semantics within the category FHilb.

**Main result.** The paper establishes that fundamental quantum operations like entanglement generation and algorithm execution can be mapped to algebraic structures (Frobenius structures) whose properties reveal deep mathematical connections between computation and topology.

**Method.** It employs advanced tools from category theory, specifically utilizing tensor-graph semantics and Frobenius structures, to topologically reinterpret established quantum algorithms and physical processes.

**Model / system.** The focus is on abstract quantum computation within finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces (FHilb), analyzing protocols involving qubits and qutrits. The formalism uses monoidal dagger categories to define state preparation and gate operations diagrammatically.

**Key observables.** Topological skeletons of algorithms, entanglement properties (non-copyability), and the structure constants derived from complementary Frobenius structures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the Hilbert space ($n$), the base field ($\mathbb{C}_2$ for some analyses), and the constant $s$ characterizing complementary bases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that complex quantum operations can be accurately modeled and understood through the lens of categorical algebraic structures like Frobenius algebras.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate tensor diagrams for algorithms (BV, Simon) and state preparation protocols (Bell, GHZ, W-state), alongside formal definitions for various categorical structures (monoid, comonoid, Frobenius structure).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the necessary algebraic framework (Frobenius structures in FHilb) to applying this framework to reinterpret specific quantum algorithms (BV, Simon, Deutsch-Jozsa generalization), and finally analyzing physical phenomena like entanglement using these structural tools.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper systematically investigates quantum computing protocols from the intuitive perspective of categorical tensor-graph semantics within the category FHilb. While conventional Hilbert-space formalisms conceal the structural nature of quantum algorithms behind high-dimensional matrix operations, the topological framework developed herein directly encodes algorithmic functionalities to their graphical skeletons. We provide a comprehensive topological reinterpretation of the Bernstein-Vazirani and the Simon algorithms, demonstrating how topological transformations distill their core mathematical essence and clarify the operational mechanism of oracles. Going beyond standard qubit models, we formalize the qutrit-adapted generalized Deutsch-Jozsa algorithm as well as the generalized single-shot Grover algorithm, explicitly laying out their graphical representations. We further implement CNOT gates via complementary Frobenius structures, trace the diagrammatic genesis of quantum entanglement including Bell and GHZ states, and present a diagrammatic simplification for W-state preparation protocol. By bridging tensor category theory with practical quantum algorithmic design, this work furnishes composable, scalable diagrammatic toolkit essential for automated circuit optimization across the evolving quantum hardware ecosystem.

</details>

### [Maximal Classicalization of Finite-Group Quantum Reference-Frame Noise](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12968v1)

**Authors:** Maxim V. Churilov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12968v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<img src="2607.12968_figures/2607.12968_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Uniform-perturbation deficiencies on S3. The representation-complete carrier attains the classical value; the faithful standard carrier has exact slope 9/10. For the two re- ducible incomplete supports, Eq. (37) gives the shown explicit upper bounds; equality is not asserted for those two curves.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper characterizes when noisy quantum measurements derived from finite group actions can be perfectly modeled by classical probability convolutions. It proves that this perfect 'classicalization' occurs if and only if the underlying unitary representation of the group is 'representation complete.' The work provides quantitative measures for how far a system deviates from ideal classical behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools to quantify the limits of quantum information processing when noise is structured by group symmetries, which has implications for quantum metrology and quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the precise conditions under which optimal quantum post-processing (channel discrimination) for a noisy finite quantum reference frame reduces exactly to classical convolution over group elements.

**Main result.** Representation completeness of the unitary representation $U$ of the group $G$ is proven to be the exact necessary and sufficient condition for this universal reduction from quantum optimization to classical convolution.

**Method.** The authors use algebraic techniques involving irreducible representations, isotypic decomposition, and comparing quantum channel distances (diamond norm) against classical probability metrics ($\ell_1$ norm).

**Model / system.** The model involves a noisy finite quantum reference frame characterized by a random-unitary channel $\Phi^U$, derived from the action of a unitary representation $U$ of a finite group $G$. The analysis extends to infinite compact groups using Peter-Weyl theory.

**Key observables.** Directional post-processing error ($\delta_U$), visible Plancherel dimension ($S(U)$), and the deficiency gap quantifying the failure of classicalization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the group $G$, the specific unitary representation $U$, and the dimensions related to irreducible representations ($d_\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** For finite groups, the analysis relies on the equivalence between various mathematical conditions (e.g., orthonormal orbits, representation completeness). For infinite groups, stability is restricted to a 'visible Peter-Weyl band' $\Lambda_0$.

**Figures summary.** No specific figure summaries were extracted; the notes detail theoretical theorems and derived formulas for dimensions and distances.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes equivalences between representation completeness and classicalization (Theorem III.1), quantifies resource costs like minimum ancilla dimensions, derives explicit deficiency gaps for incomplete carriers, and finally extends these concepts to infinite compact groups using spectral theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A finite quantum reference token with group-valued misalignment induces a random-unitary channel, but optimal degradation is generally an optimization over all quantum post-processings. For a unitary representation U of a finite group G, we prove that the following conditions are equivalent: U contains every irreducible type; one ancilla-assisted input has an orthonormal G-orbit; signed group measures embed isometrically into channels in diamond norm; and, for every pair of noise laws p,q, $\inf_{Λ\in\mathrm{CPTP}} \frac{1}{2}\|Φ_q^U-Λ\circΦ_p^U\|_\diamond =\min_{r\in\mathcal{P}(G)}\frac{1}{2}\|q-r*p\|_1$. Thus representation completeness is the exact carrier condition for universal reduction of quantum post-processing to classical convolution. We determine the minimum ancilla dimensions for an orthogonal orbit and for an invariant calibration seed. For an incomplete carrier, with visible Plancherel dimension S(U), we derive the exact conditional-expectation distance $\frac{1}{2}\|\operatorname{id}-Φ_u^U\|_\diamond=1-1/S(U)$ and an explicit quantum--classical deficiency gap. For irreducible carriers the deficiency is obtained in closed form; the faithful two-dimensional representation of $S_3$ yields an exact ten-percent reduction relative to classical convolution. We also characterize law identifiability through the conjugation representation, provide finite linear programs and decision witnesses, and establish both a finite-dimensional obstruction and stable visible-band reconstruction for infinite compact groups. Deterministic ancillary code reproduces the finite-group examples and numerical regression checks.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an advanced geometric framework using higher-order dual number algebras to represent the state space of quantum systems. It shows that complex, non-linear quantum dynamics can be mapped onto simple, flat, linear flows in this specialized algebraic setting. This offers a powerful, unified mathematical language for studying quantum kinematics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a highly abstract yet powerful mathematical machinery that unifies quantum geometry and dynamics by translating non-linear evolution into linear algebraic flows within nilpotent structures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a rigorous, unified geometric framework for quantum state spaces by embedding them into higher-order dual number algebras.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the non-linear Liouville-von Neumann dynamics map globally onto flat, linear, and rigid algebraic flows within these nilpotent dual algebras.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing gauge-invariant density map embeddings ($\Psi_{Q_N}$) from the physical projective Hilbert space $	ext{P}(H_N)$ into truncated polynomial rings $Q_N$.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the quantum state space of an N-level system (e.g., qubits, qutrits), whose dynamics are governed by the Liouville-von Neumann equation.

**Key observables.** Fubini-Study metric ($ds^2_{FS}$), density matrix $ho$, and related geometric scaling factors ($\omega_N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $N$ of the system, which dictates the order of nilpotency in the dual algebra ($\varepsilon^{N^2-1}=0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that using non-reduced schemes and nilpotent dual number algebras provides a complete, non-singular container for all physical degrees of freedom.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; the analysis focuses on deriving metric relations ($ds^2_{QN} = 	ext{factor} \cdot ds^2_{FS}$) and algebraic isomorphisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from abstract algebraic structures (Grothendieck schemes, dual algebras) to physical systems ($	ext{P}(H_N)$), using the density embedding map as the unifying tool across different dimensions $N$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper establishes a rigorous, unified geometric framework for quantum state spaces by constructing smooth, regular embeddings into higher-order dual number algebras $\mathcal{Q}_N \equiv \mathbb{R}[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{N^2-1})$, wherein every quantum state is faithfully represented as a non-reduced scheme-theoretic point. We show that under this unified family of truncated rings, the non-linear matrix commutators governing the Liouville-von Neumann dynamics map globally onto flat, linear, and rigid algebraic flows, establishing nilpotent dual algebras as a pristine geometric landscape for higher-dimensional quantum kinematics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the discovery of a new crystalline phase, $	ext{Pr}_9	ext{Ge}_{16}$, which exhibits ordered vacancies within its structure. Electrically, it behaves as a metal with electron-like carriers, and magnetically, it displays an anisotropic magnetic order that can be suppressed by a small external field.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily experimental, the investigation into the interplay between crystal lattice distortion (vacancy ordering) and emergent magnetic/electronic states is central to strongly correlated electron physics. The comparison to topological semimetals suggests potential avenues for studying exotic quantum phases in these materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study reports the discovery of a new crystal structure ($	ext{Pr}_9	ext{Ge}_{16}$) and characterizes its complex physical properties, including magnetic and electronic behavior.

**Main result.** Single crystals were grown adopting an orthorhombic $Fdd2$ structure with ordered Ge vacancies. The material exhibits metallic conduction, electron-like carriers ($n \sim 10^{27} 	ext{ m}^{-3}$), and a magnetic transition at $T_C = 14.3 	ext{ K}$.

**Method.** The research employed single crystal growth (high-temperature flux), structural characterization via XRD/Neutron Diffraction, and transport measurements (resistivity, Hall effect) alongside magnetization studies.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the rare earth germanide $	ext{Pr}_9	ext{Ge}_{16}$. The electronic structure analysis suggests that the non-Kramers $	ext{Pr}^{3+}$ ion's $J=4$ ground state splits into nine singlet states due to low site symmetry.

**Key observables.** Anisotropic magnetization (easy axis along $b$), resistivity anomaly at $14.3 	ext{ K}$, Hall carrier concentration ($10^{27} 	ext{ m}^{-3}$), and satellite reflections indicating vacancy ordering.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field required to suppress order ($\approx 0.4 	ext{ T}$); Carrier concentration ($n \sim 10^{27} 	ext{ m}^{-3}$); Transition temperature ($T_C = 14.3 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors assume the structure is best described by the $Fdd2$ space group, despite discrepancies with published patterns; analysis of electronic properties relies on fitting Hall data to a one-band model.

**Figures summary.** Figures show powder XRD patterns comparing observed data to proposed structures ($I4_1/amd$ vs. $Fdd2$), and phase diagrams mapping magnetic order versus temperature and field.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a progression from structural discovery (new $Fdd2$ structure with vacancy ordering) to electronic characterization (metallic behavior, electron carriers), culminating in the detailed analysis of anisotropic magnetic properties and phase transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we report the discovery of a new crystal structure on the Ge-rich side of the Pr-Ge binary phase diagram. Using a high-temperature flux technique, we grew single crystals of $Pr_9Ge_{16}$, which adopt a previously unreported orthorhombic $Fdd$2 structure type featuring ordered Ge vacancies. We present the anisotropic magnetic properties and identify the crystallographic $b$ axis perpendicular to the crystal plane as the magnetic easy axis. Temperature-dependent resistivity measurements reveal metallic behavior with a distinct anomaly at $T_{\mathrm{C}}$ = 14.3 K. Hall resistivity data indicate that electron-like carriers dominate, with a carrier concentration on the order of $10^{27}~\mathrm{m}^{-3}$. The magnetic order is readily suppressed by a magnetic field of approximately 0.4 T applied along the easy $b$ axis.

</details>

**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12802v1>

**Summary.** The authors propose a novel variational framework to solve finite-horizon quantum state transfer problems. They replace the difficult task of optimizing time-dependent controls with minimizing an infidelity cost function over parameters defining a trainable, hardware-efficient quantum circuit. This method offers a flexible and implementable alternative for controlling quantum systems on current quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a practical, device-compatible method for quantum control that bypasses complex Hamiltonian synthesis by framing the problem as a variational circuit optimization, which is highly relevant for near-term quantum hardware implementation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses quantum control for steering an N-qubit system from an initial state to a target state over a fixed time horizon.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that replacing explicit time-dependent controls with optimization over hardware-efficient parameterized circuits achieves high-fidelity state transfer, though fidelity degrades with increasing system size or circuit depth due to optimization challenges.

**Method.** A variational quantum approach is used where the control objective is minimized by optimizing parameters ($	heta$) within a trainable, hardware-efficient ansatz unitary $U(	heta)$, avoiding direct time evolution simulation.

**Model / system.** The system is an N-qubit closed quantum system whose evolution is governed by controlled dynamics. The method uses a parameterized circuit composed of alternating single-qubit rotations and nearest-neighbor entangling gates.

**Key observables.** Terminal fidelity ($F(	heta)$) between the evolved state and the target state, and the corresponding loss function $J(	heta)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($N \in \{3, 5, 7\}$), circuit depth ($L$), and optimization parameters ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the terminal evolution can be accurately approximated by a fixed-depth, hardware-efficient ansatz; it also operates in an open-loop control setting.

**Figures summary.** Figures show how final fidelity changes with circuit depth ($L$) for various system sizes ($N$), and illustrate the increased sensitivity (broader distribution of fidelities) to initialization as $N$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the variational framework, details the hardware-efficient ansatz structure, presents numerical results showing performance vs. depth/size, and discusses the trade-offs between expressivity and optimization complexity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a variational quantum framework for finite-horizon quantum control based on hardware-efficient ansätze. The objective is to steer a quantum system from a given initial state to a desired target state over a fixed time horizon by minimizing a terminal cost defined in terms of state fidelity. Instead of explicitly synthesizing time-dependent control fields or enforcing Hamiltonian reachability constraints, the proposed method reformulates the control objective as a variational optimization problem in which a hardware-efficient parameterized quantum circuit provides a surrogate parameterization of the terminal evolution. The circuit consists of alternating layers of single-qubit rotations and entangling gates, whose parameters are optimized using classical routines to minimize the terminal infidelity. This formulation avoids reliance on problem-specific or physics-inspired ansätze, providing a flexible and implementation-friendly approach compatible with near-term quantum devices. Numerical experiments on multi-qubit state-transfer benchmarks demonstrate high-fidelity state transfer while highlighting the trade-off between ansatz expressivity, optimization complexity, and scalability with respect to system size and circuit depth.

</details>

**Summary.** The study investigates the field-induced phase diagram of $	ext{Ca}_3(	ext{Ru}_{0.99}	ext{Ti}_{0.01})_2	ext{O}_7$, an antiferromagnetic Mott insulator. By measuring magnetization and resistivity under applied fields, the authors reveal distinct first-order transitions along the easy axis, leading to a forced ferromagnetic metallic state. This work highlights the extreme sensitivity of correlated oxides to external perturbations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work exemplifies how external fields can tune correlated electron systems between distinct insulating (Mott/AFM) and metallic (FM) phases, providing experimental realization of complex magnetic quantum phase transitions relevant to strongly correlated physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates magnetic field-driven phase switching in a bandwidth-controlled antiferromagnetic Mott insulator to map out its complex magnetic H-T phase diagram.

**Main result.** Applying a field along the easy $b$-axis induces a first-order spin-flop transition ($\approx 6$ T) followed by a forced ferromagnetic metallic state ($>10.5$ T), while the $a$-axis shows only conventional AFM suppression up to 14 T.

**Method.** The research combines magnetic susceptibility ($\chi(T)$), magnetization ($M(H)$), and in-plane resistivity ($ho$) measurements under applied fields along different crystallographic axes.

**Model / system.** The system is $	ext{Ca}_3(	ext{Ru}_{0.99}	ext{Ti}_{0.01})_2	ext{O}_7$, a Mott insulator derived from $	ext{Ca}_3	ext{Ru}_2	ext{O}_7$. Its properties are governed by intertwined electron-electron and electron-lattice interactions, leading to competing magnetic/metallic ground states.

**Key observables.** Magnetization ($M$), resistivity ($ho$), critical fields ($H_f^b$, $H_{	ext{sat}}^b$), and the temperature dependence of magnetic order ($T_N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied magnetic field strength (up to 14 T), doping concentration (1% Ti substitution), and temperature range (down to 5 K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares experimental anomalies against theoretical models for anisotropic antiferromagnets, noting that the observed critical fields are significantly renormalized compared to simple Heisenberg models.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $\chi(T)$ vs $H$ and $M(H)$ vs $H$ along both axes. Color maps show $ho(T, H)$, detailing distinct phase boundaries corresponding to spin-flop and forced FM states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents measurements of magnetic and transport properties as a function of applied field ($a$ vs $b$ axis) at low temperatures, building up the complete H-T phase diagram by correlating magnetization jumps with resistivity changes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A bandwidth-controlled antiferromagnetic Mott-insulating phase in Ca$_3$(Ru$_{1-x}$Ti$_x$)$_2$O$_7$ is realized through isovalent substitution at the Ru site. For a dilute substitution with only 1% Ti, the Mott insulator ground state remains nearly degenerate with the ground state of pristine Ca$_3$Ru$_2$O$_7$, where the Ru moments are ferromagnetically aligned within the metallic RuO$_2$ bilayers stacked in an antiferromagnetic fashion. The exceptionally shallow free energy landscape of this doped compound arises from intertwined electron-electron and electron-lattice interactions. This makes its magnetic and transport properties highly sensitive to external perturbations. We systematically investigated magnetic-field-induced phase switching in Ca$_3$(Ru$_{0.99}$Ti$_{0.01}$)$_2$O$_7$ to explore its magnetic $H$-$T$ phase diagram. With the field applied along the easy $b$-axis, parallel to the antiferromagnetic moments, the magnetization exhibits a first-order spin-flop transition at $\approx $ 6 T, indicating reorientation of the Ru moments perpendicular to the field. The transition is accompanied by a decrease in the electrical resistance, but the spin-flop phase remains insulating. Above 10.5 T, all Ru moments align with the $b$-axis, resulting in a forced ferromagnetic metallic phase. In contrast, neither spin-flop nor forced-ferromagnetic phases are observed up to 14 T, when the field is applied along the $a$-axis. While the electronic kinetic energy and the electron-lattice coupling contribute to the free-energy balance of this system, the resulting $H$-$T$ phase diagram is remarkably simple and closely resembles that of a canonical anisotropic antiferromagnet, albeit with substantially renormalized critical fields.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the error convergence of splitting methods applied to simulate quantum dynamics with a Yukawa potential. It proves that the simulation achieves an optimal global time-step convergence rate of $O(t^{1/4})$ by establishing both upper and lower bounds. The work is significant for providing mathematically rigorous tools for simulating many-body systems where interactions are short-ranged but singular at the origin.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides highly technical convergence guarantees for simulating complex quantum many-body systems, which directly informs the development of stable and efficient numerical algorithms for condensed matter simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the convergence rate of splitting methods used to simulate quantum dynamics governed by the Schrödinger equation with a Yukawa potential.

**Main result.** It proves a global O(t^1/4) convergence rate for many-body interactions, establishing this rate as sharp by deriving a corresponding lower bound. The result shows that the short-range nature of the Yukawa potential does not improve upon the leading error obstruction dictated by the origin singularity.

**Method.** The analysis employs rigorous mathematical techniques including Sobolev estimates, Fourier analysis, and Kato smoothing to derive both upper (convergence) and lower bounds on the time evolution error.

**Model / system.** The system is a many-body quantum mechanical model described by the Schrödinger equation with a Yukawa potential, which acts as a screened Coulomb interaction. The Hamiltonian is $H_\mu = -\Delta + V_\mu$, where $V_\mu$ decays exponentially at large distances.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the error norm $\left\|U_{1,\mu}(t)^L\psi_0 - e^{-iT}H_\mu\psi_0ight\|_{L^2(\mathbb{R}^{3N})}$, which measures the difference between the numerical approximation and the exact evolution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the time step ($\Delta t$ or $t$), the total time ($T$), the number of particles ($N$), and the screening parameter ($\mu$) defining the potential range.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires working in strong norms, specifically the $H^2(\mathbb{R}^{3N})$ domain, due to the unbounded kinetic energy and singular nature of the potential at the origin.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the physical model (Yukawa potential) to developing rigorous error estimates. It first proves an upper bound on the splitting error, leveraging novel system-size Sobolev estimates. Subsequently, it establishes a matching lower bound using Fourier analysis and Kato smoothing to prove the rate is optimal.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Splitting methods are among the most classical and fundamental tools for the simulation of quantum dynamics, and their importance has grown further with the rise of quantum computing. In this work, we analyze the Schrödinger equation with Yukawa potential, a physically relevant and widely used model potential. It may be viewed as a Coulomb interaction with exponential decay at spatial infinity, preserving the Coulomb singularity at the origin while removing the long-range Coulomb tail. We prove that the operator splitting for this unbounded Hamiltonian achieves a global $1/4$-order convergence rate in the time step for many-body Yukawa interactions, with explicit polynomial dependence on the number of particles. The result holds for all initial wavefunctions in $H^2(\mathbb R^{3N})$, the natural domain of the Hamiltonian, and our numerical experiments are consistent with the theoretical estimates. To identify the sharp obstruction behind this rate, we prove a short-time lower bound in the one-body setting of order $t^{5/4}$ for the one-step error, which rules out any uniform global estimate of order better than $1/4$ in general. This agreement with the optimal $1/4$ rate in the Coulomb case is particularly interesting, as Yukawa potential is short-ranged compared to Coulomb potential. For the many-body upper bound, one of the new technical ingredients is the explicit polynomial-in-system-size Sobolev estimates of many-body Yukawa systems. These estimates are crucial for obtaining fully a priori bounds that depend only on the norms of the initial states, rather than on the solution at time $t$. For the one-body lower bound, we leverage a new analysis argument based on Fourier analysis and Kato smoothing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, traceable method for measuring microwave RF power directly at the cryogenic device plane within a dilution refrigerator. It achieves this by correlating measured DC heating power with absorbed RF power via thermal transient analysis of a Variable Temperature Stage (VTS). The technique integrates advanced cryogenic S-parameter calibration to provide reliable power readings essential for quantum circuit characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on metrology, this work provides a critical enabling technology for quantum physics experiments by ensuring that the input power to sensitive qubits or superconducting circuits is known with traceable accuracy.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately measuring microwave power delivered to a superconducting quantum circuit (DUT) inside a dilution refrigerator is challenging due to various cryogenic signal path losses and mismatches.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated an in situ measurement method, achieving relative standard uncertainties between 2% and 40% across the tested power range (-43 to -58 dBm).

**Method.** The technique combines AC/DC thermal power transfer measurements using a Variable Temperature Stage (VTS) with cryogenic two-port scattering parameter (S-parameter) calibration.

**Model / system.** The experiment is performed in a dilution refrigerator, utilizing a custom VTS weakly coupled to the cold finger. The system's thermal dynamics are modeled by analyzing transient temperature changes when heated alternately by DC current and microwave power dissipated via an attenuator.

**Key observables.** VTS temperature ($T_{	ext{VTS}}$), measured DC heating power ($	ext{P}_{	ext{DC,H}}$), and RF attenuated power ($	ext{P}_{	ext{RF,A}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurement range covers powers from -43 dBm to -58 dBm; key derived parameters include the thermal conductance ($G_0$) and background heat load ($P_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the temperature difference across the attenuator is negligible compared to uncertainty, and it relies on accurate SOLR calibration for S-parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the experimental setup involving the VTS weakly linked to the Cold Finger, showing connections for DC heating, RF attenuation, and thermometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the difficulty of cryogenic power measurement, details the combined thermal/electrical methodology (using transient fitting and steady-state analysis), presents the necessary S-parameter calibration steps, and concludes with experimental results and uncertainty quantification.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate knowledge of the microwave power delivered to a cryogenic device under test (DUT) is essential for the characterization and operation of superconducting quantum circuits. However, this information is difficult to obtain inside dilution refrigerators because of distributed attenuation, impedance mismatch, switch-path repeatability, and temperature-dependent microwave components. This paper presents an in situ measurement method for RF power at the cryogenic device plane. The method uses a custom variable temperature stage (VTS) as a cryogenic thermal-transfer element. The TVS is alternately heated by a four-wire DC heater and by microwave power dissipated in a 20 dB pass-through attenuator. By fitting the thermal transients and comparing the corresponding steady-state temperatures, the absorbed microwave power is inferred from a directly measured DC electrical power through an AC/DC substitution procedure. The finite reflection and transmission of the attenuator are then accounted for by cryogenic two-port scattering-parameter measurements based on a switch-assisted Short--Open--Load--Reciprocal calibration, so that the result is referred to the DUT reference plane. The system is demonstrated in a dilution refrigerator with powers between -43 and -58 dBm at the DUT input plane. The demonstrated relative standard uncertainty ranges from about 2% at -43.9 dBm to about 40% at -57.6 dBm. The proposed approach combines thermal RF power transfer, cryogenic S-parameter correction, and uncertainty evaluation in a measurement architecture compatible with quantum-device experiments, providing a practical route toward traceable microwave-power calibration at millikelvin stages.

</details>

**Authors:** Nguyen Dinh Quyen, Vu Tuan Hai, Quoc Chuong Nguyen, Le Bin Ho, Lan Nguyen Tran  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12487v1>

This intersection creates a severe dynamical bottleneck, maximizing inter-class overlap and causing the sharp drop in accuracy observed during early training iterations. **Summary.** This work systematically benchmarks three loss functions for training quantum feature maps in Quantum Machine Learning. By comparing Distance Loss, Measurement Loss, and Log-Likelihood Loss, the authors demonstrate that LLL offers a superior balance of optimization stability and linear computational scaling. This provides crucial practical guidance for designing robust variational quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QML classification rather than fundamental AMO physics, the comparison of optimization objectives (loss landscapes) is highly relevant to understanding variational quantum algorithms used in simulating complex many-body Hamiltonians or characterizing open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks different loss functions for Trainable Quantum Feature Maps (TQFMs) used in Quantum Machine Learning (QML) to improve classification performance.

**Main result.** Log-Likelihood Loss consistently achieves more stable optimization than Measurement Loss while maintaining the desirable linear computational complexity, offering practical guidance for QML design.

**Method.** The authors compare three loss functions—Distance Loss, Measurement Loss, and Log-Likelihood Loss—by optimizing TQFMs using numerical experiments on various datasets.

**Model / system.** The model involves Quantum Support Vector Machines (QSVMs) utilizing parameterized quantum circuits (TQFMs) to encode classical data into quantum states for classification tasks.

**Key observables.** Optimization dynamics, state overlap convergence, Mean Classification Accuracy, and computational scaling ($O(M^2)$ vs $O(M)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of training samples ($M$), number of variational layers ($\#Layers$), and the specific loss function used.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that optimizing the loss function guides the quantum state towards optimal class separation, although the final decision boundary often relies on a classical SVM classifier.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare optimization dynamics (loss/overlap vs. iteration) for different losses; one figure illustrates the 'trajectory crossing problem' in ML-based objectives; and tables summarize classification accuracy across datasets and circuit depths.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces TQFMs, details the mathematical formulation of DL, ML, and LLL loss functions, compares their computational costs ($O(M^2)$ vs $O(M)$), presents results on optimization stability and final test accuracy, and concludes with practical guidelines.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many quantum machine learning models employ quantum feature maps to encode classical data into quantum states. While fixed feature maps often lack sufficient expressivity for complex nonlinear classification tasks, trainable quantum feature maps (TQFMs) enable adaptive quantum kernels with enhanced learning capability. Different loss functions can induce distinct optimization dynamics, yet their effects remain poorly understood. In this work, we apply the Log-Likelihood Loss function for TQFMs and provide a systematic comparison with Distance Loss and Measurement Loss. Through extensive numerical experiments, we compare their optimization dynamics, computational costs, and classification performance. Our results show that Log-Likelihood Loss consistently achieves more stable optimization than Measurement Loss while retaining linear computational complexity. The resulting benchmark offers practical guidance for balancing trainability, computational efficiency, and predictive performance in quantum kernel optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates strange metallicity in overdoped cuprates by measuring magnetothermopower up to 35 T. The key finding is that the anomalous field dependence cannot be explained by standard theory, suggesting two coexisting charge carrier sectors. The authors propose a model where conventional Fermi-liquid behavior coexists with an unusual, particle-hole symmetric component linked to superconducting fluctuations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cuprates, the concept of separating transport into distinct symmetry sectors (e.g., particle-hole symmetric vs. asymmetric) is relevant for understanding exotic quantum states in other correlated systems where multiple degrees of freedom coexist.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the microscopic origin of 'strange metallicity' observed in overdoped cuprate superconductors by analyzing their transport properties.

**Main result.** The anomalous magnetothermopower suggests the existence of two distinct charge carrier sectors: a conventional Fermi-liquid sector and a nearly particle-hole symmetric sector dominated by superconducting fluctuations above Tc.

**Method.** The research combines high-field magnetothermopower measurements with theoretical modeling using Boltzmann transport theory and phenomenological real-space models.

**Model / system.** The physical system is overdoped (Bi,Pb)2(Sr,La)2CuO6+delta single crystals. The model proposes disconnected Fermi-liquid islands embedded in a compensated Dirac liquid of d-wave Bogoliubov quasiparticles.

**Key observables.** Magnetothermopower $S(H)$, zero-field Seebeck coefficient $S(T)$, Hall resistivity $ho_{xy}(T, H)$, and magnetoresistance (MR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic fields up to 35 T; temperature dependence near Tc; doping level in cuprates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model is phenomenological, assuming the coexistence of two distinct transport sectors governed by different symmetries (particle-hole asymmetry).

**Figures summary.** Figures show $ho_{ab}(T)$ and $S(T)$ vs. doping, detailed characterization of magnetothermopower $|S/T|$ vs. magnetic field, and procedures for symmetrizing the measured thermoelectric voltages.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the failure of standard Boltzmann theory to explain field-dependent thermopower, leading to the hypothesis of multiple transport sectors; it then develops a two-fluid model (FL islands in a Dirac liquid) to reconcile zero-field and high-field anomalies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Here, we report magnetothermopower measurements on overdoped (Bi,Pb)2(Sr,La)2CuO6+delta (Bi2201) single crystals in magnetic fields up to 35 T. Whereas the temperature dependence of the zero-field Seebeck coefficient S(T) can be captured using Boltzmann transport theory, the field-dependent response S(H) cannot. Instead, the magnetothermopower contains a large additional contribution whose field and temperature dependence is consistent with the presence of short-range superconducting order well above Tc. Combined with earlier Hall and magnetoresistance results, these data imply that the overdoped cuprate strange metal contains two transport sectors with distinct particle-hole symmetry: a conventional particle-hole-asymmetric Fermi-liquid (FL) contribution governing the Hall effect and the zero-field thermopower, and a nearly particle-hole-symmetric sector dominating the anomalous longitudinal magnetotransport. We formulate a phenomenological real-space model in which disconnected FL islands are embedded in a compensated Dirac liquid of phase-incoherent d-wave Bogoliubov quasiparticles. This picture reconciles conventional zero-field transport with anomalous magnetothermopower and magnetoresistance and offers a concrete framework for thinking about strange metallicity in overdoped cuprates.

</details>

### [Quantum Weakest Preconditions Revisited: Pre-expectations for Expected Runtime Analysis](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12532v1)

**Authors:** Christina Gehnen, Dominique Unruh, Joost-Pieter Katoen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12532v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

The measurement operators are 𝑀0 = |0⟩⟨0|, 𝑀1 = |1⟩⟨1| for the first loop on variable 𝑞2 and 𝑀0 = |0⟩⟨0|, 𝑀1 = I −|0⟩⟨0| for the second loop on variable 𝑞1.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel weakest pre-expectation framework designed to analyze the expected runtime of general quantum programs. By extending traditional verification tools to handle reward statements and infinite expectations, it overcomes limitations that previously restricted analysis to bounded or terminating cases. This advances the theoretical understanding of resource quantification in quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous tools (weakest pre-expectations) to analyze resource usage (expected time) in complex quantum algorithms, which is crucial for assessing the feasibility of large-scale quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper revisits quantum weakest preconditions to develop a framework for analyzing the expected runtime of general quantum programs without requiring an upper bound on that runtime.

**Main result.** A novel pre-expectation framework is introduced, allowing analysis of expected runtimes even when they are potentially infinite, by defining weakest pre-expectations for quantum while loops with rewards.

**Method.** The authors define a syntactic and semantic calculus for 'qrWhile' programs, utilizing weak pre-expectations that incorporate reward statements to track expected values.

**Model / system.** The model is general quantum computation involving density operators on Hilbert spaces, supporting unbounded operators and control flow structures like while loops.

**Key observables.** Expected runtime ($\mathbb{E}[	ext{ertJ} \dots]$), rewards, and the weak pre-expectation itself.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis handles potentially infinite expected runtimes and operates in settings involving unbounded operators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous work often required programs to be Almost Surely Terminating (AST) or restricted to bounded preconditions; this framework aims to overcome those limitations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from foundational concepts of Hilbert spaces and operators, defines the necessary semantics for quantum programs with rewards, establishes the weakest pre-expectation calculus, and finally demonstrates its application to compute expected runtimes via syntactic transformations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum weakest preconditions are a fundamental tool for program verification of quantum programs. Many variations have been reported in the literature. We revisit quantum weakest preconditions from the perspective of expected runtime analysis of quantum programs and introduce a novel pre-expectation framework that enables to reason about the preconditions of quantum programs without the need of an upper bound. This is particularly interesting for quantum programs involving reward statements. The overall goal is to analyze runtime behavior even in the case of programs with potentially infinite expected runtime. This paper presents several ways to do so, e.g., a program transformation such that the expected runtime of a quantum program can be expressed using the weakest pre-expectation calculus with rewards.

</details>

**Authors:** Zi Yuan, Jun Zhan, Xianxin Wu, Shaozhi Li

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how the combination of electronic correlations and lattice vibrations dictates superconducting symmetry in nickelates. By applying advanced many-body techniques, the authors predict that spin fluctuations favor d-wave pairing while electron-phonon coupling induces s-wave pairing, resulting in a mixed $s+id$ state. This mechanism offers a quantitative explanation for the experimentally observed spatial inhomogeneity of superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit inclusion of electron-phonon coupling alongside strong electronic correlations to predict a mixed pairing symmetry ($s+id$) provides a rich, multi-faceted model for unconventional superconductivity mechanisms relevant to condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the microscopic origin of spatially varying superconducting symmetries observed experimentally in infinite-layer nickelates, moving beyond simple d-wave models.

**Main result.** The interplay between spin fluctuations (driving d-wave pairing) and electron-phonon coupling (inducing s-wave pairing) leads to a mixed $s+id$ superconducting state. This $s$-wave component is highly sensitive to carrier density, suggesting local defects can explain observed symmetry variations.

**Method.** The authors combine first-principles calculations with the Fluctuation-Exchange–Migdal-Eliashberg (FLEX–ME) theory to model pairing mechanisms.

**Model / system.** The system is infinite-layer nickelates ($\mathrm{R}_{1-x}\mathrm{Sr}_x\mathrm{NiO}_2$), modeled using a two-orbital approach that incorporates on-site Coulomb repulsion ($U_d$) and electron-phonon coupling ($\mathcal{H}_{	ext{eph}}$) involving the oxygen $A_{1g}$ phonon mode.

**Key observables.** Superconducting order parameters ($\Phi_d$ for d-orbital, $\Phi_s$ for IO orbital), superconducting transition temperatures ($T_{c}$), and SC gaps (measured in meV).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron-phonon coupling strength ($\lambda$), carrier density ($n$), and the specific phonon mode involved ($A_{1g}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on combining DFT calculations with FLEX–ME theory, which captures both spin fluctuation (d-wave) and charge fluctuation/e-ph (s-wave) pairing contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phonon properties, phase diagrams showing SC regimes vs. $\lambda$ for fixed $n$, and temperature dependence of the superconducting order parameters ($\Phi_d, \Phi_s$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical system (nickelates) and the theoretical framework (FLEX–ME theory). It then calculates the contributions from spin fluctuations (d-wave) and e-ph coupling (s-wave), culminating in the prediction of an $s+id$ state whose stability depends critically on carrier density.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent tunneling measurements on infinite-layer nickelates have revealed spatially varying superconducting symmetries, whose microscopic origin remains unclear. Motivated by this observation, we investigate the interplay between electron correlations and electron-phonon interactions in infinite-layer nickelates by combining first-principles calculations with the fluctuation-exchange-Migdal-Eliashberg theory. Our calculations show that spin fluctuations yield robust $d$-wave superconductivity on the Ni $d_{x^2-y^2}$ orbital, whereas electron-phonon coupling induces $s$-wave pairing on an interstitial orbital, leading to an $s+id$ superconducting state. The emergence of the $s$-wave component is strongly carrier-density dependent: an intermediate electron-phonon coupling of $λ=0.4$ stabilizes the $s+id$ state at $n=0.9$ but not at $n=0.8$. These results imply that local oxygen defects tune the local electron density and form finite-size domains with distinct pairing symmetries, offering a compelling explanation for the spatially inhomogeneous superconducting symmetries observed in experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the complex Charge Density Wave (CDW) states in hole-doped layered tellurides using low-temperature STM. By analyzing unoccupied electronic states, the authors resolved three distinct, interweaved CDW wave vectors ($\mathbf{q}_1, \mathbf{q}_2, \mathbf{q}_3$) and a superstructure component ($\mathbf{p}$). These findings provide critical evidence linking the emergence of multiple CDWs to Fermi surface nesting driven by hole doping.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed mapping of multiple, interweaved CDW vectors in relation to specific orbital character ($p$-orbitals) provides a rich playground for understanding charge instabilities and electronic correlations beyond simple mean-field models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is elucidating the origin of complex, interweaved Charge Density Wave (CDW) states observed in hole-doped layered rare-earth tellurides.

**Main result.** STM measurements revealed three distinct, superimposed CDW wave vectors ($q_1, q_2, q_3$) in unoccupied electronic states, which are theoretically linked to nesting vectors connecting $p_x$ and $p_y$ bands due to hole doping.

**Method.** The study combines low-temperature Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) on cleaved surfaces with Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) analysis of the resulting electronic maps, supplemented by theoretical calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is layered rare-earth tellurides ($	ext{LaTe}_{2-x}	ext{Sb}_x$), focusing on the $	ext{Te-Te}$ or $	ext{Sb}$-substituted sheets. The physics involves CDW formation, superstructure development, and electronic structure mapping in response to hole doping.

**Key observables.** Three distinct CDW wave vectors ($\mathbf{q}_1 = 0.73 	ext{ a}^*$, $\mathbf{q}_2 = 0.47 	ext{ a}^*$, $\mathbf{q}_3 = 0.27 	ext{ a}^*$), the superstructure vector ($\mathbf{p}$), and modulation patterns observed in unoccupied electronic states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature (9 K); hole doping concentration ($x$); lattice constants; nesting vectors connecting $p_x$- and $p_y$-bands.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The CDW modulations are primarily governed by the filling of unoccupied electronic states, and the observed wave vectors can be mapped to Fermi surface nesting conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures, SAED patterns showing multiple reflections, and STM/FFT results demonstrating superimposed stripe modulations corresponding to the three distinct CDW wave vectors and a superstructure spot $\mathbf{p}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying complex diffraction features via TEM to performing detailed STM measurements on unoccupied states. It resolves three interweaved CDWs ($\mathbf{q}_1, \mathbf{q}_2, \mathbf{q}_3$) and attributes the superstructure ($\mathbf{p}$) to covalent-like quasi-bonding (CLQB), confirming their origin through theoretical nesting vector analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multiple CDW-ordered layered rare-earth tellurides have increasingly emerged as a research hotspot, owing to their unconventional CDW formation, high transition temperature, and confirmed existence of axial Higgs modes. Recently, interweaved CDW in LaTe2 and its element-substituted phase LaTe2-xSbx have been investigated through TEM and ARPES measurements, revealing their distinct origins. Nevertheless, several complex diffraction features observed in TEM patterns remain unelucidated. In this work, we carried out scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) on LaTe1.6Sb0.4 crystals at 9 K. Three interweaved CDW wave vectors, q1=8/11a*, q2=5/11a* and q3=3/11a* were observed, which are induced by hole doping in unoccupied states. The q1 and q3 are theoretically verified to be nesting vectors connecting px- and py- bands. Furthermore, the satellite spots relative to the main Bragg spots p corresponding to a 11-a-superlattice have also been detected. Our findings provide critical insights for further exploring the origin of the interweaved CDW in hole/electron doping materials.

</details>

**Authors:** Gleydson Fernandes de Jesus, Bruno Oziel Fernandez, Marcelo A. Moret  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12060v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
