**Summary.** This paper tackles the computational bottleneck of molecular docking by reformulating it as an NP-hard clique finding problem solvable with quantum computation. The authors introduce and apply Quantum Circuit Evolution (QCE), a novel, gradient-free quantum evolutionary method, to find optimal receptor-ligand binding configurations. Results show that QCE demonstrates fast and stable convergence toward the best possible solutions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of a novel, gradient-free quantum optimization technique like QCE to a complex physical problem rooted in molecular interactions provides a concrete testbed for near-term quantum algorithm development beyond standard QAOA implementations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The high computational cost of molecular docking, a crucial step in drug discovery, limits its applicability.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum Circuit Evolution (QCE) method successfully found the maximum clique solution for tested systems in many experiments, often requiring fewer steps than previous quantum algorithms.

**Method.** The authors propose using QCE, a gate-based and gradient-free quantum evolutionary method, to solve the problem by minimizing an objective function derived from the Hamiltonian of the molecular interaction graph.

**Model / system.** The biological docking problem is mapped onto finding the Maximum Vertex Weight Clique Problem (MVWCP), which is formulated as a QUBO Hamiltonian ($H$) suitable for quantum computation on qubits.

**Key observables.** Convergence behavior, cost function values, and the size of the found clique relative to the maximum possible clique.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit counts (6, 8, and 12), temperature/distance thresholds ($	au$), and the perturbation parameter $\epsilon$ used in circuit evolution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work relies on simulations using the CUDA-Q SDK due to the prohibitive cost of accessing real quantum hardware; QCE is claimed not to require classical optimization of parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate distance comparisons for binding interactions, show molecular structures (SARS-CoV-2 Mpro complex), and display convergence plots showing the mean cost function value decreasing over generations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first frames the problem by modeling docking as an MVWCP/QUBO. It then details the QCE algorithm—including its evolutionary operations (INSERT, DELETE, SWAP, MODIFY)—and presents results across multiple qubit counts and complexes, comparing performance against prior quantum methods.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Molecular docking is an important step in drug discovery, enabling the evaluation of receptor-ligand affinity while reducing experimental costs and increasing the number of possible tests. However, the high computational cost associated with molecular docking remains a limiting factor that can restrict both the experimental precision and the scale of the problems being addressed. To improve the future applicability of molecular docking, recent works have proposed the use of quantum algorithms based on Gaussian Boson Sampling quantum computers and also gate-based quantum computers. In this work, we propose the use of Quantum Circuit Evolution (QCE) for solving the molecular docking problem, a gate based and gradient-free quantum evolutionary method whose evolution is driven by the random application of unitary operations to a quantum circuit. The proposed algorithm demonstrated the ability to find the best solution to the problem in fewer steps than the methods presented in previous studies, exhibiting fast and stable convergence.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report establishing an unprecedentedly precise laboratory benchmark for the methanol radio line used in astrophysics. They achieve this by measuring related near-infrared optical transitions and combining them via a quantum 'triangulation' method, bypassing direct microwave measurement limitations. This high accuracy significantly enhances the potential of molecular spectroscopy to test fundamental physical constants over cosmic timescales.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work exemplifies how advanced molecular spectroscopy techniques—specifically using optical transitions to deduce microwave frequencies—can push precision limits far beyond what is achievable by direct measurement. The theoretical underpinning involving symmetry breaking due to internal rotation is a rich topic in molecular physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to determine a highly accurate laboratory rest frequency for the astrophysically important 12.2 GHz methanol transition to enable precise tests of fundamental physics, such as variations in the proton-to-electron mass ratio.

**Main result.** A sub-kHz benchmark frequency was established for this key radio line with an uncertainty of $135 	ext{ Hz}$, representing a 20-fold improvement over previous microwave spectroscopy methods.

**Method.** The measurement avoids direct microwave observation by using a novel optical 'triangulation scheme' involving pairs of near-infrared rovibrational transitions, which are then combined to yield the ground-state rotational difference frequency.

**Model / system.** The system is methanol ($	ext{CH}_3	ext{OH}$), treated as an achiral asymmetric rotor exhibiting hindered internal rotation. The symmetry analysis uses the Longuet–Higgins group $G_6$, which allows for a closed electric-dipole transition loop that would otherwise be forbidden by simple parity rules.

**Key observables.** The derived frequency of the $3_{-1}^E - 20^E$ rotational combination difference, measured via near-infrared Lamb-dip frequencies ($\approx 1.4 \mu	ext{m}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Statistical reproducibility of $10 	ext{ Hz}$ and absolute uncertainties as low as $130 	ext{ Hz}$. The measurement relies on locking to an ultra-stable optical frequency comb referenced to a hydrogen maser.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The systematic error from unresolved hyperfine structure is estimated at $\approx 10 	ext{ Hz}$, and the underlying multiplet is assumed to blend into a single symmetric profile.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the quantum level scheme used for the triangulation, connecting three common upper levels. Other figures detail the experimental setup using NICE-OHMS and frequency stability comparisons against external standards.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical necessity (testing fundamental constants), outlines the advanced spectroscopic technique (optical triangulation on $	ext{CH}_3	ext{OH}$), describes the high-precision measurement platform (NICE-OHMS locked to an OFC/H-maser), and finally presents the derived, highly accurate frequency benchmark.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Methanol radio lines observed in space provide sensitive probes of whether the proton-to-electron mass ratio has changed over cosmic time, but such tests require laboratory rest frequencies with very high accuracy. Here we determine the frequency of the astrophysically important 12.2 GHz $3_{-1}$E -- $2_{0}$E transition of CH$_3$OH by measuring near-infrared rovibrational transitions rather than the microwave line directly. Using wavelength-modulated NICE-OHMS locked to an ultra-stable optical frequency comb and referenced via a fiber link to a hydrogen-maser frequency standard, we measure Lamb-dip frequencies near 1.4 $μ$m (216 THz) with 10 Hz statistical reproducibility and absolute uncertainties as low as 130 Hz. Pairs of optical transitions sharing common upper levels form a triangulation scheme that yields the ground-state rotational combination difference. We obtain 12 178 596.415(135) kHz, improving on earlier molecular-beam microwave spectroscopy by a factor of 20 and agreeing with a recent free-induction-decay measurement. This result establishes a sub-kHz laboratory benchmark for a key radio-astronomical methanol line and demonstrates that optical triangulation can be extended to non-chiral molecules with internal rotation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Parallel Hadamard Test (PHT) to overcome the exponential growth of distinct circuits needed when estimating many quantum expectation values. By combining multiple standard Hadamard Tests into one circuit, PHT significantly reduces computational and financial overheads across various quantum workloads, including VQA gradient estimation and kernel calculations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource optimization in quantum algorithms by providing a hardware-aware compilation technique that minimizes circuit overheads, which is crucial for running complex variational algorithms on NISQ devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The conventional estimation of multiple quantum expectation values, such as those required for gradient calculation or kernel evaluation, leads to a proliferation of distinct quantum circuits.

**Main result.** The proposed Parallel Hadamard Test (PHT) unifies these estimations into a single circuit type, significantly reducing overheads in terms of circuit count, time, and cost across various workload structures.

**Method.** PHT combines multiple independent Hadamard Tests (HTs)—used to estimate $	ext{Re}[\langle\psi|U|\psiangle]$ or $	ext{Im}[\langle\psi|U|\psiangle]$—into one optimized quantum circuit structure.

**Model / system.** The work is general, applying to three structural classes of workloads: arbitrary sets of unitary operators (e.g., VQA gradients), prefix-product arrays (e.g., QCELS), and Gram-matrix elements (quantum kernel methods).

**Key observables.** Expectation values $	ext{Re}[\langle\psi|U|\psiangle]$ and $	ext{Im}[\langle\psi|U|\psiangle]$, energy expectation values $\langle H angle$, and gradients $rac{\partial\langle H angle}{\partial	heta_j}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required precision ($\epsilon$) dictates the number of shots; fixed per-circuit overheads become critical when many estimations are needed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that hardware supports mid-circuit measurement and reset (MCMR) to implement PHT efficiently, and that typical off-diagonal overlaps are small for shot reduction in Gram-matrix settings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the standard HT circuit, VQE circuits, gradient estimation circuits, and the core PHT structure showing how multiple estimations are combined into one gate preparation $V_{+W}(M)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the fundamental problem of estimating $\langle\psi|U|\psiangle$ via HT. It then proposes PHT as a unified solution, demonstrating its applicability and cost savings across three distinct structural classes: arbitrary sets, prefix-product arrays, and Gram matrices.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Hadamard test is a fundamental building block widely used in many quantum computing algorithms. It estimates the real or imaginary part of $\langle ψ\vert U \vert ψ\rangle$, where $\vert ψ\rangle$ is a quantum state and $U$ is a unitary operator. In many algorithms, however, many such quantities must be estimated, leading to a large number of distinct circuit types, long computational times, and high financial costs. In this work, we propose the parallel Hadamard test, which combines multiple Hadamard tests into a single circuit type. We demonstrate how the parallel Hadamard test applies to three structural classes of workloads: arbitrary sets of unitary operators, prefix-product arrays, and Gram-matrix elements. For each class, we compare the cost of the parallel Hadamard test with that of the conventional one. Our unified approach significantly reduces the number of distinct circuit types, and can lower both computational time and financial costs in regimes where fixed per-circuit overheads dominate the total cost. In Gram-matrix workloads, it can also reduce the total number of shots when typical off-diagonal overlaps are small.

</details>

### [The Resolution of the Identity as a Generator of Exact Integral Identities: A Coherent-State Approach](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12822v1)

**Authors:** Ghasem Asadi Cordshooli, Mehdi Mirzaee  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12822v1>

**Summary.** This work treats the Resolution of the Identity not as a given postulate but as an active generator for exact integral identities in quantum theory. By analyzing coherent states and projecting onto discrete bases, the authors show that the structure of state preservation dictates whether the resulting identity is a Kronecker delta or a Dirac delta. This offers a unifying perspective on basis representations across quantum mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep structural insight into quantum mechanics foundations, showing how seemingly independent mathematical postulates like orthonormality are unified under the single principle of Hilbert space completeness.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper reinterprets the Resolution of the Identity (ROI) from a passive mathematical tool into an active generator that dictates exact integral identities across different quantum bases.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the structural consistency required by Hilbert space completeness naturally yields both discrete orthonormality (Kronecker delta) and continuous localization kernels (Dirac delta).

**Method.** The core method involves systematically projecting the continuous coherent-state completeness relation onto the discrete Fock basis to derive integral representations for expansion coefficients.

**Model / system.** The study is set in general separable Hilbert spaces, utilizing Glauber coherent states ($\alpha$), position eigenstates ($x$), and the discrete Fock basis ($n$).

**Key observables.** Overlap functions (e.g., $\langleeta|\alphaangle$, $\langle x'|xangle$) and resulting identity kernels ($\delta_{nm}$, $\delta(x-x')$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coherent state parameter $\alpha \in \mathbb{C}$ and the scaling parameter $\sigma$ used to transition between overcomplete and localized representations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes a separable Hilbert space structure and relies on analyzing asymptotic limits ($\sigma 	o 0$) when recovering strictly orthogonal bases from coherent states.

**Figures summary.** Conceptually compares identity kernels, showing that overcomplete manifolds require an asymptotic contraction ($\sigma 	o 0$) to yield the sharp kernel seen in strictly orthogonal bases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the general formalism, then applying it specifically to coherent states (deriving a master integral identity and noting non-commutativity), followed by analyzing position eigenstates and Fock states separately before unifying these findings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The resolution of the identity is typically utilized as a passive representation tool in standard quantum mechanics textbooks. In this paper, we reinterpret this traditional perspective by introducing the resolution of the identity associated with Glauber coherent states as a direct generator of exact complex Gaussian integral identities. By systematically projecting the continuous coherent-state completeness relation onto the discrete Fock basis, highly generalized Gaussian integral relations emerge naturally as a direct consequence of state preservation, rather than being introduced as independent mathematical postulates. We demonstrate that this overarching formal structure simultaneously dictates both discrete (Kronecker delta) and continuous (Dirac delta) localization kernels under appropriate basis projections, providing a sharp conceptual contrast between the structural features of overcomplete and strictly orthogonal representations. Crucially, we highlight an intriguing non-commuting behavior in the parameter limits of the master identity. Requiring only the elementary properties of coherent states and the Dirac formalism, this approach offers a transparent and visually intuitive illustration of how Hilbert-space completeness inherently encodes vast libraries of exact, solvable mathematical relations -- providing a unifying perspective suitable for advanced undergraduate and graduate instruction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves a maximal Hohenberg-Kohn theorem for non-interacting quantum many-body systems. It achieves this by linking the uniqueness of the potential to the quasi-everywhere positivity of the ground state particle density, relying fundamentally on advanced techniques from classical potential theory. This work solidifies the mathematical basis for Density Functional Theory (DFT) in a rigorous setting.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on non-interacting systems (a simplification), establishing the rigorous mathematical foundation for the HK theorem is crucial background knowledge for understanding strongly correlated electron physics where density functionals are paramount.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a maximal Hohenberg-Kohn (HK) theorem for non-interacting quantum many-body systems within the framework of potential theory.

**Main result.** It successfully establishes the HK theorem for non-interacting systems, demonstrating that the uniqueness of the Kohn-Sham potential is guaranteed if the ground state single-particle density is positive quasi-everywhere.

**Method.** The proof heavily utilizes classical potential theory, particularly concepts related to form-bounded potentials and unique continuation properties of elliptic PDEs.

**Model / system.** The system involves many-electron quantum particles governed by Schrödinger operators. The analysis focuses on the ground state energy and density derived from Hamiltonians involving external potentials $v$ and pair interactions $w$.

**Key observables.** Ground state single-particle density $ho_\Psi(x)$ and its quasi-everywhere positivity ($ho_\Gamma(x) > 0$ q.e.).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The class of potentials considered is the maximal Laplace form-bounded potentials, and the analysis often assumes the ground state energy is discrete.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial results assume the ground state is 'weakly correlated' (finite rank single-particle density matrix), which is noted as a technical limitation for general interacting systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from necessary and sufficient conditions for potential uniqueness based on density positivity, extends these results to the maximal class of potentials, and proves the HK theorem specifically for non-interacting models using advanced tools from potential theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we show that for Schrödinger operators with weakly correlated ground states, the Hohenberg-Kohn theorem holds within the maximal class of form-bounded external potentials if and only if the single-particle density is positive quasi-everywhere. Furthermore, we show that these conditions are satisfied for the ground state of non-interacting Schrödinger operators with a discrete ground state energy. Consequently, we establish the Hohenberg-Kohn theorem for non-interacting systems, and therefore the uniqueness of the Kohn-Sham potential, within the maximal class of Laplace form-bounded potentials. The key ingredient to establish these results is a characterization of weakly correlated regular states, whose proof relies on classical potential theory. Moreover, our proof reveals that, in the continuum setting, the fundamental mechanism underlying the Hohenberg-Kohn theorem is the (quasi)-unique continuation of the density rather than of the many-body wavefunction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses barren plateaus in Quantum Recurrent Neural Networks by showing that the issue stems from independent parameterization across time steps. By enforcing time-translation symmetry through parameter sharing, the gradient variance scaling is stabilized from exponential decay to polynomial growth. This establishes task-aligned symmetry as a key architectural principle for building trainable quantum sequential models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QNN training, the concept of enforcing symmetries (like time-translation invariance) to stabilize optimization landscapes is highly relevant in theoretical physics when analyzing effective Hamiltonians or constrained dynamics in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The exponential vanishing of gradients, known as barren plateaus (BPs), poses a fundamental obstacle to training scalable quantum neural networks.

**Main result.** Enforcing time-translation symmetry via parameter sharing transforms the gradient variance scaling from an exponentially decaying function of sequence length to a polynomial one, thereby suppressing BPs.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving and calculating the gradient variance ($	ext{Var}[\partial_
u C]$) for Quantum Recurrent Neural Networks (QRNNs) under both independent and shared parameter settings.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on QRNNs, which process sequential data using variational quantum circuits. The architecture involves recurrent blocks ($W_t$) applied over a sequence length $T$ to couple hidden states (Storer) and inputs (Loader).

**Key observables.** Gradient variance ($	ext{Var}[\partial_
u C]$) of the loss function with respect to circuit parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sequence length ($T$), register size ($m$), and the presence/absence of parameter sharing across time steps.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that certain ansatz circuits form 2-designs, allowing for specific variance calculations; it also compares the idealized Haar measure approach with approximations necessary due to computational complexity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of gradient variance scaling: non-sharing leads to exponential decay (BP), while parameter sharing maintains a polynomial or constant variance, confirming BP mitigation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes that QRNNs without parameter sharing suffer from exponentially decaying gradient variance. It then rigorously proves that imposing time-translation symmetry through parameter sharing fundamentally alters this scaling to a polynomial dependence, thus resolving the expressivity-trainability tension.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Barren plateaus -- the exponential vanishing of gradients -- are a fundamental obstacle to training scalable quantum neural networks. Whether they arise in quantum recurrent neural networks (QRNNs), a natural architecture for sequential data, remains a pressing question. Here we show that the decisive ingredient for trainability in QRNNs is not the recurrent circuit topology per se, but enforcing time-translation symmetry through parameter sharing across time steps. We prove that, without parameter sharing, QRNNs suffer from barren plateaus, with gradient variance decaying exponentially with sequence length. Imposing parameter sharing across time steps fundamentally alters this scaling, transforming it into a polynomial dependence and thereby suppressing the barren plateau. Numerical simulations corroborate these analytical predictions. By rigorously showing how time-translation symmetry suppresses barren plateaus and enhances learning capability in QRNNs, our work establishes task-aligned symmetry as a constructive resolution to the expressivity-trainability tension in quantum neural networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces HarmQ, a novel backdoor attack specifically designed to exploit the unique spectral properties of Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs). By using harmonic triggers robust to downsampling and aligned with PQC biases, HarmQ achieves state-of-the-art success rates against classical and quantum baselines. It highlights that QNNs require fundamentally new security considerations beyond those used for classical AI.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses security vulnerabilities in quantum machine learning models, which are increasingly relevant for simulating complex physical systems or processing quantum data.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) are vulnerable to backdoor attacks, but existing classical attack methods fail due to quantum-specific constraints like downsampling and the spectral bias of Parameterized Quantum Circuits (PQCs).

**Main result.** The proposed HarmQ attack achieves over 99% Attack Success Rate while maintaining high clean accuracy, significantly outperforming all compared backdoor attacks.

**Method.** HarmQ introduces quantum-native harmonic trigger patterns—sinusoidal perturbations on coarse grids—that exploit the inherent Fourier decomposition bias of PQCs.

**Model / system.** The system is a QNN operating in the NISQ era, composed of encoding circuits, trainable PQCs (sequences of unitaries $U_\ell(	heta_\ell)$), and measurement. Data must undergo downsampling to fit limited qubits.

**Key observables.** Attack Success Rate (ASR) and Clean Accuracy (ACC); quantum state representation analyzed via parametric t-SNE distance metrics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trigger frequency ($f$), downsampling methods (e.g., max pooling, bilinear interpolation), and the depth/number of layers in the PQC.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The attack operates under a black-box threat model where the attacker only has access to poisoned training data without knowing internal parameters or gradients.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate QNN structure, quantum gates, and comparative visualizations (t-SNE) showing that harmonic triggers create distinct clusters in quantum state space compared to other attacks.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the vulnerability of QNNs. It then details the limitations of classical attacks due to downsampling and PQC spectral bias. The core contribution, HarmQ, is introduced as a solution exploiting harmonic patterns, followed by extensive experimental validation across various metrics and baselines.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Neural Networks (QNNs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for quantum machine learning in the Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era, leveraging quantum phenomena such as superposition and entanglement to process information in exponentially large Hilbert spaces. However, QNNs inherit critical security vulnerabilities from classical neural networks, particularly susceptibility to backdoor attacks. Existing attack methods designed for classical systems fail against QNNs due to quantum-specific constraints: aggressive downsampling required by limited qubit resources destroys conventional triggers, while the spectral learning bias of parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs) restricts learnable patterns. To tackle this, we present HarmQ, a quantum-native backdoor attack that exploits PQCs' inherent Fourier decomposition bias through harmonic trigger patterns. Our approach employs sinusoidal perturbations on coarse grids with block-uniform structure, ensuring survival through downsampling while aligning with PQCs' preference for low-frequency components. This enables effective backdoor injection under realistic black-box conditions where attackers access only training data. Experiments on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST demonstrate that HarmQ achieves attack success rates exceeding 99% while maintaining over 90% clean accuracy, significantly outperforming existing methods including BadNets (2.77% ASR), Watermark (7.96% ASR), Q-FGSM (44.32% ASR) and QUAP (3.40% ASR). Parametric t-SNE visualizations of quantum state representations confirm that harmonic triggers create distinctly separated clusters, evidencing HarmQ as a fundamental security threat for QNNs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the maximum number of Mutually Orthogonal Quantum Latin Squares (MOQLS) that can exist for a given order $n$. The authors significantly improve theoretical bounds by proving that having $n-2$ such squares forces them to be classical. They achieve this using advanced algebraic methods involving Frobenius rings, advancing the understanding of quantum combinatorial structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is combinatorial/algebraic, its direct application to quantum information theory makes it relevant. Understanding the limits of these structured quantum objects informs the feasibility of certain quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is determining the maximum size, $M(n)$, of a set of Mutually Orthogonal Quantum Latin Squares (MOQLS) for a given order $n$. The paper seeks to improve existing upper and lower bounds on this quantity.

**Main result.** The authors establish a new upper bound showing that a set of $n-2$ MOQLS$(n)$ must be classical, implying $M(n) \le n-3$. They also provide improved lower bounds for prime power orders $q$.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on algebraic structures, specifically Frobenius rings ($R$), and utilizes techniques involving character sums and linear algebra to prove structural constraints derived from the orthogonality conditions.

**Model / system.** The model is the Quantum Latin Square ($\Psi$) defined over a finite Frobenius ring $R$ of order $n$, which generalizes classical MOLS. These squares are relevant in quantum information contexts like teleportation and dense coding.

**Key observables.** The number of mutually orthogonal squares ($t$), the classification of squares as 'classical' or 'non-classical', and the weight/structure of the entries within the square.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order $n$ of the quantum Latin squares, and the specific structure of the underlying Frobenius ring $R$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a finite Frobenius ring $R$ to define the algebraic setting for the quantum structures. A key assumption used in proofs relates non-classicality to structural properties of the defining permutation function $f$.

**Figures summary.** Tables summarize previously known and newly derived bounds on $M(n)$ for small orders ($2 \le n \le 17$), illustrating the improvement over prior results.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing structural properties of MOQLS, culminating in Theorem 5 (the upper bound). It then uses constructions based on Frobenius rings and permutation functions to derive improved lower bounds via Corollary 7. The structure moves from general existence questions to specific algebraic characterizations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

How large can a set of mutually orthogonal quantum Latin squares (MOQLS) get? We show that a set of n - 2 MOQLS of order n is necessarily classical and construct large non-classical sets of MOQLS of orders that are prime powers, improving both the previously known lower and upper bounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents advanced numerical analysis techniques to create highly accurate polynomial approximations for step functions essential in Quantum Singular Value Transform (QSVT). By developing a two-stage approximation method and deriving tight error bounds using special functions, it provides a roadmap for implementing complex quantum algorithms with quantifiable precision.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, mathematically rigorous techniques to implement complex quantum operations (like QSVT) on near-term hardware by providing explicit control over the required polynomial degree based on desired accuracy.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops methods for creating nearly optimal polynomial approximations of discontinuous step functions required for implementing quantum algorithms.

**Main result.** It derives rigorous error bounds showing that the proposed approximation scheme achieves near-optimal accuracy for functions used in Quantum Singular Value Transform (QSVT) calculations.

**Method.** The core method involves approximating target functions using Chebyshev polynomial expansions, specifically developing a two-step process to handle discontinuities by first smoothing the function and then applying polynomial approximation.

**Model / system.** The context is rooted in Quantum Signal Processing (QSP) and the QSVT, which are general frameworks for implementing quantum operations like Hamiltonian simulation and QPE using polynomial functions of singular values.

**Key observables.** Approximation error ($\epsilon$ or $\delta$), the required polynomial order ($N$), and the scaling parameters ($k_0$, $k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The desired accuracy tolerance ($\epsilon$) dictates the necessary polynomial degree ($N \propto O(1/\epsilon)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that approximating discontinuous functions via smooth intermediate steps (like using error functions) mitigates Gibbs phenomena, and it relies on bounding complex terms using special functions like elliptic theta functions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 sketches the approximation of discontinuous step functions $\Theta_{\pm}(a; x)$ by smooth functions $f_{\pm}$ and $g_{\pm}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing general polynomial approximations using Chebyshev coefficients to focusing on the specific challenge of approximating step functions. It then details a two-stage approximation ($g_{\pm}$) and culminates in deriving rigorous, tight error bounds for this scheme using advanced mathematical tools like elliptic theta functions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce polynomial approximations of the even and odd step functions on the interval $[-1,1]$ with simple Chebyshev coefficients, making their numerical implementation straightforward. We derive rigorous error bounds and demonstrate that these polynomials are nearly optimal in the sense that their error deviates from the theoretically optimal error by a multiplicative factor that grows logarithmically with the polynomial order. From these polynomials, we derive related nearly optimal polynomial approximations that can be used to perform quantum phase estimation, linear amplitude amplification, eigenvalue thresholding, and other quantum algorithms using the quantum singular value transform.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the deep mathematical problem of extending an existing low-order deformation quantization of a vector bundle to higher orders. It establishes that the existence of such extensions is governed by the vanishing of specific cohomology obstruction classes derived from analyzing the underlying algebraic and geometric structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, this work provides rigorous mathematical tools for understanding how quantum corrections (quantization) can be consistently built up order-by-order in a physical theory defined on a curved background manifold.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the cohomological obstructions that govern whether an existing order $k$ deformation quantization of a vector bundle can be extended to a higher order $\ell$. This involves analyzing how algebraic structures defined by symplectic forms behave under formal power series expansions.

**Main result.** For certain regimes ($\ell \le 2k+1$), the obstruction class in specific cohomology groups ($H^2$) is both necessary and sufficient for the extension to exist. For higher orders, vanishing of the obstruction is only proven to be a necessary condition.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced techniques from algebraic geometry and deformation theory, utilizing sheaf cohomology ($H^i(M, 	ext{End}(E)^{(k, \ell]})$) and relating these obstructions to Lie algebra extensions via concepts like Harish-Chandra pairs and the Gelfand-Fuchs map.

**Model / system.** The mathematical model is the deformation quantization of a vector bundle $E$ over a smooth algebraic variety $(M, \omega)$ equipped with an algebraic symplectic form. The structure is formalized using formal power series algebras $\mathcal{O}_\hbar$.

**Key observables.** Obstruction classes residing in cohomology groups like $H^2(M, 	ext{End}(E)^{(k, \ell]})$ or $	ext{Ext}^1_{\mathcal{O}_\hbar}(\dots)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The orders of quantization ($k$ and $\ell$) relative to each other ($\ell > k$), and the characteristic zero nature of the base field $\kappa$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence of a symplectic form $\omega$ on $M$, and local analysis relying on formal Darboux lemmas or jet bundle constructions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the necessary and sufficient conditions for extension in specific regimes ($\ell \le 2k+1$), then analyzing the structure of obstructions using Lie algebra cohomology, and finally addressing the more complex case $\ell \ge 2k+2$ where only necessity is proven.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Let $\left(M, \mathcal{O}_M \right)$ be a smooth algebraic variety over field $κ$ of characteristic $0$ with an algebraic symplectic form $ω$, or a complex manifold with a holomorphic form $ω$. Furthermore, let $E$ be a vector bundle over $\left(M, \mathcal{O}_M \right)$ and $\mathcal{O}_{\hbar}$ a deformation quantization of $\mathcal{O}_M$ compatible with $ω$.   Assuming that $E$ possesses a deformation quantization to order $\hbar^k$ we consider the problem of extending it to order $\hbar^\ell$ for $\ell > k$, and establish triviality of an obstruction class as a necessary condition for this extension to exist. Furthermore, in the case $\ell \le 2k+1$, we prove that this condition is also sufficient.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel Jordan-canonical-form framework for constructing $q$-ary quantum stabilizer codes. It allows the construction to proceed from any classical linear code by quantifying its deviation from self-orthogonality using rank reduction ($r$). This systematic approach yields explicit, record-breaking quantum codes with improved parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful algebraic tool (Jordan form) to bridge classical coding theory with quantum error correction, offering systematic methods to generate high-performance codes without requiring overly restrictive initial conditions on the underlying classical structure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to construct $q$-ary quantum stabilizer codes from arbitrary classical linear codes over $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$, circumventing the restrictive requirement that the classical code must be Hermitian self-orthogonal.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a Jordan-canonical-form framework that systematically constructs an explicit Hermitian self-orthogonal code $C_{so}$ and, consequently, a quantum code with parameters $[[n+r, 2k-n+r, \geq d]]_q$.

**Method.** The method involves measuring the obstruction to Hermitian self-orthogonality using the rank $r$ of the matrix $HH^\dagger$, followed by iteratively reducing this rank via rank-one perturbations along the Jordan basis.

**Model / system.** The model is Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs) realized through the stabilizer formalism, which are mathematically mapped to classical linear codes over $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$ equipped with a Hermitian inner product.

**Key observables.** Quantum code parameters $[[n+r, 2k-n+r, \geq d]]_q$, and the rank $r = 	ext{Rank}(HH^\dagger)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The field size $q$ (and $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$), code parameters $[n, k, d]_{q^2}$, and the rank reduction parameter $r$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on the existence of the Jordan canonical form for Hermitian matrices over $\mathbb{F}_{q^2}$ and a distance-preservation criterion based on this decomposition.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference TABLE VI, which lists numerous calculated quantum codes derived from cyclic codes using the Jordan extension method, detailing parameters like $r$ and the defining set $\mathbf{w}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting the limitations of existing constructions; it then introduces the Jordan-canonical-form framework to quantify the deficiency ($r$) and systematically construct a self-orthogonal code via rank reduction theorems, culminating in explicit examples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a Jordan-canonical-form framework for constructing $q$-ary quantum stabilizer codes from arbitrary classical linear codes over $\F_{q^2}$. The framework does not require the classical linear code $\mathcal{C}$ to satisfy the dual-containing condition (i.e., self-orthogonality). Given a classical code $\mathcal{C}=[n,k,d]_{q^2}$ with parity-check matrix $H$, we measure the obstruction to Hermitian self-orthogonality by the rank $r=(n-k)-\dim_{\F_{q^2}}(\mathcal{C}^{\perp_h}\cap \mathcal{C})$. The ingredient code $\mathcal{C}$ is $r$-nearly dual containing, or, equivalently, $\mathcal{C}^{\perp_h}$ is $r$-nearly self-orthogonal, by which we mean that $r=\Rank(HH^{\dagger})=\dim_{\F_{q^2}}(\mathcal{C}^{\perp_h})-\dim_{\F_{q^2}}(\mathcal{C}^{\perp_h}\cap \mathcal{C})$. By systematically reducing the rank of the Hermitian inner-product matrix $A=HH^{\dagger}$ through rank-one perturbations along the Jordan basis $W=P^{-1}$ of the decomposition $A=PJ_AP^{-1}$, we construct an explicit Hermitian self-orthogonal code $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{so}}=[n+r,n-k]_{q^2}$. A sufficient distance-preservation criterion guarantees that the resulting $q$-ary quantum code has parameters $[[n+r,2k-n+r,\geq d]]_q$. Applying this construction to classical codes produces several record quantum codes that improve or supplement the best-known parameters in Grassl's tables.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents advanced techniques for synthesizing arbitrary $n$-qubit unitaries using only Clifford and T gates, focusing heavily on minimizing the required T-gate count. By relating the complexity to the unitary's distance from the Clifford group, it establishes new near-optimal upper bounds that improve upon previous state-of-the-art results.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource estimation in quantum computation, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant algorithms. The focus on near-optimal scaling relative to the deviation from Clifford gates provides a powerful theoretical tool for assessing circuit overhead.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the minimum number of T gates required (T-count) to synthesize an arbitrary $n$-qubit unitary operator $U$ using only Clifford+T quantum gates.

**Main result.** The authors establish a near-optimal upper bound for the T-count, showing it scales favorably with the Frobenius norm distance of $U$ from the Clifford group ($d_{C_F}(U)$), improving previous bounds significantly when $d_{C_F}(U)$ is small.

**Method.** The approach combines Hamiltonian simulation techniques (like QSVT) with a low T-count decomposition derived from leveraging hypercontractivity of Boolean functions to approximate the required unitaries.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum computation on $n$ qubits, restricted to using Clifford and T gates. The synthesis targets an arbitrary unitary $U$, often derived from simulating a Hamiltonian $H$.

**Key observables.** T-count (the primary resource measure), Frobenius norm distance ($d_{C_F}(U)$), and approximation error ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $n$ (number of qubits), $\epsilon$ (precision parameter), and $d_{C_F}(U)$ (measure of non-Clifford character).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ability to implement linear combinations of unitaries (LCU) and utilizes hypercontractivity results for decomposition.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from general synthesis bounds to a Hamiltonian-based framework, detailing the low T-count decomposition of the Hamiltonian $H$ into linear combinations of unitaries ($V_k$), and finally deriving complexity bounds for both unitary and Hamiltonian synthesis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an approach to unitary synthesis that implements an arbitrary $n$-qubit unitary operator $U$ by a Clifford+T circuit with T-count $\widetilde{O}(2^n d_F^{\mathcal{C}}(U))$, where $d_F^{\mathcal{C}}(U)$ is the Frobenius norm distance of $U$ to the Clifford group. The T-count is shown to be near-optimal when $d_F^{\mathcal{C}}(U)$ is a constant. Our approach improves the previous best upper bound $\widetilde{O}(2^{4n/3})$ due to Tan (2025) for a large class of unitary operators $U$ as long as $d_F^{\mathcal{C}}(U) \ll 2^{n/3}$.

</details>

### [When Close Enough Is Not Enough: Autoregressive Drift in Quantum Circuit Synthesis](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12780v1)

**Authors:** Mehdi Saeedi, Eddie Richter, Paul Hartke  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.12780v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</details>

<img src="2607.12780_figures/2607.12780_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>

</details>

<img src="2607.12780_figures/2607.12780_fig5.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Fig. 5. Autoregressive drift illustrated. The model matches the first few tokens (green) but diverges early (red X), after which every subsequent token is conditioned on wrong context. The Clifford+T fidelity distribution is bimodal (F ≈0 or F ≈1), whereas parameterized fidelity is smooth and recoverable via angle post-processing.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates using transformer models for optimizing quantum circuits by minimizing T-gate count. The authors find that while the AI excels at approximate synthesis, it fundamentally fails when exact gate sequence correctness is required due to 'autoregressive drift.' This limitation shows that simple scaling of data or advanced search methods cannot overcome this inherent sequential decoding flaw.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on quantum computation, the analysis of model failure modes—specifically how sequential generation breaks down under strict discrete constraints—provides insights relevant to any complex physical simulation or state preparation process where cumulative error is critical.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is optimizing quantum circuits for fault-tolerant computation by achieving exact functional equivalence while minimizing expensive non-Clifford resources like T gates.

**Main result.** While the transformer model succeeds when post-processing can rescue approximate outputs, its reliance on autoregressive decoding causes an irreversible failure (drift) when exact discrete correctness is required, especially for longer circuits.

**Method.** The authors use a structured encoder-decoder transformer trained to map input quantum circuits to equivalent, optimized output sequences of Clifford+T gates.

**Model / system.** The system involves synthesizing quantum circuits on 3-6 qubits using the Clifford+T gate set. The model is a compact transformer architecture utilizing structured tokenization for circuit representation.

**Key observables.** Process fidelity (exact equivalence), T-count reduction, and the rate of exact match success as a function of target circuit length.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The failure exhibits strong dependence on target circuit length; performance degrades sharply from high accuracy on short circuits to near zero for longer ones.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The central assumption tested is that autoregressive generation must maintain perfect discrete correctness, which the model fails to do beyond a certain circuit depth due to drift.

**Figures summary.** Notes imply figures comparing success rates across different search strategies (greedy vs. beam search) and showing the sharp drop-off in exact equivalence as target length increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts two regimes: parameterized circuits (where classical optimization rescues approximate errors) versus discrete Clifford+T circuits (where exactness is mandatory). It then systematically tests mitigation strategies like inference-time searching and data scaling against the persistent failure mode of autoregressive drift.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum circuit optimization for fault-tolerant computing requires exact functional equivalence while minimizing expensive non-Clifford resources such as T gates. We study this problem using a compact 44.8M-parameter encoder-decoder transformer with structured circuit tokenization, evaluating on parameterized circuits (2-6 qubits) and Clifford+T circuits (3-6 qubits). On parameterized circuits, a hybrid approach -- structure from the transformer, angles from classical optimization -- achieves median fidelity 1.000 on 3-6 qubit circuits. On Clifford+T circuits, where all gates are discrete and no post-processing is possible, the model learns valid syntax and accurate T-Count statistics, yet exact equivalence degrades sharply with target length -- from 88% on circuits with <=9 gates to near zero beyond 26 gates.   We trace this failure to autoregressive drift: early-token divergence cascading irrecoverably through left-to-right decoding. Two levers partially mitigate the drift: inference-time strategies that generate multiple candidates and select via equivalence verification raise exact-match rates from 7% to 22.5%, while scaling training data by 2.5x pushes them to 39.5%. Yet the degradation with target length persists -- even with more data, exact equivalence drops from 94% on short circuits to under 4% beyond 26 gates.   The contrast between settings is our central finding: when approximate outputs can be rescued by post-processing, the transformer succeeds; when exact discrete correctness is required, autoregressive drift limits reliability, with both inference-time search and data scaling as effective levers while training-side fine-tuning and model-level diversification are not.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work resolves contradictions in CISS measurements by introducing geometric spin-orbit coupling into the analysis. The framework predicts that measurable spin polarization only occurs within an intermediate regime of environmental decoherence. It provides a unified understanding of CISS across various chiral molecular structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit modeling of spin transport in nanoscale molecular junctions using geometric SOC and decoherence provides a rich platform for studying open quantum systems dynamics, particularly how environmental coupling dictates observable quantum effects like spin polarization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to resolve conflicting experimental reports regarding the Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) effect in chiral single molecules.

**Main result.** It shows that CISS is suppressed in both strong-coherence and strong-decoherence regimes, becoming pronounced only in an intermediate decoherence regime. The mechanism unifies observations by incorporating geometric spin-orbit coupling.

**Method.** The authors developed a theoretical framework using tight-binding models to incorporate geometric SOC and environmental decoherence into the Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system involves chiral single molecules modeled as helical chains (e.g., cone-shaped, uniform helix). The theory incorporates molecular Hamiltonians augmented by geometric SOC terms and environmental decoherence via a master equation approach.

**Key observables.** Spin Polarization (SP), which is calculated using the Landauer-Büttiker formalism; $	ext{SP}_{\max}$ dependence on temperature and coupling strengths.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Decoherence strength ($\Gamma_d$), electron-vibration coupling constant ($	ilde{\lambda}_{	ext{e-vib}}$), and molecular geometry parameters (radius, pitch).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that geometric SOC provides the necessary theoretical extension beyond traditional intrinsic or field-driven SOC models to explain CISS.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate three types of SOC mechanisms; one shows SP for different molecular geometries across varying parameters; another presents phase diagrams mapping observable CISS to intermediate decoherence regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the conflict in experimental data, then develops a theoretical framework incorporating geometric SOC and decoherence. It systematically calculates SP for various chiral models (A, B, C) under different coupling conditions ($	ext{e-vib}$, $	ext{e-e}$), concluding with generalized phase diagrams.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Some studies have reported clear chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) effect in four classes of chiral single molecules with remarkable spin polarization. In contrast, a recent high-precision measurement involving nearly a thousand individual tests failed to detect significant CISS signals in the same molecular systems (J. Am. Chem. Soc. 2025, \textbf{147}, 25043). These conflicting results cast doubt on whether CISS truly occurs in these chiral systems at the single-molecular level. To resolve this discrepancy, we develop a theoretical framework incorporating geometric spin-orbit coupling and environmental decoherence, enabling systematic study of the CISS in four chiral single molecules with distinct geometries and sizes. Our calculations show that the CISS effect is completely suppressed in both strong-coherence and strong-decoherence regimes, but becomes pronounced in the intermediate-decoherence regime, where observable spin polarization emerges. In the strong-coherence regime, both electron-electron interaction and electron-vibration coupling enhance the CISS effect: the former is more effective in large molecules, whereas the latter plays a more significant role in smaller ones. Increasing temperature further enhances spin polarization. The proposed mechanism unifies contradictory experimental observations and reveals how the CISS effect evolves from regular helical (helical symmetric) to irregular helical (point-symmetric or axially symmetric) chirality. This framework thus provides a basis for unifying CISS phenomena across single-molecule systems, regardless of their specific molecular configurations or symmetry classes.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that ferroelectric BNT barriers can be used to create Josephson junctions whose superconducting properties are dynamically controlled by external Terahertz (THz) radiation. By coupling THz fields to the BNT polarization, researchers successfully modulated both the critical current and the superconducting energy gap in YBCO/BNT/YBCO structures. This establishes a pathway for building next-generation, light-controlled superconducting quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges condensed matter physics (superconductivity, high-Tc cuprates) with quantum optics and materials science (ferroelectrics, THz response). The ability to use an external electromagnetic field to tune a fundamental quantum parameter like the superconducting gap is highly relevant for developing tunable quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to systematically modulate superconducting gap dynamics in YBCO|BNT|YBCO Josephson Junctions by utilizing the interaction between external Terahertz (THz) fields and the ferroelectric polarization of the BNT barrier.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that THz irradiation can dynamically enhance the critical current ($I_c$) and increase the superconducting gap ($\Delta$), confirming that the Josephson coupling is intrinsically controlled by the tunable, dipolar response of the ferroelectric BNT barrier.

**Method.** A combination of epitaxial thin-film deposition (PLD) to create high-quality trilayers, electrical transport measurements ($I$-$V$, $dI/dV$) under THz excitation, and theoretical modeling using a modified Josephson junction Hamiltonian was employed.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the YBCO|BNT|YBCO Josephson Junction. The dynamics are modeled by incorporating a time-dependent modulation term ($\mathcal{H}_{mod}$) into the standard Josephson Hamiltonian, where this modulation arises from the THz-induced polarization ($P_{THz}(t)$) within the BNT ferroelectric barrier.

**Key observables.** Superconducting gap ($\Delta$), critical current ($I_c$) under THz excitation, BNT polarization ($P$), and Fraunhofer interference patterns in $I_c(B)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** THz frequency (0.5-2.5 THz), ferroelectric polarization magnitude ($33.4 	o 46.4 \mu	ext{C}/	ext{cm}^2$), and superconducting gap values ($\Delta = 1.3 	ext{ meV}$ vs $1.05 	ext{ meV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The coupling mechanism is assumed to be dominated by the dipolar interaction between the THz field/polarization and the junction capacitance, leading to a time-dependent barrier potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate structural characterization (XRD/XRR) confirming high epitaxial quality; energy diagrams show how the external THz field introduces an additional gap ($E_g$) into the Josephson coupling energy ($	ext{E}_{	ext{J}}$); and spectroscopic plots detail the modulation of $\Delta$ and $I_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure moving from system definition (YBCO/BNT/YBCO stack) to theoretical modeling incorporating THz interaction, followed by detailed experimental characterization (structural confirmation via XRR/XRD, electrical measurements under THz), culminating in the demonstration of dynamic control over superconducting parameters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The integration of ferroelectric barriers into high-temperature Josephson junctions offers a pathway to tunable superconducting quantum devices. Here, we demonstrate robust Josephson coupling in YBa2Cu3O7-x (YBCO)/Bi0.5Na0.5TiO3 (BNT)/YBCO trilayer junctions incorporating a 40 nm ferroelectric BNT barrier. Epitaxial trilayers were fabricated by pulsed laser deposition on SrTiO3 substrates and investigated under broadband terahertz (THz) irradiation (0.5-2.5 THz). At 1.5 THz, close to the Josephson plasma resonance, the BNT polarization increased from 33.4 to 46.4 uC/cm2 at 30 K, enhancing superconducting transport. The junctions exhibited a well-defined zero-voltage supercurrent branch, with the critical current Ic(T) remaining nearly constant up to 60 K and reaching 468.2 uA under 1.5 THz excitation, evidencing strong phase coherence. Scanning tunneling spectroscopy revealed an enhanced superconducting gap in YBCO/BNT/YBCO (Delta = 1.3 meV) compared with pure YBCO (Delta = 1.05 meV), while optical conductivity measurements showed a reduction in conductivity and gap with increasing temperature, consistent with BCS theory under THz excitation. Atomic force microscopy and X-ray reflectivity confirmed uniform morphology and sharp interfaces, excluding defect-mediated transport. Magnetic field modulation of Ic(B) exhibited a canonical Fraunhofer interference pattern, and resistance mapping revealed alternating lobes of high and low dissipation, indicating coherent Josephson tunneling. These results establish that Josephson coupling is intrinsic to the YBCO/BNT/YBCO junctions, enabled by the dipolar character and dynamic THz response of the BNT barrier. This study identifies BNT as a viable, tunable barrier material for next-generation high-Tc superconducting quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a general theory for how an oscillating external field can induce a steady, rectified electric current in a periodic system, even if the static case forbids it. The resulting DC current is shown to have both extrinsic (impurity-related) and intrinsic (Berry curvature-derived) origins. By applying this framework to magnetic materials, the authors propose that this effect can act as a novel electrical probe for detecting specific types of magnetic ordering.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of generating DC currents from AC drives via nonlinear coupling is central to understanding non-equilibrium quantum transport. The explicit connection between this effect and measurable magnetic orders makes it highly relevant for condensed matter theorists studying emergent phenomena in frustrated magnets.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work addresses the nonlinear transport phenomenon of electric current rectification, converting an alternating external drive into a steady direct current (DC) even when static theory forbids it.

**Main result.** A general theoretical framework is established showing that this rectified DC current arises from two distinct contributions: impurity scattering and a dynamical mixed Berry curvature. This effect can serve as a sensitive electrical probe for magnetic order, such as Néel ordering in antiferromagnets.

**Method.** The analysis uses a semiclassical approach, expanding the system's response to the time-varying drive ($\lambda(t)$) up to second order in the drive amplitude to isolate the DC component. The theory decomposes the current into extrinsic (scattering-dependent) and intrinsic (Berry curvature-derived) parts.

**Model / system.** The general model involves Bloch electrons in a periodic system driven by an external oscillating field coupling to an arbitrary operator $\hat{O}$. A specific application is studied using a tight-binding Hamiltonian for a buckled magnetic lattice subjected to an out-of-plane oscillating electric field.

**Key observables.** Rectified DC current ($J_{dc}$), which is proportional to the product of two drive amplitudes ($\lambda_a\lambda_b$), and its components $\sigma_{	ext{abc}}^{	ext{ex}}$ (extrinsic) and $\sigma_{	ext{abc}}^{	ext{in}}$ (intrinsic).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The driving frequency ($\Omega$) relative to the optical transition gap ($\Delta_{	ext{gap}}$), and the scattering rate ($\Gamma$). The DC current persists even when $\hbar\Omega \ll \Delta_{	ext{gap}}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the semiclassical framework, utilizing the relaxation-time approximation for the distribution function. Some derivations employ a time-dependent Schrieffer-Wolff transformation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the generation of $J_{dc}$ from an oscillating drive. Figure 2 shows the scaling behavior of different conductivity components ($\sigma_{	ext{ex,1}}$, $\sigma_{	ext{ex,2}}$, $\sigma_{	ext{in}}$) across various frequency regimes relative to the scattering rate. Figure 3 depicts the physical realization in a buckled honeycomb lattice.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general theory for rectification, derives the current components ($\sigma^{	ext{ex}}, \sigma^{	ext{in}}$), analyzes their frequency dependence and symmetry constraints (P, T, PT), and concludes with a specific application to magnetic systems to probe Néel order.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rectification is a fundamental nonlinear transport process that converts an alternating drive into a direct current. In this work, we propose a general theoretical framework for electric current rectification triggered by a dynamical external drive that couples to an arbitrary well-defined operator of a periodic system, and which in the static limit forbids any steady current. In the dynamical regime, the finite frequency $Ω$ of the time-varying drive breaks time-translation invariance and injects energy into the system, enabling a second-order {\it nonlinear rectified} current that has no static counterpart. This rectification process has two distinct origins: (i) an impurity-scattering-modified distribution function at finite frequency, and (ii) a time-domain anomalous velocity stemming from a dynamical mixed Berry curvature. Both contributions persist when the driving frequency lies well below the optical transition gap. Applying our general theory to a buckled magnetic system subject to an out-of-plane oscillating electric field, we characterize the generated current as a {\it nonlinear magnetoelectric gyrotropic effect} and predict that the induced rectified current is sensitive to the magnetic order, thereby offering a feasible electrical probe of Néel order in non-coplanar antiferromagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models domain wall motion in ferromagnetic nanowires driven by localized laser heating. The simulations show that the DW velocity scales linearly with temperature but is attenuated by distance due to magnon current decay. Key findings establish that both the laser spot size and the separation distance act as independent control parameters for thermal spintronics devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the use of stochastic equations (sLLG) to model thermal fluctuations and non-equilibrium dynamics in a spatially varying field provides relevant computational techniques applicable to open quantum systems or dissipative many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how magnetic domain wall (DW) motion is driven by a localized Gaussian thermal gradient generated by a focused laser spot.

**Main result.** The DW velocity increases linearly with peak laser temperature and decreases as the distance from the heat source increases due to magnon current attenuation. The dynamics are controlled independently by both the laser width and the separation distance.

**Method.** Micromagnetic simulations utilizing the stochastic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (sLLG) equation were employed to model the time evolution of magnetization.

**Model / system.** The system is a uniaxial ferromagnetic nanowire containing a head-to-head DW. The dynamics are governed by the sLLG equation under the influence of a localized Gaussian temperature profile, with motion primarily driven by magnonic spin-transfer torque ($\mu$STT).

**Key observables.** Translational velocity ($v$), angular velocity ($\mathrm{d}\phi/\mathrm{dt}$), and DW displacement ($\Delta x/d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Peak laser temperature ($T_L$), laser to DW distance ($d = N\sigma_L$), Gilbert damping ($\alpha$), uniaxial anisotropy ($K_u$), and laser width ($\sigma_L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The entropic torque is negligible because the initial DW position has an effectively zero local temperature gradient, simplifying the driving mechanism to $\mu$STT.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the linear dependence of velocity on $T_L$, the decay of velocity with increasing separation distance ($N$), and non-monotonic dependencies on damping ($\alpha$) and anisotropy ($K_u$).

**Paper structure.** The analysis systematically varies key physical parameters—$T_L$, distance, $\alpha$, $K_u$, and $\sigma_L$—to map out the functional dependence of DW velocity and angular dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate magnetic domain wall (DW) dynamics in a uniaxial ferromagnetic nanowire under the localized Gaussian temperature profile of a laser spot using the stochastic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation. The DW velocity increases linearly with peak laser temperature and decreases with increasing laser to DW distance. The velocity varies nonlinearly with Gilbert damping because damping strengthens thermal magnon excitation but shortens the magnon propagation length. The DW initially lies away from the laser-heated region, so the temperature gradient at its position is effectively zero and the entropic torque is negligible. The DW motion is therefore mainly driven by magnonic spin-transfer torque. We analyze laser temperature, laser to DW distance, damping, uniaxial anisotropy, and laser width. The analysis shows that laser width and laser to DW distance independently control the DW response. These findings may clarify the mechanism of localized thermally driven DW motion and guide thermal control strategies in spintronic racetrack-memory devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reveals that the real-time spin polarization in chiral molecules like D-Bχ-A systems originates primarily from a geometric spin-orbit coupling ($	ext{SOC}_{	ext{geo}}$). By modeling electron transport along the molecule's twisted bridge, the authors show this mechanism generates strong, measurable spin polarizations that match experimental data. This provides a general theoretical blueprint for designing chiral spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly detailed, first-principles theoretical mechanism for CISS that moves beyond simple models. The explicit derivation of geometric SOC from molecular curvature is crucial for designing next-generation chiral spintronic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to elucidate the real-time, atomistic mechanism responsible for Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) in Donor-Chiral Bridge-Acceptor (D-Bχ-A) molecules.

**Main result.** The study proposes that a geometric spin-orbit coupling ($	ext{SOC}_{	ext{geo}}$), arising from the curvature of electron transport along the chiral bridge, is the dominant mechanism generating observable high spin polarizations. This framework successfully accounts for both axial and transverse polarization components observed in experiments.

**Method.** The authors developed and utilized a quantum dynamical model incorporating time-dependent charge-state populations and explicitly calculating geometric SOC derived from the Frenet–Serret frame along the twisted molecular pathway.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the D-Bχ-A molecule, focusing on binaphthyl-type chiral bridge dimers. The theoretical framework involves deriving a Hamiltonian that includes $	ext{SOC}_{	ext{geo}}$ and analyzing spin dynamics using quantum master equations or density matrix propagation.

**Key observables.** Spin polarization components ($	ext{SP}_x, 	ext{SP}_y, 	ext{SP}_z$), the magnitude of geometric SOC (predicted to be $1-2$ orders of magnitude larger than intrinsic coupling), and population/coherence polarizations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative magnitudes of polarization components ($\sim 30\%–40\%$ for CISS contribution) and the ratio $\lambda/t_0 \approx 0.64$ defining the SOC energy scale are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the geometric SOC mechanism provides a self-consistent picture, allowing polarization to be understood without relying solely on conventional radical-pair mechanisms (RPM).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate molecular geometries and transport pathways, detailing how the geometric SOC is decomposed along orthogonal axes ($x, y, z$) across different enantiomers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the mechanistic gap in CISS understanding. It then develops a quantum dynamical model incorporating $	ext{SOC}_{	ext{geo}}$ derived from molecular geometry (Frenet–Serret frame). Finally, it calculates and compares resulting spin polarization components against time-resolved electron paramagnetic resonance measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chiral-induced spin selectivity (CISS) has been experimentally observed in photo-excited donor-chiral bridge-acceptor (D-Bχ-A) molecules [Science 382, 197-201 (2023)]. However, the microscopic mechanism underlying CISS in such chiral systems remains elusive. Here we develop a quantum dynamical model that precisely maps the atomic structure of binaphthyl-type bridge dimers in isolated D-Bχ-A molecules and introduce a geometric spin-orbit coupling (SOC) mechanism to unveil the intrinsic origin of CISS in axially chiral systems. During photo-excited electron transport along the twisted pathways, the geometric SOC coupling strength exceeds the intrinsic coupling of light atoms by one to two orders of magnitude, readily producing observable high spin polarizations. The resulting spin polarization comprises two components: the CISS-associated polarizations along and perpendicular to the chiral axis are intrinsic to axial chirality, requiring neither external fields nor spin-superexchange transfer, while a non-Abelian curvature correction provides a rigorous mathematical definition of the chiral axis direction. Our calculated polarization components, chirality dependence, and relative magnitudes (30-40\%) quantitatively match time-resolved electron paramagnetic resonance measurements. This geometric SOC framework offers a self-consistent and general physical picture of CISS in axially chiral molecules and provides explicit theoretical guidance for the design of chiral spintronic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work proposes that Quantum Anomalous Hall Effect can be engineered in compensated magnets by utilizing a d-wave sublattice-staggered altermagnetic interaction. By tuning parameters like the staggered potential and lattice anisotropy, the model predicts the realization of multiple quantized Chern number states ($C=\pm 1, \pm 2$). This provides a simple, magnetically driven route to topological quantum phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter topology, the concept of engineering robust quantum states using internal symmetries (like altermagnetism) is highly relevant to designing novel platforms for quantum simulation or topological quantum computing elements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is realizing the Quantum Anomalous Hall Effect (QAHE) in compensated magnetic systems without applying external magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that a d-wave sublattice-staggered altermagnetism can induce tunable QAHE phases, allowing for Chern numbers $C=\pm 1$ and $C=\pm 2$.

**Method.** The study employs theoretical modeling using a minimal spinful tight-binding Hamiltonian on a square lattice to calculate topological invariants.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a spinful tight-binding model on a square lattice, driven into QAHE by adding a d-wave sublattice-staggered altermagnetic exchange term. The physics relies on the interplay between this magnetic coupling and structural anisotropies.

**Key observables.** Tunable Chern numbers ($C$), quantized two-terminal conductance plateaus ($	ext{G} = n \cdot e^2/h$), and chiral edge states visible in ribbon spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Altermagnetic strength ($J$), sublattice potential ($\mu$), and hopping anisotropy ($t_x$ vs. $t_y$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the existence of a d-wave sublattice-staggered altermagnetism, which breaks time-reversal symmetry but keeps the net magnetization zero.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures showing topological transitions (e.g., $C=0 	o C=2$ vs. sequential steps), phase diagrams mapping allowed Chern numbers based on parameter tuning, and ribbon spectra confirming gapless edge modes corresponding to quantized conductance plateaus.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the argument by first defining the minimal model Hamiltonian incorporating altermagnetism, then analyzing how varying parameters ($\mu$, $t_x/t_y$) controls the topological phase diagram (Chern number), and finally confirming these predictions via edge state calculations and quantized transport measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a minimal spinful tight-binding model on a square lattice, where a $d$-wave sublattice-staggered altermagnetism drives the quantum anomalous Hall effect. Here the exchange field is staggered between the two sublattices, where it takes opposite signs on $A$ and $B$ described by the Pauli matrix $τ_z$. The resulting insulating phases host tunable Chern numbers $\mathcal{C}=\pm1$ and $\mathcal{C}=\pm2$, controlled by the staggered exchange strength and the sublattice-staggered potential. We determine the complete phase diagram, identify valley-resolved band inversions at the $X$ and $Y$ points in the Brillouin zone, and demonstrate chiral edge states together with quantized two-terminal conductance plateaus. Our work provides a simple route to realizing the quantum anomalous Hall effect in compensated magnets via a $d$-wave sublattice-staggered altermagnetism.

</details>
